Higher-Order Analogs of Lie Algebroids via Vector Bundle Comorphisms
Michał JÓŹWIKOWSKI † and Mikołaj ROTKIEWICZ ‡

Abstract
We introduce the concept of a higher algebroid, generalizing the notions of an algebroid and a higher tangent bundle. Our ideas are based on a description of (Lie) algebroids as vector bundle comorphisms – differential relations of a special kind. In our approach higher algebroids are vector bundle comorphism between graded-linear bundles satisfying natural axioms. We provide natural examples and discuss applications in geometric mechanics.



keywords
higher algebroid; vector bundle comorphism; almost-Lie algebroid; graded manifold; graded bundle; algebroid lift; variational principle

AMS
58A20; 58A50; 70G65; 58E30; 70H50

1
Introduction

The main goal of this paper is to introduce a concept of a (general)
higher algebroid
1
1

1

In this paper under the name
algebroid
we understand generalizations of the notion of a Lie algebroid obtained by weakening its axioms. In the geometric mechanics literature these generalizations are commonly known as
almost-Lie
(no Jacobi identity),
skew
(no Jacobi and no anchor-bracket compatibility), or
general
(no Jacobi, no anchor-bracket compatibility, no skew-symmetry of the bracket) algebroids (the latter are often simply called
algebroids
). Throughout the paper we shall refer to these objects as to
(
Lie
)
algebroids
meaning “Lie algebroids and their above-mentioned generalizations”. They should not be confused with
Courant algebroids
or other similarly named concepts.
and study basic properties and applications of this notion. We work essentially within the framework of the theory of
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifolds and the theory of differential relations (vector bundle comorphisms).

Why higher algebroids?
Lie algebroids and their generalizations proved to be a fruitful area of study in the last three decades, either on their own right, as an offspring of Poisson geometry, and, particularly, in geometric mechanics. The latter direction was originated by Weinstein
[
52
]
and, following seminal papers of Martínez
[
36
,
37
]
, it developed into many different sub-branches (see a survey paper
[
11
]
for a detailed discussion of the available literature).

From our point of view one of the most spectacular achievements of the algebroid-oriented study of mechanics is the recognition of the geometric structures standing behind variational calculus. The relation between (Lie) algebroids and variations is, perhaps, best described in two papers
[
13
,
14
]
, which themselves were inspired by much earlier works of Tulczyjew
[
44
,
45
]
. What makes the mentioned works particularly interesting is that the authors were able to identify the true geometric essence of variational calculus, which keeps working despite getting rid of several most natural assumptions, such as the Jacobi identity and even the skew-symmetry of the Lie algebroid bracket. In fact, putting aside the existence of real-life examples, it seems that the concept of a general algebroid
[
18
,
19
]
is the uttermost geometric reality behind first-order variational calculus.

From the above perspective it is most natural to look for similar geometric structures related with variational calculus of higher order. Thus we would like to introduce a geometric object, a
(
Lie
)
higher algebroid
, which is present whenever a variational problem involving higher velocities is considered. We have already tried to address this question from the perspective of the classical groupoid–algebroid reduction
[
27
]
, yet now we propose a more abstract and a more general approach.

Towards a proper definition – objects.
In order one, the (Lie) algebroid structure “lives” on a vector bundle, which can be often interpreted as the velocity bundle of some physical system, i.e., as a quotient (in a suitable sense) of some tangent bundle
\mathrm{T}M
. The structure of the tangent bundle is thus our most important example of a (Lie) algebroid. Obviously, in higher-order variational calculus the role of
\mathrm{T}M
is taken over by its higher analogue
\mathrm{T}^{k}M
, the bundle of
k
-velocities.
2
2

2

\mathrm{T}^{k}M
is the bundle of
k
-jets of curves in
M
and it should not be mistaken with
\mathrm{T}^{(k)}M=\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{T}(\ldots(\mathrm{T}M)\ldots))
– the iterated tangent bundle.
Locally,
\mathrm{T}^{k}M
can be characterized by specifying the points, velocities, accelerations, and higher derivatives of curves on
M
. Thus, having the perspective of variational calculus in mind, it is reasonable to expect that a proper object hosting a higher algebroid structure would be a bundle of some sort with fibre coordinates sharing a similar derivative-like nature as coordinates on
\mathrm{T}^{k}M
. This expectation is met by a class of fibre bundles with a typical fibre diffeomorphic with
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, however, in general, transition functions need not to be linear but are polynomials which are homogeneous with respect to a prescribed gradation of coordinate functions (see Section
3
for a detailed discussion). They are a special case of the concept of a
graded manifold
introduced by Voronov
[
47
]
.
Namely, they are
purely even non-negatively graded manifolds
in the sense of Voronov, meaning that local coordinates are graded by non-negative integers. We follow Voronov’s idea that such a non-negatively graded manifold, which expands into a tower of fibrations with polynomial transition functions (of a special form), should be regarded as a generalization of a vector bundle
[
47
]
, see also
[
48
,
49
,
50
]
. (For this, we use notation typical for (vector) bundles – see Section
3
.)
Properties of such bundles and their application in theoretical mechanics where also considered in several papers
[
4
,
6
,
17
]
. For brevity, we shall use “
\mathbb{N}
-graded” for “non-negatively graded” (thinking that zero is included into the natural numbers) or simply “graded” if no other gradings will appear. Note that we consider only the even case, though the results are expected to hold in the super setting too.

Towards a proper definition – structures.
The structure of an order one (Lie) algebroid on a vector bundle
\sigma\colon E\rightarrow M
is most commonly introduced as a bi-linear bracket operation on the space of sections of its host bundle
\sigma
. It is quite obvious that this definition has no direct generalization to the higher-order case as, in particular, the higher tangent bundle
\tau^{k}_{M}\colon\mathrm{T}^{k}M\rightarrow M
, which is an obvious candidate to host a higher (Lie) algebroid structure, admits no canonical bracket operation on its space of sections. Fortunately, the (Lie) algebroid structure on
\sigma
has several other characterizations equivalent to the standard definition, perhaps more suitable for a generalization. To mention just a few, we may describe an algebroid on
\sigma
as a certain homological vector field on a graded supermanifold
E[1]
, a de Rham-like derivative in the space of
E
-differential forms (i.e., sections of
\bigwedge^{\bullet}E^{\ast}
), a certain linear 2-tensor field on the dual bundle
\sigma^{\ast}
, a morphism
\varepsilon\colon\mathrm{T}^{\ast}E\rightarrow\mathrm{T}E^{\ast}
of double vector bundles, or as a differential relation of a special kind (a vector bundle comorphism)
\kappa\subset\mathrm{T}E\times\mathrm{T}E
.

From the perspective of variational calculus, the latter characterization is the one most appealing to us.
3
3

3

Let us remark that, the double vector bundle morphism
\varepsilon\colon\mathrm{T}^{\ast}E\rightarrow\mathrm{T}E^{\ast}
favored in the so-called Tulczyjew approach to geometric mechanics
[
13
,
14
]
is just the dual of
\kappa
.
This important role of
\kappa
can be easily explained. For the tangent algebroid structure on
\tau_{M}\colon\mathrm{T}M\rightarrow M
,
\kappa
is, in fact, the canonical isomorphism
\kappa_{M}\colon\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}M\rightarrow\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}M
interchanging the two vector bundle structures on
\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}M
– cf. Example
2.13
. Its role in variational calculus is to present ‘a jet of curves as a curve of jets’ (i.e., a variation of the tangent lift of a trajectory in
M
is constructed from the tangent lift of the curve of virtual displacements by means of
\kappa_{M}
). The situation in the higher-order case is no different and, perhaps, even easier to understand. Namely on a higher-tangent bundle
\tau_{M}^{k}\colon\mathrm{T}^{k}M\rightarrow M
, we have the canonical isomorphism
\kappa^{k}_{M}\colon\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{T}M\rightarrow\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k}M
which, similarly as before, allows to express a variation of a
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-tangent lift of a trajectory in
M
as a
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-tangent lift of the related generator (a curve of virtual displacements)
[
26
]
. When performing the standard Lie groupoid – Lie algebroid reduction the character (but not the role) of
\kappa
changes – the reduced object is no longer a differentiable map but, in general, only a differential relation (but of a special kind). Again there is no essential difference between the first- and higher-order cases
[
27
]
.

Summing up, geometrically relation
\kappa\subset\mathrm{T}E\times\mathrm{T}E
is responsible for the construction of admissible variations of curves on a (Lie) algebroid on
E
. Its presence can be easily motivated by the groupoid – algebroid reduction procedure: when performing a reduction of a variational problem it is not enough to reduce the trajectories, but also the variations! An this is how
\kappa
appears. Variational calculus on (Lie) algebroids can be, however, successfully developed in abstract terms (with a prominent role of
\kappa
) without referring to the reduction procedure
[
13
]
.

All the above suggests that the language of differential relations is suitable to speak about higher (Lie) algebroids (at least from the perspective of applications in variational calculus). The first order-case
[
13
]
and the known higher-order examples from
[
25
]
advocate that these relations should have a special nature, namely they should be
vector bundle comorphisms
(
VBCs
in short; see Definition
2.1
). This assumption, in principle, allows to uniquely characterize admissible variations in terms of their generators (virtual displacements). Let us remark that the important role of vector bundle comorphisms in the theory of Lie groupoids and Lie algebroids was recognized also in
[
7
]
.

Higher (Lie) algebroids, examples and applications.
Summing up the above heuristic considerations, we postulate a
higher
(
Lie
)
algebroid
to be a
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold
\sigma^{k}\colon E^{k}\rightarrow M
equipped with a vector bundle comorphism
\kappa^{k}\subset\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{1}\times\mathrm{T}E^{k}
, which also should be naturally graded (Definition
4.1
). Here
E^{1}
is the canonical reduction of
E^{k}
to a first-order bundle, i.e., to a vector bundle.

For
k=1
we recover the standard notion of a (Lie) algebroid. In fact, we devote entire Section
2
to carefully formulate the theory of (the first order) Lie algebroids and their generalizations in the language of VBCs. This has a two-fold purpose. First of all, the language of differential relations was so far hardly used in the theory of (Lie) algebroids and most of the results of this section are new (yet rather straightforward, so no true originality can be claimed by us). Secondly, this first-order formulation has a direct generalization to the higher order case, with most of the definitions being completely analogous.

Apart from the first-order case, important examples of such structures are provided by higher tangent bundles
\mathrm{T}^{k}M
with the isomorphisms
\kappa^{k}_{M}
mentioned above. Another important class of examples is given by the
prolongations of almost-Lie algebroids
introduced in
[
9
]
and
[
27
]
(see also
[
43
]
). Prolongations naturally appear in higher-order variational calculus as reductions of higher tangent bundles of Lie groupoids. In our previous publications
[
25
,
27
]
(see also
[
39
]
) we study several concrete cases. Additionally some examples related to Lie groups are discussed in Section
6
.

We further argue for the usefulness of the concept of a higher (Lie) algebroid introduced in this work by providing two particular applications. First of all, given a higher algebroid structure on
\sigma^{k}\colon E^{k}\rightarrow M
we were able to define a whole family of
algebroid lifts
of sections of
E^{1}
to vector fields on
E^{k}
. These generalize the well-know constructions of the vertical and the complete lifts of a section in the presence of a (Lie) algebroid structure. Secondly, in Section
5
we show that the geometric formulation of higher-order variational calculus is possible within the framework of higher (Lie) algebroids (from this perspective the algebroid lifts are directly related with conservation laws). In fact, we show that the latter works fine also for a more general class of
higher pre-algebroids
, thus further generalization of our theory is possible. We refer to a recent publication
[
8
]
and the references therein for numerous concrete interesting examples of higher-order variational problems.

Alternative approaches.
In the literature there have been a few attempts to introduce higher analogs of (Lie) algebroids. The version of a higher analog of Lie algebroids that we develop is quite distinct from the work of Mackenzie
[
35
]
and Voronov
[
48
,
50
]
, though we make use of some of their concepts and methods. Voronov
[
48
]
proposed that higher algebroids should be understood as homological vector fields of weight one on a non-negatively graded supermanifold (generalizing Vaintrob’s description of Lie algebroids as
Q
-manifolds
[
46
]
). The language of
Q
-manifolds is central in the whole bracket geometry. Connections between higher (Lie) algebroids in the sense of this work and Voronov’s ones or
k
-fold Lie algebroids of Mackenzie are not clear and need further studies.

A much more down-to-earth idea is to define higher (Lie) algebroids as bundles of higher-jets of admissible curves on a standard Lie algebroid. In this paper we refer to this construction as the
prolongation of an algebroid
and briefly discuss it in Section
4.2
. Such objects appeared for the first time in a seminar talk by Colombo and de Diego
[
9
]
, however without a full recognition of the relevant geometric structure. The latter was studied by us in
[
27
]
and successfully applied in higher-order variational problems on Lie algebroids and Lie groupoids
[
25
]
(see also
[
39
]
). Prolongations of algebroids in the above sense constitute a particular subclass of higher (Lie) algebroids in the understanding of this paper, perhaps the most important one as it contains reductions of higher tangent bundles of Lie groupoids.

The most recent attempt is due to Bruce, Grabowska and Grabowski. In
[
5
]
they constructed a
linearisation functor
, which, to a
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold
\sigma^{k}\colon E^{k}\rightarrow M
of order
k
, canonically associates a manifold
l\big(E^{k}\big)
equipped with a graded-linear structure of a bi-order
(k-1,1)
over
E^{1}
and
E^{k-1}
, respectively. Now the higher (Lie) algebroid structure on
\sigma^{k}
is defined as a (Lie) algebroid structure on the vector bundle
l\big(E^{k}\big)\rightarrow E^{k-1}
compatible with the second grading. The idea here is to mimic the canonical inclusion
\mathrm{T}^{k}M\hookrightarrow\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}M
, which makes the higher tangent bundle
\mathrm{T}^{k}M
a sub-object of the tangent Lie algebroid of
\mathrm{T}^{k-1}M
. In this way the construction of
[
5
]
admits higher tangent bundles and also prolongations of algebroids as examples of higher (Lie) algebroids, similarly to our approach. However, these two notions of a higher algebroid are different, as can be easily seen from coordinate calculations already in order 2. The construction of
[
5
]
can be adapted to develop higher-order mechanics on
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifolds
[
4
]
, equivalent to our results
[
25
]
in comparable cases. So perhaps the difference here is of a philosophical nature. In the classical case: should we treat
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-order variational calculus on a manifold
M
as the first-order theory on
\mathrm{T}^{k-1}M
thus passing through unnecessary degrees of freedom (as would be in the spirit of
[
5
]
) or do we prefer to work directly with
\mathrm{T}^{k}M
, as is in our formalism.

Organization of the paper.
In Section
2
we reformulate the theory of (Lie) algebroids in the language of vector bundle comorphisms. Section
3
contains basic information concerning
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifolds and weighted structures. In Section
4
, using the result of the previous two sections, we formulate the definition of a higher algebroid, provide a few natural examples and study in detail higher algebroid lifts and the Lie axiom in Section
4.1
. In Section
5
we develop the framework of higher-order variational calculus on higher algebroids, putting emphasis on admissible variations and their relation with conservation laws. Section
6
contains a study of higher algebroid structures inherited from higher tangent bundles of a Lie group
G
. In particular, we characterize all higher subalgebroids and describe a class of quotients of
\mathrm{T}^{k}G/G
. In Section
7
we sketch a few perspectives of future research. Finally, in Appendix
A
we have hidden most of the proofs of more technical results appearing in this work.

1.1
Double vector bundles

We shall frequently work with
double vector bundles
, geometric objects sharing two (compatible) vector bundle structures. A typical example is the total space
\mathrm{T}E
of the tangent bundle of a vector bundle
\sigma\colon E\rightarrow M
. The two vector bundle structures are: the tangent projection
\tau_{E}\colon\mathrm{T}E\rightarrow E
, and
\mathrm{T}\sigma\colon\mathrm{T}E\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M
, the tangent lift of
\sigma
. The foundations of the theory of double vector bundles were laid by J. Pradines
[
41
]
(see also
[
34
, Chapter 9]
for basic definitions, examples and historical remarks). Due to the results of
[
16
]
we may formulate a definition of a double vector bundle in the following way.

Definition 1.1

(double vector bundle)
.

A structure of a
double vector bundle
(
DVB
, in short) on a manifold
D
is a pair of vector bundles
\sigma_{A}\colon D\rightarrow A
,
\sigma_{B}\colon D\rightarrow B
such that for any
t,s\in\mathbb{R}
and
x\in D

\displaystyle t\cdot_{A}s\cdot_{B}x=s\cdot_{B}t\cdot_{A}x,

(1.1)

where
\cdot_{A}
and
\cdot_{B}
denote the multiplications by scalars in
\sigma_{A}
and
\sigma_{B}
, respectively.

A
morphism of DVBs

(D,\sigma_{A},\sigma_{B})
and
(D^{\prime},\sigma_{A^{\prime}},\sigma_{B^{\prime}})
is a smooth map
\phi\colon D\rightarrow D^{\prime}
which is linear as a map from
\sigma_{A}
to
\sigma_{A}^{\prime}
and, simultaneously, as a map from
\sigma_{B}
to
\sigma_{B^{\prime}}
.

The bases
A
,
B
of a double vector bundle as above carry induced vector bundle structures over the common base
M
giving rise to a diagram

\textstyle{D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{B}}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma^{B}_{M}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma^{A}_{M}}
\textstyle{M}

consisting of four vector bundle projections. All
M
,
A
,
B
can be seen as submanifolds of
D
via the zero section embeddings. In particular, the zero section
0_{A}\colon A\rightarrow D
defines a vector bundle structure on
A
as a substructure of the vector bundle structure on
\sigma_{B}\colon D\rightarrow B
. Double vector bundles are often said to be ‘vector bundles in the category of vector bundles’ what can be understood as the condition that all four structure maps of
\sigma_{A}
(the bundle projection
\sigma_{A}
, the zero section
0_{A}
, the scalar multiplication
\cdot_{A}
and the addition
+_{A}
) are vector bundle morphism with respect to the vector bundle structure of
\sigma_{B}
. The notion of a double vector bundle naturally generalizes to the notion of a multi-
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold (see Section
3
).

2
(Lie) algebroids as vector bundle comorphisms

In this section we characterize Lie algebroids and their relaxed versions (such as generalized algebroids in the sense of Grabowski–Urbański
[
19
]
) by means of differential relations of a special kind (vector bundle comorphisms, VBC in short). This will be the cornerstone of our definition of higher algebroids in Section
4
.

2.1
The category of vector bundle comorphisms

Following
[
22
,
34
]
we will recall a definition of a
comorphism
between vector bundles (VBC, in short). Later we shall study basic properties of VBCs, i.e., the corresponding map on sections, morphisms between VBCs, and VBCs compatible with an additional linear structure.

Vector bundle comorphisms.

Definition 2.1

(vector bundle comorphism, VBC)
.

A
vector bundle comorphism
(
VBC
, in short) from a vector bundle
\sigma_{1}\colon E_{1}\rightarrow M_{1}
to a vector bundle
\sigma_{2}\colon E_{2}\rightarrow M_{2}
is a relation
r\subset E_{1}\times E_{2}
of a special form. Namely, there exists an ordinary vector bundle morphism
\phi\colon E_{2}^{\ast}\rightarrow E_{1}^{\ast}
covering a smooth map
\underline{\phi}\colon M_{2}\rightarrow M_{1}
such that
r=\phi^{\ast}
, i.e.,
r
is the union of graphs of linear maps
r_{y}\colon(E_{1})_{\underline{\phi}(y)}\rightarrow(E_{2})_{y}
such that
\phi_{y}\colon(E_{2}^{\ast})_{y}\rightarrow(E_{1}^{\ast})_{\underline{\phi}(y)}
is the dual of the linear map
r_{y}
, where
y
varies in
M_{2}
. We also say that the VBC
r

covers
the base map
\underline{r}:=\underline{\phi}
. Usually we shall denote a VBC as
r\colon\sigma_{1}\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\sigma_{2}
or as a diagram

\textstyle{E_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{1}}
\scriptstyle{r}
\textstyle{E_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{2}}
\textstyle{M_{1}}
\textstyle{M_{2}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\underline{r}}

In particular, the VBC
r
is a vector subbundle of
\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{2}
.

There is a general concept of comorphisms between fibre bundles, (Lie) algebroid comorphisms and the corresponding (Lie) groupoid comorphisms. A full presentation of these ideas can be found in
[
7
]
and
[
34
]
with details on the history of these and related notions. The concept of a comorphism can be dated back at least to Bourbaki
[
2
]
and has been appearing ever since, sometimes under different names, see, e.g.,
[
20
,
21
,
23
]
. We would like to acknowledge that VBC is the simplest example of a groupoid morphism in the sense of Zakrzewski
[
53
,
54
]
. In the appendix of
[
27
]
we studied some properties of VBCs (using the name
Zakrzewski morphism
proposed in this context in
[
13
]
).

Remark 2.2

(VBCs and maps on sections)
.

An important property of a VBC is that it induces a mapping between the corresponding spaces of sections. Namely, given a VBC
r\colon\sigma_{1}\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\sigma_{2}
as in Definition
2.1
and a sections
s\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M_{1}}(E_{1})
we define
\hat{r}(s)\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M_{2}}(E_{2})
by

\displaystyle\hat{r}(s)(y):=r_{y}(s(\underline{r}(y))),\qquad\text{for every $y\in M_{2}$.}

In other words, the value of
\hat{r}(s)
at a given point
y\in M_{2}
is the unique element of the fibre
(E_{2})_{y}
which is
r
-related to the value of
s
at
\underline{r}(y)
.
By the linearity of
r
, for every two sections
s,s^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M_{1}}(E_{1})
we have
\hat{r}(s+s^{\prime})=\hat{r}(s)+\hat{r}(s^{\prime})
. Moreover, by construction, for any
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M_{1})

\displaystyle\hat{r}(f\cdot s)=\underline{r}^{\ast}(f)\cdot\hat{r}(s).

(2.1)

Conversely, any
{\mathbb{R}}
-linear map
\operatorname{Sec}_{M_{1}}(E_{1})\rightarrow\operatorname{Sec}_{M_{2}}(E_{2})
satisfying (
2.1
) for some underlying map
\underline{r}\colon M_{2}\rightarrow M_{1}
gives rise to a VBC
r\colon\sigma_{1}\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\sigma_{2}
. Checking this property is left to the reader.

Morphisms between VBCs.
A VBC can describe a single geometric object. As we shall see shortly (Lemma
2.11
and Proposition
2.15
), a (Lie) algebroid structure on a bundle
\sigma\colon E\rightarrow M
is fully encoded by a VBC
\kappa
from the tangent lift
\mathrm{T}\sigma\colon\mathrm{T}E\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M
to the tangent bundle
\tau_{E}\colon\mathrm{T}E\rightarrow E
. This suggests that the standard notion of a (Lie) algebroid morphism should be expressible by a, properly defined, morphism between the corresponding vector bundle comorphisms. Now we are going to introduce such a notion under the name
{\mathcal{VBC}}
-morphism, i.e., a morphism in the category
{\mathcal{VBC}}
of vector bundle comorphisms.

Definition 2.3

(
{\mathcal{VBC}}
-morphism)
.

Let
(\sigma_{i}\colon E_{i}\to M_{i})
and
(\sigma_{i}^{\prime}\colon E_{i}^{\prime}\to M_{i}^{\prime})
,
i=1,2
, be four VBs and let
r
and
r^{\prime}
be the following vector bundle comorphisms:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern (2.2)

We say that a pair
(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})
of VB morphisms
\phi_{i}\colon E_{i}\to E_{i}^{\prime}
covering smooth maps
\underline{\phi_{i}}\colon M_{i}\to M_{i}^{\prime}
,
i=1,2
, is a
{\mathcal{VBC}}
-morphism from
r
to
r^{\prime}
(and write
(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})\colon r\Rightarrow r^{\prime}
) if the following diagram

38.42532pt\raise-88.82335pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.20335pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\underline{r}^{\prime}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.6389pt\raise-95.0267pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 132.83331pt\raise-95.0267pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{split}

(2.3)

is commutative in the following sense:

(i)

The base maps commute, i.e.,
\underline{\phi_{1}}\circ\underline{r}=\underline{r}^{\prime}\circ\underline{\phi_{2}}\colon M_{2}\to M_{1}^{\prime}
.

(ii)

At the level of fibres, for any
y\in M_{2}
the following compositions of linear maps

\displaystyle E_{1,x}\xrightarrow{r_{y}}E_{2,y}\xrightarrow{\phi_{2}}E_{2,y^{\prime}}^{\prime}\qquad\text{and}\qquad E_{1,x}\xrightarrow{\phi_{1}}E_{1,x^{\prime}}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{r^{\prime}_{y^{\prime}}}E_{y^{\prime}}^{\prime}

coincide. Here
x=\underline{r}(y)
,
y^{\prime}=\underline{\phi_{2}}(y)
,
x^{\prime}=\underline{r}^{\prime}(y^{\prime})=\underline{\phi_{1}}(x)
are determined by
y\in M_{2}
.

It is easy to see that
{\mathcal{VBC}}
-morphisms can be naturally composed, and that VBCs with
{\mathcal{VBC}}
-morphisms form a
category of vector bundle comorphisms
, denoted
{\mathcal{VBC}}
.

Remark 2.4

(dualization of VBCs)
.

By dualizing the diagram (
2.3
) we get a diagram of the same type:

\textstyle{E_{1}^{\ast}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{1}^{\ast}}
\textstyle{E_{2}^{\ast}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{2}^{\ast}}
\textstyle{E_{1}^{{}^{\prime}\ast}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{1}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{(\sigma_{1}^{\prime})^{\ast}}
\textstyle{E_{2}^{{}^{\prime}\ast}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r^{{}^{\prime}\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{2}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{(\sigma_{2}^{\prime})^{\ast}}
\textstyle{M_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\underline{\phi_{1}}}
\textstyle{M_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\underline{\phi_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\underline{r}}
\textstyle{M_{1}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{M_{2}^{\prime}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\underline{r}^{\prime}}

It is clear that
(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})\colon r\Rightarrow r^{\prime}
is a
{\mathcal{VBC}}
-morphism if and only if
(r^{\prime\ast},r^{\ast})\colon\phi_{2}^{\ast}\Rightarrow\phi_{1}^{\ast}
is so. Thus the notion of a morphism in the category
{\mathcal{VBC}}
does not reduce to the notion of a vector bundle morphism.

Example 2.5

(phase lift)
.

Let
\underline{\phi}\colon M\rightarrow M^{\prime}
be a smooth map, and consider the phase lift of
\underline{\phi}
which is a VBC
\mathrm{T}^{\ast}\underline{\phi}\colon\mathrm{T}^{\ast}M^{\prime}\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\mathrm{T}^{\ast}M
covering
\underline{\phi}
. Then the tangent lift of
\mathrm{T}^{\ast}\underline{\phi}
is a VBC as well and
(\mathrm{T}\tau^{\ast}_{M^{\prime}},\tau_{\mathrm{T}^{\ast}M})\colon\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{\ast}\underline{\phi}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{\ast}\underline{\phi}
is a morphism in the category
{\mathcal{VBC}}
.

Another useful formulation of the conditions presented in the definition of a
{\mathcal{VBC}}
-morphism is possible:

Proposition 2.6

(characterization of
{\mathcal{VBC}}
-morphisms)
.

Let
\phi_{i}\colon E_{i}\rightarrow E_{i}^{\prime}

(i=1,2)
be vector bundle morphisms and let
r,r^{\prime}
be VBCs as in Definition
2.3
. Then
(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})\colon r\Rightarrow r^{\prime}
is a
{\mathcal{VBC}}
-morphism if and only if for any
r
-related vectors
X\in E_{1}
,
Y\in E_{2}
their images
\phi_{1}(X)
and
\phi_{2}(Y)
are
r^{\prime}
-related. Equivalently,
(\phi_{1}\times\phi_{2})(r)\subset r^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Assume that
(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})\colon r\Rightarrow r^{\prime}
is a
{\mathcal{VBC}}
-morphism and take
X
and
Y
such that
X\sim_{r}Y
. Denote
x:=\sigma_{1}(X)
,
y:=\sigma_{2}(Y)
,
x^{\prime}:=\underline{\phi_{1}}(x)
and
y^{\prime}:=\underline{\phi_{2}}(y)
. According to Definition
2.3

r^{\prime}_{y^{\prime}}(\phi_{1}(X))=\phi_{2}(Y)
, in other words
\phi_{1}(X)
and
\phi_{2}(Y)
are
r^{\prime}
-related. The proof the in reverse direction is analogous and is left to the reader.
∎

Linear VBCs.
Of our special interest will be
linear VBCs
, i.e., VBCs compatible with an additional linear structure. Later in Section
3
(Definition
3.6
) we will generalize this notion to the concept of
weighted VBCs
, which are compatible with an additional graded structure. We shall follow a general scheme already presented in the definitions of a
linear
Poisson structure,
\mathcal{VB}
-groupoids,
\mathcal{VB}
-algebroids, weighted algebroids etc.
[
3
,
34
]
.

Definition 2.7

(linear VBC)
.

Let
r
be a VBC from a vector bundle
\sigma\colon E\rightarrow M
to a vector bundle
\sigma^{\prime}\colon E^{\prime}\rightarrow M^{\prime}
. Assume that the total spaces
E
and
E^{\prime}
carry linear structures
\tau\colon E\rightarrow N
and
\tau^{\prime}\colon E^{\prime}\rightarrow N^{\prime}
compatible with
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
, respectively (i.e.,
E
and
E^{\prime}
are DVBs in the sense of Definition
1.1
). We say that
r
is a
linear VBC
if
r\subset E\times E^{\prime}
is a vector subbundle of
\tau\times\tau^{\prime}
.

Note that a linear VBC
r\subset E\times E^{\prime}
defined as above is a vector subbundle of both
\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime}
and
\tau\times\tau^{\prime}
. It follows that
r
projects to linear relations in
M\times M^{\prime}
and
N\times N^{\prime}
. In particular, the base map
\underline{r}\colon M^{\prime}\rightarrow M
is a VB morphism (cf. (
i
) and (
ii
) in Lemma
2.9
below). Particular examples of linear VBCs (not all) are provided by dualizing DVB morphisms.

Recall that if
\sigma\colon E\rightarrow M
is a vector bundle, then the tangent space
\mathrm{T}E
carries two compatible vector bundle structures
\mathrm{T}\sigma\colon\mathrm{T}E\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M
and
\tau_{E}\colon\mathrm{T}E\rightarrow E
. In the remaining part of this paragraph we shall study linear VBCs intertwining these two VB structures. The common kernel of
\mathrm{T}\sigma
and
\tau_{E}
(called the
core

\mathbf{C}(\mathrm{T}E)
of DVB
(\mathrm{T}E,\mathrm{T}\sigma,\tau_{E})
) is the
subbundle
C=\mathbf{V}_{M}E\simeq E
of the vertical bundle
\mathbf{V}E=\ker\mathrm{T}\sigma
consisting of vertical vectors based at
M\subset E
. The two additive structures of
\mathrm{T}E
coincide on the core
C
. Note that the core
C
acts on
\mathrm{T}E
by an addition of vertical vectors, i.e., for every
A\in\mathrm{T}_{a}E
and every
e\in C_{\sigma(a)}\simeq E_{\sigma(a)}
we define

\displaystyle A\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}e:=A+_{\tau_{E}}\mathbf{V}_{a}e\in\mathrm{T}_{a}E,

where
\mathbf{V}_{a}\colon\mathbf{V}_{\sigma(a)}E\rightarrow\mathbf{V}_{a}E\subset\mathrm{T}_{a}E
are canonical isomorphisms.
Note that the above action does not affects the two VB projections
\mathrm{T}\sigma
and
\tau_{E}
on
\mathrm{T}E
, i.e.,
\mathrm{T}\sigma(A\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}e)=\mathrm{T}\sigma(A)
and
\tau_{E}(A\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}e)=\tau_{E}(A)
. Moreover, the following useful identity holds

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}(f\cdot a)v=f\cdot_{\mathrm{T}\sigma}(\mathrm{T}a)v\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}v(f)\cdot a,

(2.4)

where
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
,
v\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
and
a\in\operatorname{Sec}(E)
.

Remark 2.8

(local form of a class of linear VBCs)
.

Let us write a general (local) form of a linear VBC
\kappa
intertwining the two compatible VB structures on
\mathrm{T}E
. For simplicity and further applications we also assume that
\kappa
projects onto
\operatorname{graph}(\operatorname{id}_{M})
under the bundle projection
\tau_{M}\circ\mathrm{T}\sigma\times\sigma\circ\tau_{E}\colon\mathrm{T}E\times\mathrm{T}E\rightarrow M\times M
, i.e.,
\kappa
relates only elements in the same fibre over
M
. (Note that
\tau_{M}\circ\mathrm{T}\sigma=\sigma\circ\tau_{E}
.) Let
(x^{a},y^{i})
be local linear coordinates on
E
. We use the standard notation
(x^{a},y^{i},\dot{x}^{b},\dot{y}^{j})
for induced coordinates on the tangent bundle
\mathrm{T}E
and, to describe
\kappa\subset\mathrm{T}E\times\mathrm{T}E
, we shall underline coordinates in the second copy of
\mathrm{T}E
.

As
\kappa
is a double vector subbundle of
(\mathrm{T}E\times\mathrm{T}E,\mathrm{T}\sigma\times\tau_{E},\tau_{E}\times\mathrm{T}\sigma)
, the graph of the base map
\rho_{L}:=\underline{\kappa}\colon E\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M
of
\kappa
is a vector subbundle in
E\times\mathrm{T}M
, i.e., it is a graph of a VB morphism. Thus it maps a vector
(\underline{x}^{a},\underline{y}^{i})
from
E
to a vector of the form
(x^{a}=\underline{x}^{a},\dot{x}^{b}=Q^{b}_{i}(x)\underline{y}^{i})
in
\mathrm{T}M
. Now, since for every fixed
a\in E
relation
\kappa_{a}\colon(\mathrm{T}E)_{\rho_{L}(a)}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}_{a}E
is a linear map, it turns out that
\kappa
is given by a linear mapping of coordinates
(y^{i},\dot{y}^{j})
to coordinates
(\underline{\dot{x}}^{b},\underline{\dot{y}}^{j})
with coefficients depending on coordinates
(\underline{x}^{a},\underline{y}^{i})
in
E
. Furthermore, we know that
\kappa
is bi-homogeneous. Calculation on weights:
w(y^{i})=(1,0)=w(\dot{\underline{x}}^{a})
,
w(\dot{x}^{a})=(0,1)=w(\underline{y}^{i})
,
w(\dot{y}^{i})=(1,1)=w(\dot{\underline{y}}^{i})
ensures us that
\kappa
is determined by equations of the form

\displaystyle\kappa\colon\ \begin{cases}x^{a}=\underline{x}^{a},\\
\dot{x}^{b}=Q^{b}_{i}(x)\,\underline{y}^{i},\\
\dot{\underline{x}}^{b}=\widetilde{Q}^{b}_{i}(x)y^{i},\\
\dot{\underline{y}}^{k}=\alpha^{k}_{i}(x)\dot{y}^{i}+Q^{k}_{ij}(x)\underline{y}^{i}y^{j},\end{cases}

(2.5)

where
x=(x^{a})
and
Q^{b}_{i}(x)
,
\widetilde{Q}^{b}_{i}(x)
,
\alpha^{j}_{i}(x)
and
Q^{k}_{ij}(x)
are some smooth functions defined locally on
M
.

Note that if we additionally assume that
\kappa
induces the identity on the core bundle, then the coefficients
\alpha^{j}_{i}(x)
are simply constants
\delta^{j}_{i}
. We shall show later (see Lemma
2.11
) that such linear VBCs correspond to (Lie) algebroid structures. The anchor maps (left and right) are locally given by functions
Q^{b}_{i}(x)
and
\widetilde{Q}^{b}_{i}(x)
, respectively, while functions
Q^{k}_{ij}(x)
encode the bracket operation in a given basis of sections of the bundle
\sigma
, dual to functions
(y^{i})
.

Lemma 2.9

(a class of linear VBCs)
.

Let
\sigma\colon E\rightarrow M
be a vector bundle and let
\kappa\colon\mathrm{T}\sigma\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\tau_{E}
be a linear VBC. Assume additionally that
\kappa
induces the identity on the core bundles, i.e.,
\kappa\cap(C\times C)=\operatorname{graph}(\operatorname{id}_{C})
. Then:

(i)

The base map
\rho_{L}:=\underline{\kappa}\colon E\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M
is a VB morphism covering the identity map
\operatorname{id}_{M}
.

(ii)

Relation
\kappa^{\prime}:=(\tau_{E}\times\mathrm{T}\sigma)(\kappa)\subset E\times\mathrm{T}M
, which is the
\tau_{E}\times\mathrm{T}\sigma
-projection of
\kappa
, is a graph of a VB morphism
\rho_{R}\colon E\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M
covering the identity map
\operatorname{id}_{M}
.

(iii)

Relation
\kappa
respects the action of the core
C
, i.e., if
(A,B)\in\kappa
then

(A\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}c,B\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}c)\in\kappa
, whenever the addition of
c\in C
makes sense.

(iv)

The inverse relation
\kappa^{T}
is a linear VBC from
\mathrm{T}\sigma
to
\tau_{E}
over the base map
\rho_{R}\colon E\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M
which also induces the identity on the cores.

Proof.

The assertion has a rather straightforward geometric justification based essentially on the bi-homogeneity of
\kappa
. For brevity, however, we prefer the following local argument.

Since
\kappa
is the identity on the cores, we conclude that
\kappa\cap(M\times M)=\Delta_{M}\subset M\times M
is a diagonal, and hence
\kappa
relates only the elements in the same fibres over
M
. Now we are precisely in the situation described in Remark
2.8
and the assertion follows easily from the local description (
2.5
). ∎

2.2
Description of (Lie) algebroids in terms of VBCs

In this part we shall recall the definition of a general algebroid, and latter rephrase it in the language of vector bundle comorphisms introduced above. A possibility of such a reformulation is of course well-recognized in the literature since the very introduction of the concept of a general algebroid
[
19
]
(see also
[
12
,
38
]
). However, this topic was never systematically studied for its own sake. In particular, we are not aware that the axioms of a (Lie) algebroid were ever directly formulated in terms of the corresponding VBCs. Despite this, we do not claim any originality in this area, as such a formulation is straightforward and natural. Our goal is rather to show the consistency and naturality of the approach to (Lie) algebroids based on differential relations (the notions of a subalgebroid, a morphism between algebroids, the Lie axiom, various specific types of algebroids, etc. are intrinsically defined within the category of vector bundle comorphisms). In consequence, we prepare the ground for a later definition of a higher algebroid in Section
4
.

General algebroids.
General algebroids were introduced by Grabowski and Urbański
[
18
,
19
]
as double vector bundle morphisms of a special kind. Their approach was motivated by the study of the geometry of mechanics and variational calculus originated by Tulczyjew
[
44
,
45
]
. Skew algebroids, almost-Lie algebroids and Lie algebroids may be regarded as special subclasses of this general notion.

Definition 2.10

(general algebroid)
.

A
general algebroid
structure on a vector bundle
\sigma\colon E\!\rightarrow\!M
is given by a bilinear
bracket

[\cdot,\cdot]
on the space of smooth sections of
\sigma
, together with a pair of vector bundle maps (
left
and
right anchors
)
\rho_{L},\rho_{R}\colon E\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M
over the identity on
M
such that

\displaystyle[f\cdot a,g\cdot b]=f\rho_{L}(a)(g)\cdot b-g\rho_{R}(b)(f)\cdot a+fg\cdot[a,b]

(2.6)

for every sections
a,b\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M}(E)
and every smooth functions
f,g\in C^{\infty}(M)
.

In addition

(i)

If the bracket is skew-symmetric, i.e.,
[a,b]=-[b,a]
(in particular, left and right anchors coincide, i.e.,
\rho_{L}=\rho_{R}=:\rho
and we speak simply about the
anchor
) we call
\sigma
a
skew algebroid
.

(ii)

If
\sigma
is a skew algebroid and the anchor
\rho
maps the algebroid bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]
to the Lie bracket of vector fields on
M
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\rho[a,b]=[\rho(a),\rho(b)]_{\mathrm{T}M},

(2.7)

we call
\sigma
an
almost-Lie algebroid
(AL algebroid, in short).

(iii)

If
\sigma
is an almost-Lie algebroid and the bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]
satisfies the Jacobi identity, we call
\sigma
a
Lie algebroid
.

We will refer to the objects from the above definition as to
(
Lie
)
algebroids
, i.e., writing that, say,
(\sigma,\rho_{L},\rho_{R},[\cdot,\cdot])
is a (Lie) algebroid, means that it is either a Lie algebroid, an AL algebroid, a skew algebroid, or just a general algebroid. By using the word “(Lie)” we emphasize that all the above concepts were derived from the standard notion of the Lie algebroid by relaxing its axioms. Moreover, this should prevent possible confusions with Courant algebroid and other notions of an algebroid present in the literature.

From (Lie) algebroids to VBCs.
We will now construct a canonical Zakrzewski morphism related with a given (Lie) algebroid structure. Recall Lemma
2.9
describing the structure of a class of linear VBCs intertwining the two VB structures on the total space of the tangent bundle
\mathrm{T}E
of a vector bundle
\sigma\colon E\rightarrow M
. It turns out that an algebroid structure on
\sigma
induces such a VBC.

Lemma 2.11

(from (Lie) algebroids to VBCs)
.

Let
(\sigma\colon E\rightarrow M,[\cdot,\cdot],\rho_{L},\rho_{R})
be a
(
Lie
)
algebroid. Formula

\displaystyle\kappa_{a}\left[\mathrm{T}b(\rho_{L}(a))\right]:=\mathrm{T}a(\rho_{R}(b))\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}[a,b],

(2.8)

where
a,b\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M}(E)
are arbitrary sections, extends to the unique relation
\kappa\subset\mathrm{T}E\times\mathrm{T}E
such that

(i)

\kappa
is a linear vector bundle comorphism from
\mathrm{T}\sigma\colon\mathrm{T}E\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M
to the tangent bundle
\tau_{E}\colon\mathrm{T}E\rightarrow E
covering the left anchor
\rho_{L}\colon E\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M
,

(ii)

\kappa
induces the identity on the core bundle, i.e.,
\kappa\cap(C\times C)=\operatorname{graph}(\operatorname{id}_{C})
.

Moreover, the
(\tau_{E}\times\mathrm{T}\sigma)
-projection of
\kappa
is the right anchor
\rho_{R}\colon E\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M
.

The proof is given in Appendix
A
.

Remark 2.12

(the inverse of
\kappa
)
.

Note that, according to Lemma
2.9
(
iv
), the inverse relation
\kappa^{T}
is also a linear
VBC (this time over the right anchor
\rho_{R}
) inducing the identity on the cores.
It clearly satisfies (we use the properties (
i
) and (
ii
) of
\kappa
to transform equality (
2.8
)) the condition

\displaystyle\kappa_{b}^{T}\left[\mathrm{T}a(\rho_{R}(b))\right]=\mathrm{T}b(\rho_{L}(a))\bm{\boldsymbol{-}}[a,b],

i.e., passing from
\kappa
to
\kappa^{T}
corresponds to changing the bracket
[a,b]
to
[a,b]^{T}:=-[b,a]
(note that this operation interchanges left and right anchors). Obviously
\kappa=\kappa^{T}
if the bracket is skew-symmetric.

Example 2.13

(the tangent algebroid)
.

The tangent bundle
\tau_{M}\colon\mathrm{T}M\rightarrow M
of a manifold
M
carries a canonical Lie algebroid structure. The left and right anchors are the identity maps
\rho_{L}=\rho_{R}=\operatorname{id}_{\mathrm{T}M}
and the bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathrm{T}M}
is the commutator of vector fields.

In this case the corresponding VBC is, in fact, an isomorphism of DVB
\kappa_{M}\colon\mathrm{T}\tau_{M}\rightarrow\tau_{\mathrm{T}M}
. If
(x^{a})
are local coordinates on
M
,
(x^{a},\dot{x}^{b})
induced coordinates on
\mathrm{T}M
and
(x^{a},\dot{x}^{b},\delta x^{c},\delta\dot{x}^{d})
induced coordinates on
\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}M
,
\kappa_{M}
is expressed as

\displaystyle\kappa_{M}\colon\ \big(x^{a},\dot{x}^{b},\delta x^{c},\delta\dot{x}^{d}\big)\longmapsto\big(x^{a},\delta x^{b},\dot{x}^{c},\delta\dot{x}^{d}\big).

Note that
\kappa_{M}
interchanges the two VB structures on
\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}M
,
\mathrm{T}\tau_{M}
and
\tau_{\mathrm{T}M}
.

Example 2.14

(the tangent lift of a (Lie) algebroid)
.

If
(\sigma\colon E\rightarrow M,\rho_{L},\rho_{R},[\cdot,\cdot])
is a (Lie) algebroid structure, then
\mathrm{T}\sigma\colon\mathrm{T}E\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M
also carries a canonical algebroid structure
(\mathrm{T}\sigma,\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}}\rho_{L}
,
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}}\rho_{R},[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}}})
called the
tangent lift of the algebroid
structure on
\sigma
. This structure is determined by conditions

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}}\rho_{L}=\kappa_{M}\circ\mathrm{T}\rho_{L},\qquad\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}}\rho_{R}=\kappa_{M}\circ\mathrm{T}\rho_{R}\qquad\text{and}\qquad[\mathrm{T}a,\mathrm{T}b]_{\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}}}=\mathrm{T}[a,b]

for any sections
a,b\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M}(E)
.

In this case (see
[
19
]
) the VBC
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}}\kappa
, corresponding to the considered algebroid structure on
\mathrm{T}\sigma
, is the tangent lift of the VBC
\kappa
corresponding to the initial algebroid structure on
\sigma
composed with two canonical isomorphisms
\kappa_{E}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}}\kappa=\kappa_{E}\circ\mathrm{T}\kappa\circ\kappa_{E}.

This construction has a natural generalization to higher tangent lifts
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma\colon\mathrm{T}^{k}E\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}M
. It will be discussed in detail in the second paragraph of Section
4.1
.

From VBCs to algebroids.
In fact, relation
\kappa
introduced in Lemma
2.11
completely characterizes the (Lie) algebroid structure on
\sigma
.

Proposition 2.15

(from VBCs to (Lie) algebroids)
.

Let
\sigma\colon E\rightarrow M
be a vector bundle. A linear vector bundle comorphism
\kappa
from
\mathrm{T}\sigma\colon\mathrm{T}E\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M
to the tangent bundle
\tau_{E}\colon\mathrm{T}E\rightarrow E
, which induces the identity on the core bundles, i.e.,
\kappa\cap(C\times C)=\operatorname{graph}(\operatorname{id}_{C})
, provides
\sigma
with the unique general algebroid structure.

The left anchor
\rho_{L}

(
resp., the right anchor
\rho_{R})
is given by the base map of
\kappa

(
resp., the base map of
\kappa^{T})
and the bracket is given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}_{a}[a,b]:=\kappa_{a}\left[\mathrm{T}b(\rho_{L}(a))\right]-\mathrm{T}a(\rho_{R}(b)),

(2.9)

for any sections
a,b\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M}(E)
.

The proof is given in Appendix
A
.

Remark 2.16

(alternative definition of a general algebroid)
.

By the results of Proposition
2.15
and Lemma
2.11
we can equivalently define a general algebroid structure
(\sigma,\rho_{L},\rho_{R},[\cdot,\cdot])
on
\sigma\colon E\rightarrow M
as a pair
(\sigma,\kappa)
, where
\kappa\colon\mathrm{T}\sigma\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\tau_{E}
is a linear VBC satisfying natural properties. In what follows we shall often refer to this description.

Remark 2.17

(the dual of
\kappa
)
.

The fact that
\kappa
induces the identity on the core implies that its dual (which is a proper vector bundle morphism, and even a DVB morphism by the linearity of
\kappa
)

\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{\ast}E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{E}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\kappa^{\ast}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}E^{\ast}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{T}\sigma^{\ast}}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{L}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}M}

covers the identity
\operatorname{id}_{E^{\ast}}
under the projections
\mathrm{T}^{\ast}E\rightarrow E^{\ast}
and
\mathrm{T}E^{\ast}\rightarrow E^{\ast}
(the core of a DVB becomes a side bundle under dualization
[
29
]
). In other words,
\kappa^{\ast}\colon\mathrm{T}E^{\ast}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{\ast}E\simeq\mathrm{T}^{\ast}E^{\ast}
corresponds to a linear bi-vector on
E^{\ast}
. This is an original point of view of
[
18
]
.

Remark 2.18

(a local form of
\kappa
)
.

By Remark
2.8
, the local form of a linear VBC corresponding to a given general algebroid structure is given by formulas (
2.5
) with
\alpha^{k}_{i}(x)=\delta^{k}_{i}
. Within this description
\rho_{L}\colon E\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M
, the base map of
\kappa
, is given by
\rho_{L}\colon E\ni(\underline{x}^{a},\underline{y}^{i})\longmapsto(x^{a}=\underline{x}^{a}
,
\dot{x}^{b}=Q^{b}_{i}(x)\underline{y}^{i})\in\mathrm{T}M
. Similarly, the right anchor reads as
\rho_{R}\colon E\ni(x^{a},y^{i})\longmapsto(\underline{x}^{a}=x^{a}
,
\dot{\underline{x}}^{b}=\widetilde{Q}^{b}_{i}(x)y^{i})\in\mathrm{T}M
.

Take now local sections
a(x)\sim(x^{a},y^{j}=a^{i}(x))
, and
b(x)\sim(x^{a},y^{j}=b^{i}(x))
of
E
. We can use formula (
2.5
) together with (
2.9
) to calculate a local expression for an algebroid bracket of these two sections. Simple calculations (which we omit here) lead to

\displaystyle[a,b](x)\sim\left(x^{a},y^{k}=\frac{\partial b^{k}}{\partial x^{a}}(x)Q^{a}_{i}(x)a^{i}(x)-\frac{\partial a^{k}}{\partial x^{a}}(x)\widetilde{Q}^{a}_{j}(x)b^{j}(x)+Q^{k}_{ij}a^{i}(x)b^{j}(x)\right).

(Lie) algebroid morphisms as
\bm{{\mathcal{VBC}}}
-morphisms.
Next we shall show that within the interpretation of (Lie) algebroids as vector bundle comorphisms, the notion of an algebroid morphism corresponds to a
{\mathcal{VBC}}
-morphism between appropriate relations.

Intuitively, a morphism between two (Lie) algebroid structures on
\sigma\colon E\rightarrow M
and on
\sigma^{\prime}\colon E^{\prime}\rightarrow M^{\prime}
should be a vector bundle map
\phi\colon E\rightarrow E^{\prime}
over
\underline{\phi}\colon M\rightarrow M^{\prime}
which intertwines the anchors and the algebroid bracket on sections. This intuition, however, faces immediate problems as, in general, a VB morphism
\phi
does not map sections of
\sigma
to sections of
\sigma^{\prime}
. This problem is solved by passing to the pull-back bundles.

Definition 2.19

(morphism of (Lie) algebroids,
[
34
]
)
.

Let
(\sigma\colon E\rightarrow M,[\cdot,\cdot],\rho_{L},\rho_{R})
and
(\sigma^{\prime}\colon E^{\prime}\rightarrow M^{\prime},[\cdot,\cdot]^{\prime},\rho_{L}^{\prime},\rho_{R}^{\prime})
be (Lie) algebroids. A VB morphism
\phi\colon E\rightarrow E^{\prime}
over
\underline{\phi}\colon M\rightarrow M^{\prime}
is a
morphism
between the algebroid structures on
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
if and only if the VB morphisms
\phi
and
\mathrm{T}\underline{\phi}
relate the left and the right anchors of
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 12.00694pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.97916pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 50.80556pt\raise-16.00891pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.21725pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\rho_{R}^{\prime}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 50.80556pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-12.00694pt\raise-32.01782pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{T}M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 17.04858pt\raise-26.17894pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.83888pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{T}\underline{\phi}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 36.00694pt\raise-32.01782pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 36.00694pt\raise-32.01782pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{T}M^{\prime},}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{split}

(2.10)

and if for every sections
a,b\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M}(E)
such that their push-forwards
\phi_{\ast}a,\phi_{\ast}b\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M}(\underline{\phi}^{\ast}E^{\prime})
can be (locally) presented as finite sums
\phi_{\ast}a=\sum_{i}f_{i}\cdot\underline{\phi}^{\ast}a_{i}
and
\phi_{\ast}b=\sum_{j}g_{j}\cdot\underline{\phi}^{\ast}b_{j}
for some functions
f_{i},g_{j}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
and some sections
a_{i},b_{j}\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M^{\prime}}(E^{\prime})
we have

\displaystyle\phi_{\ast}[a,b]=\sum_{i,j}\left(\rho_{L}(a)(g_{j})\cdot\underline{\phi}^{\ast}b_{j}-\rho_{R}(b)(f_{i})\cdot\underline{\phi}^{\ast}a_{i}+f_{i}g_{j}\cdot\underline{\phi}^{\ast}[a_{i},b_{j}]^{\prime}\right).

(2.11)

Remark 2.20

(on notion of an algebroid morphism)
.

For any
x\in M
there is a neighbourhood
U
of
x
in
M
and a neighbourhood
U^{\prime}
of
\underline{\phi}(x)
in
M^{\prime}
such that for any section
a
of
\sigma
we have
\phi_{\ast}a=\sum_{i}f_{i}\underline{\phi}^{\ast}e_{i}^{\prime}
where
(e_{i}^{\prime})
is a basis of sections of
\sigma^{\prime}
over
U^{\prime}
and
f_{i}
are some functions on
U
. This explains a local character of the above definition. There are other equivalent and more elegant formulation of the definition of an algebroid morphism – we discus some of them in Proposition
A.1
(see also
[
19
, Definition 3]
, where general algebroids are considered as a special type of Leibniz structures on the dual bundle and
[
15
]
for a generalization of this notion to an algebroid relation).

It may seem unclear if condition (
2.11
) is well-posed, i.e., if it does not depend on the presentation of
\phi_{\ast}a
and
\phi_{\ast}b
as finite sums of sections with
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
coefficients. To prove that this is the case, we check that the right-hand side of (
2.11
) is tensorial with respect to
a_{i}
and
b_{j}
(for this it is crucial that condition (
2.10
) holds). The essential calculations (for a simpler case of a skew algebroid) can be found in the classical book
[
34
]
.

It turns out that the above definition has a very elegant (and much simpler) interpretation in the language of VBCs naturally related with the (Lie) algebroid structure.

Proposition 2.21

((Lie) algebroid morphisms as
{\mathcal{VBC}}
-morphisms)
.

Let
(\sigma\colon E\!\rightarrow\!M,[\cdot,\cdot],\rho_{L},\rho_{R})
and
(\sigma^{\prime}\colon E^{\prime}\rightarrow M^{\prime},[\cdot,\cdot]^{\prime},\rho_{L}^{\prime},\rho_{R}^{\prime})
be
(
Lie
)
algebroids with the corresponding vector bundle comorphisms
\kappa\colon\mathrm{T}\sigma\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\tau_{E}
and
\kappa^{\prime}\colon\mathrm{T}\sigma^{\prime}\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\tau_{E^{\prime}}
, respectively. A VB map
\phi\colon E\rightarrow E^{\prime}
over
\underline{\phi}\colon M\rightarrow M^{\prime}
is a morphism between the algebroid structures on
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
if and only if
(\mathrm{T}\phi,\mathrm{T}\phi)
is a
{\mathcal{VBC}}
-morphism from
\kappa
to
\kappa^{\prime}
.

The proof is given in Appendix
A
.

Subalgebroids and algebroidal relations.
Let us now describe the notion of a subalgebroid in terms of VBCs. Recall the following definition.

Definition 2.22

(subalgebroid,
[
15
,
34
]
)
.

Let
(\sigma\colon E\rightarrow M,\rho_{L},\rho_{R},[\cdot,\cdot])
be a (Lie) algebroid. A vector subbundle
\sigma^{\prime}\colon E^{\prime}\rightarrow M^{\prime}
of
\sigma
is called a
subalgebroid
of
\sigma
if the following two conditions are satisfied

(i)

The restrictions of the anchors
\rho_{L}|_{E^{\prime}}
and
\rho_{R}|_{E^{\prime}}
map
E^{\prime}\subset E
to
\mathrm{T}M^{\prime}\subset\mathrm{T}M
.

(ii)

If sections
a,b\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M}(E)
are such that
a|_{M^{\prime}},b|_{M^{\prime}}\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M^{\prime}}(E^{\prime})
, then
[a,b]|_{M^{\prime}}\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M^{\prime}}(E^{\prime})
.

The first of the above conditions assures us that the bracket operation
[\cdot,\cdot]_{E^{\prime}}
defined on sections of
\sigma^{\prime}
by
[\widetilde{a},\widetilde{b}]_{E^{\prime}}:=[a,b]|_{M^{\prime}}
does not depend on the choice of the extensions
a,b\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M}(E)
of sections
\widetilde{a},\widetilde{b}\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M^{\prime}}(E^{\prime})
. The second condition guarantees that the section space
\operatorname{Sec}_{M^{\prime}}(E^{\prime})
is closed with respect to this bracket.
Clearly the subbundle
\sigma^{\prime}
carries a general algebroid structure inherited from
\sigma
.

In the face of the relationship between (Lie) algebroids and VBCs, the VBC
\kappa\colon\mathrm{T}\sigma\!\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\!\tau_{E}
corresponding to the algebroid structure on
\sigma
should induce some VBC
\kappa^{\prime}\colon\mathrm{T}\sigma^{\prime}\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\tau_{E^{\prime}}
corresponding to the structure of a subalgebroid on
\sigma^{\prime}
described above. We claim that such a
\kappa^{\prime}
is a
fine restriction
of
\kappa
in the sense of the definition below.

Definition 2.23

(fine restriction)
.

Let, for
i=1,2
,
\sigma_{i}^{\prime}\colon E_{i}^{\prime}\rightarrow M_{i}^{\prime}
be a vector subbundle of
\sigma_{i}\colon E_{i}\rightarrow M_{i}
, and let
r\colon\sigma_{1}\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\sigma_{2}
be a VBC over a base map
\underline{r}\colon M_{2}\rightarrow M_{1}
. We say that
r

restricts fine to
\sigma_{1}^{\prime}\times\sigma_{2}^{\prime}
if
\underline{r}(M_{2}^{\prime})\subset M_{1}^{\prime}
and if for any
X\in E_{1}
and
Y\in E_{2}
that are
r
-related and such that
X\in E_{1}^{\prime}
while
\sigma_{2}(Y)\in M_{2}^{\prime}
we have
Y\in E_{2}^{\prime}
. If this is the case, relation
r^{\prime}
, defined as the intersection of
r
with
E_{1}^{\prime}\times E_{2}^{\prime}
, defines a VBC from
\sigma_{1}^{\prime}
to
\sigma_{2}^{\prime}
.

The equivalence of the classical notion of a subalgebroid with the notion of a fine restriction of the corresponding VBC can be easily proved.

Proposition 2.24

(on subalgebroids)
.

Let
(\sigma\colon\!E\!\rightarrow\!M,\kappa)
be a
(
Lie
)
algebroid and let
\sigma^{\prime}\colon\!E^{\prime}\!\rightarrow\!M^{\prime}
be a vector subbundle of
\sigma
. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(i)

The subbundle
\sigma^{\prime}
is a subalgebroid of
\sigma
.

(ii)

The inclusion map
\iota\colon\sigma^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\sigma
is an algebroid morphism.

(iii)

The VBC
\kappa
restricts fine to
\mathrm{T}\sigma^{\prime}\times\tau_{E^{\prime}}\subset\mathrm{T}\sigma\times\tau_{E}
.

Proof.

It involves some elementary diagram-chasing to check that (
iii
) is equivalent to
(\mathrm{T}\iota,\mathrm{T}\iota)
being a
{\mathcal{VBC}}
-morphism between
\kappa^{\prime}
and
\kappa
. Due to Proposition
2.21
the latter condition is equivalent to (
ii
). Finally the equivalence of conditions (
i
) and (
ii
) is a standard fact in the theory of Lie algebroids (see
[
34
, Chapter 4]
).
∎

Following Grabowski
[
15
]
, we recall the concept of an algebroidal relation, which is a generalization of a morphism of algebroids. It is closely related to the notion of a subalgebroid.

Definition 2.25

(algebroidal relation)
.

Let
(\sigma_{1}\colon E_{1}\rightarrow M_{1},\kappa_{1})
and
(\sigma_{2}\colon E_{2}\rightarrow M_{2},\kappa_{2})
be (Lie) algebroids. A relation
r\subset E_{1}\times E_{2}
is called an
algebroidal relation
if the graph of
r
is a subalgebroid of the product algebroid
(\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{2},\kappa_{1}\times\kappa_{2})
.

If
r
is a vector bundle morphism, we recover the notion of an algebroid morphism (see Proposition
A.1
). Algebroidal relations have an elegant characterization in terms of the anchors and the algebroid brackets.

Proposition 2.26

(on algebroidal relations)
.

Let
(\sigma_{1}\colon E_{1}\rightarrow M_{1},\rho_{1L},\rho_{1R},[\cdot,\cdot]_{1})\simeq(\sigma_{1},\kappa_{1})
and
(\sigma_{2}\colon E_{2}\rightarrow M_{2},\rho_{2L},\rho_{2R},[\cdot,\cdot]_{2})\simeq(\sigma_{2},\kappa_{2})
be (Lie) algebroids and let
r\colon\sigma_{1}\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\sigma_{2}
be a VBC covering
\underline{r}\colon M_{2}\rightarrow M_{1}
.
The following are equivalent:

(i)

r
is an algebroidal relation,

(ii)

(a)

For any section
s_{1}\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M_{1}}(E_{1})
and
I=L,R
, the vector fields
\rho_{2I}(\hat{r}(s_{1}))
and
\rho_{1I}(s_{1})
are
\underline{r}
-related and

(b)

for any sections
s_{1},s_{1}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M_{1}}(E_{1})
we have

\displaystyle\hat{r}([s_{1},s_{1}^{\prime}]_{1})=[\hat{r}(s_{1}),\hat{r}(s_{1}^{\prime})]_{2}.

The proof is given in Appendix
A
.

Remark 2.27
.

We stress that although the anchor map is uniquely determined by the bracket operation in any general algebroid, the above condition (
iia
) does not follow from (
iib
). A simple counterexample is provided by the zero endomorphism in a general algebroid.

The following result states that fine restrictions of algebroidal relations to subalgebroids remain algebroidal relations.

Proposition 2.28

(restricting algebroidal relations)
.

Let, for
j=1,2
,
(\sigma_{j}^{\prime}\colon E_{j}^{\prime}\rightarrow M^{\prime}_{j},\kappa_{j}^{\prime})
be a subalgebroid of
(\sigma_{j}\colon E_{j}\rightarrow M_{j},\kappa_{j})
. Let us assume that a VBC

\textstyle{E_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{1}}
\scriptstyle{r}
\textstyle{E_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{2}}
\textstyle{M_{1}}
\textstyle{M_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\underline{r}}

restricts fine to
r^{\prime}\subset E_{1}^{\prime}\times E_{2}^{\prime}
. Then if
r
is an algebroidal relation then so is
r^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We should check that
r^{\prime}
is a subalgebroid of
(\sigma_{1}^{\prime}\times\sigma_{2}^{\prime},\kappa_{1}^{\prime}\times\kappa_{2}^{\prime})
. Since
r
restricts fine to
E_{1}^{\prime}\times E_{2}^{\prime}
, the relation
r^{\prime}
is a vector subbundle of
E_{1}^{\prime}\times E_{2}^{\prime}
. Let us take
X,Y\in\mathrm{T}E_{1}^{\prime}\times\mathrm{T}E_{2}^{\prime}
such that
X,Y
are
\kappa_{1}^{\prime}\times\kappa_{2}^{\prime}
-related and
X\in\mathrm{T}r^{\prime}
while
Y
lies over
y\in r^{\prime}\subset E_{1}^{\prime}\times E_{2}^{\prime}
. We have to show that
Y\in\mathrm{T}r^{\prime}
.

First note that
Y\in\mathrm{T}r
, because
r
respects algebroid structures of
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
, and
X,Y
are also
\kappa_{1}\times\kappa_{2}
-related as
E_{1}^{\prime}\times E_{2}^{\prime}
is a subalgebroid of
E_{1}\times E_{2}
, and
X\in\mathrm{T}r
while
y\in r^{\prime}\subset r
. Next, by
[
27
, Theorem A.5]
,
\mathrm{T}r
restricts fine to
\mathrm{T}E_{1}^{\prime}\times\mathrm{T}E_{2}^{\prime}
. Therefore, since
Y
belongs also to
\mathrm{T}E_{1}^{\prime}\times\mathrm{T}E_{2}^{\prime}
, we get
Y\in\mathrm{T}r^{\prime}=\mathrm{T}r\cap(\mathrm{T}E_{1}^{\prime}\times\mathrm{T}E_{2}^{\prime})
as we claimed.
∎

Characterization of algebroids with an additional structure.
We shall now express specific conditions (
i
)–(
iii
) from Definition
2.10
in terms of the corresponding VBC
\kappa
.

Lemma 2.29

(characterization of various algebroids)
.

Let
(\sigma\colon E\rightarrow M,[\cdot,\cdot],\rho_{L},\rho_{R})
be a general algebroid structure on
\sigma
and let
\kappa\colon\mathrm{T}\sigma\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\tau_{E}
be the corresponding vector bundle comorphism. Then
\sigma
is

(i)

A skew algebroid if and only if
\kappa
is symmetric, i.e., if
(X,Y)\in\kappa
then
(Y,X)\in\kappa

(
equivalently
\kappa=\kappa^{T})
.

(ii)

An almost-Lie algebroid if and only if it is skew, and
(\mathrm{T}\rho,\mathrm{T}\rho)\colon\kappa\Rightarrow\kappa_{M}
is a morphism of VBCs
(
in the sense of Definition
2.3
)
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 15.85735pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-10.59027pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{T}E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-15.85735pt\raise-15.63889pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.71112pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{T}\rho}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 37.83275pt\raise 4.50694pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\kappa}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 73.50696pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 4.5pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-4.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise
