86.34723pt\raise-15.63889pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.71112pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{T}\rho}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 86.34723pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-15.61806pt\raise-31.27777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 34.10008pt\raise-26.26805pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.00417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\kappa_{M}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 69.61806pt\raise-31.27777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 39.61806pt\raise-31.27777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 69.61806pt\raise-31.27777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}M.}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{split}

(2.12)

Here
\rho=\rho_{L}=\rho_{R}
is the anchor map.

(iii)

A Lie algebroid if and only if it is almost-Lie and
\kappa\subset\mathrm{T}E\times\mathrm{T}E
is a subalgebroid of the product algebroid
(\mathrm{T}\sigma\times\tau_{E},\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}}\kappa\times\kappa_{E})
. In other words,
\kappa\colon\mathrm{T}\sigma\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\tau_{E}
is an algebroidal relation.

The proof is given in Appendix
A
.

An application – prolongations of AL algebroids.

Throughout this section we argued that VBCs provide a consistent language to describe (Lie) algebroids, alternative to the standard treatment of the topic. Besides, some known constructions in the theory of (Lie) algebroids have more evident definitions in the language of vector bundle comorphism. To justify this claim we shall now give a non-standard definition of a prolongation of an AL algebroid over a fibration
[
11
,
21
]
, the crucial notion in the Lagrangian and Hamiltonian formalisms for mechanics on Lie algebroids developed by Martínez in
[
36
,
37
]
.

Example 2.30

(prolongation of an AL algebroid over a fibration)
.

Let
\pi\colon P\rightarrow M
be a fibration and let
(\sigma\colon E\rightarrow M,\kappa)
be an almost-Lie algebroid. Denote by
\rho\colon E\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M
the related anchor map. Then
\mathcal{T}^{E}P:=E\times_{\mathrm{T}M}\mathrm{T}P=\{(e,v)\in E\times\mathrm{T}P\colon\rho(e)=\mathrm{T}\pi(v)\}
is a vector bundle over
P
which carries an almost-Lie algebroid structure defined by the restriction of the relation
\kappa\times\kappa_{P}\colon\mathrm{T}E\times\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}P\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\mathrm{T}E\times\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}P
to
\mathrm{T}(\mathcal{T}^{E}P)\simeq\mathrm{T}E\times_{\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}M}\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}P
:

\textstyle{\mathrm{T}E\times_{\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}M}\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa\times\kappa_{P}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}E\times_{\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}M}\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}P}
\textstyle{E\times_{\mathrm{T}M}\mathrm{T}P.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Indeed, the only thing to check is that
\mathcal{T}^{E}P
is a subalgebroid of the product algebroid
(\sigma\times\tau_{P}\colon E\times\mathrm{T}P\rightarrow M\times P,\kappa\times\kappa_{P})
. This is straightforward, given
X,X^{\prime}\in\mathrm{T}E
and
Y,Y^{\prime}\in\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}P
such that
(X,X^{\prime})\in\kappa
,
Y^{\prime}=\kappa_{P}(Y)
, and
(X,Y)
is tangent to
\mathcal{T}^{E}P
, we need to prove that
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
is also tangent to
\mathcal{T}^{E}P
, i.e., that

\mathrm{T}\rho(X^{\prime})=\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}\pi(Y^{\prime})
. But
\mathrm{T}\rho(X^{\prime})=\kappa_{M}(\mathrm{T}\rho(X))
while
\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}\pi(Y^{\prime})=\kappa_{M}(\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}\pi(Y))
(due to the assumption that
\kappa
is almost-Lie) and the result follows.

Another non-standard characterization of the notion of the prolongation, also emphasizing the role of the AL axiom (
2.7
), was recently provided by one of us in
[
24
, Proposition 3.1]
.

3
Recollection of
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifolds

\mathbb{N}
-graded manifolds and homogeneity structures.

Higher order algebroids
which we shall introduce and study in the next section are modeled on geometric objects which generalize the notion of a vector bundle. Such a generalization (catching, in particular, the canonical graded structure of the higher tangent bundle
\mathrm{T}^{k}M
– a fundamental example from the point of view of physical applications) was first proposed by Voronov in
[
47
]
, within the framework of supergeometry, as non-negatively graded (
\mathbb{N}
-graded, for short) manifolds. Voronov noticed that they expand into a tower of fibrations and suggested to see a non-negative grading as a generalization of a linear structure. In this paper we choose to work with
purely even non-negatively graded manifolds
, a particular subclass of Voronov’s objects. In
[
17
]
they are referred to as
graded bundles
(see also the introduction to
[
6
]
). Here we shall recall basic properties and constructions associated with these objects.

An important example is the
higher
(k^{\mathrm{th}}
-order
)
tangent bundle
\tau^{k}_{M}\colon\mathrm{T}^{k}M\rightarrow M
of a manifold
M
, consisting of
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-order tangency classes (called
k
-velocities
) of curves in
M
. Bundle
\mathrm{T}^{1}M=\mathrm{T}M
is just the tangent bundle of
M
, however for
k>1
,
\tau^{k}_{M}
is no longer a vector bundle. We shall see this at the elementary level.

Given a smooth function
f
on a manifold
M
and an integer
\alpha=0,1,\ldots,k
one can construct a function
f^{(\alpha)}
on
\mathrm{T}^{k}M
, the so-called
\alpha
-lift of
f
(see
[
40
]
). It is defined by

\displaystyle f^{(\alpha)}(\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k}\gamma):=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{\alpha}}{\mathrm{d}t^{\alpha}}\right|_{t=0}f(\gamma(t)),

where
\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k}\gamma
denotes the
k
-jet of a curve
\gamma\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow M
at zero. We shall usually write
\dot{f}
,
\ddot{f}
instead of
f^{(1)}
,
f^{(2)}
, respectively. The
adapted coordinates

(x^{a},\dot{x}^{a},\ddot{x}^{a},\ldots)
for
\mathrm{T}^{k}M
, induced by coordinates
(x^{a})
on
M
, are obtained by applying the above lifting procedure to coordinate functions
x^{a}
, and are naturally graded. (We simply assign
weight

k
to coordinates
x^{a,(k)}:=(x^{a})^{(k)}
.) In particular, on
\mathrm{T}^{2}M
they transform as

\displaystyle x^{a^{\prime}}=x^{a^{\prime}}(x),\qquad\dot{x}^{a^{\prime}}=\frac{\partial x^{a^{\prime}}}{\partial x^{b}}\dot{x}^{b},\qquad\ddot{x}^{a^{\prime}}=\frac{\partial x^{a^{\prime}}}{\partial x^{b}}\ddot{x}^{b}+\frac{\partial^{2}x^{a^{\prime}}}{\partial x^{b}\partial x^{c}}\dot{x}^{b}\dot{x}^{c}

(3.1)

thus the weight of both left and right sides of the above equalities is the same. From a geometric point of view, fibres of
\tau^{k}_{M}
are equipped with a special structure. Namely, we have a canonical action of the multiplicative monoid of real numbers
(\mathbb{R},\cdot)
on these fibres defined by re-parametrizing curves representing the elements of
\mathrm{T}^{k}M
:

\displaystyle h\colon\ \mathbb{R}\times\mathrm{T}^{k}M\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}M,\qquad\big(t,\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k}\gamma\big)\mapsto\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k}\gamma_{t},

where
\gamma_{t}(s)=\gamma(ts)
. We clearly see, that the
\alpha
-lift of a function
f
on
M
is a
homogeneous
function on
\mathrm{T}^{k}M
of
weight

\alpha
, i.e.,

\displaystyle f^{(\alpha)}(h_{t}(v))=t^{\alpha}f^{(\alpha)}(v),

where
h_{t}(v):=h(t,v)
. This also explains why the adapted coordinates on
\mathrm{T}^{k}M
are graded.

Properties of the higher tangent bundle
\mathrm{T}^{k}M
motivate the concepts of a non-negatively graded manifold and a homogeneity structure. The definition below is equivalent to the definition of a purely even positively-graded manifold according to Voronov
[
47
, Definition 4.1]
. Here we reformulate the condition of “cylindricity” of positive even coordinates from that definition as the condition that fibers are diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Definition 3.1

(
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold
[
47
]
)
.

A
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold
is a smooth fibration
\sigma^{k}\colon\!E^{k}{\rightarrow}M
in which we are given a distinguished class of fibre coordinates (called
graded coordinates
) with non-negative integer
weights
assigned. Moreover, it is assumed that these graded coordinates identify the fibres with
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(for some integer
n
), and that the transition functions are multi-variable polynomials that preserve the weights. The index
k
in
E^{k}
indicates that the weights on
E^{k}
are less or equal
k
. We say that
E^{k}
has
order

k
.

A
homogeneity structure
(the idea of which dates back at least to
[
51
]
) is a manifold
E
equipped with a smooth action
h\colon\mathbb{R}\times E\rightarrow E
of the multiplicative monoid of real numbers
(\mathbb{R},\cdot)
. Surprisingly, both concept coincide in the smooth setting
[
17
, Theorem 4.2]
, in particular, every
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold
\sigma^{k}\colon E^{k}\rightarrow M
admits a canonical homogeneity structure
h^{E^{k}}\colon\mathbb{R}\times E^{k}\rightarrow E^{k}
. For this reason we shall use the terms
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold and homogeneity structure interchangeably.

Graded coordinates on a
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold
\sigma^{k}\colon E^{k}\rightarrow M
can be denoted by
(x^{a},y^{i}_{w})
where a (superfluous) index
w=w(i)\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
at
y^{i}
indicates that a fibre coordinate
y^{i}
is homogeneous of weight
w
. The base coordinates
(x^{a})
are assumed to have weight zero. In such a notation the associated homogeneity structure reads as

\displaystyle h\colon\ \mathbb{R}\times E^{k}\rightarrow E^{k},\qquad h\big(t,\big(x^{a},y^{i}_{w}\big)\big)=\big(x^{a},t^{w}y^{i}_{w}\big),\qquad t\in\mathbb{R}.

It is convenient and fruitful to encode the structure of a
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold by means of a canonical vector field on
E^{k}
called the
weight vector field
which in graded coordinates is given by

\displaystyle\Delta=\sum_{i}w\,y^{i}_{w}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}_{w}}.

For example, the canonical weight vector field on
\mathrm{T}^{k}M
is

\Delta_{M}^{k}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{k}\sum_{a}\alpha x^{a,(\alpha)}\partial_{x^{a,(\alpha)}}
. In fact, the weight vector field provides an equivalent characterization of the structure of a
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold. For brevity, we often refer to a
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold as to a pair
(E^{k},\Delta)
.

A
morphism
from a
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold
\sigma_{E}\colon E^{k}\rightarrow M
to
\sigma_{F}\colon F^{k}\rightarrow M
is a smooth map
\phi\colon E^{k}\rightarrow F^{k}
commuting with the respective homogeneity structures, i.e.,
\phi\circ h^{E}=h^{F}\circ\phi
. Equivalently
\phi
relates the corresponding weight vector fields
\Delta_{E}
and
\Delta_{F}
.
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifolds of order
k
form a
category
denoted by
\mathcal{GM}[k]
. In view of
[
16
, Theorem 2.4]
,
\mathcal{GM}[1]
is the category of vector bundles.

Every
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold has a
zero section

0_{M}^{k}\colon M\rightarrow E^{k}
. The image of
M
in
E^{k}
is defined by
putting to zero

[
6
, Example 1.10]
all graded coordinates of positive weight. More generally, one can invariantly put to zero all
fibre
coordinates of weights less than a given number
j
. The obtained subset of
E^{k}
is denoted by
E^{k}[\Delta\geq j]
and it is, actually, a graded subbundle of
E^{k}
.

Given a
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold
\big(E^{k},\Delta\big)
of order
k
and an integer
0\leq j\leq k
one can
construct a canonical projection from
E^{k}
onto a
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold of order
j
, denoted by
E^{k}[\Delta\leq j]
, obtained by
removing all coordinates of weights greater than

j

[
6
, Definition 1.6]
. As transformation rules for
E^{k}
of coordinates of weight
\leq j
involve only coordinates of weights
\leq j
the above construction is correct. It follows that
a
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold
\sigma^{k}\colon E^{k}\to M
induces a
tower of affine fibrations

[
47
]

\displaystyle E^{k}\xrightarrow{\sigma^{k}_{k-1}}E^{k-1}\to\cdots\xrightarrow{\sigma^{2}_{1}}E^{1}\xrightarrow{\sigma^{1}_{0}}M,

(3.2)

where
E^{k}[\Delta\leq j]
is denoted shortly by
E^{j}
.

Definition 3.2

(the top core of a
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold)
.

Let
\sigma^{k}\colon E^{k}\rightarrow M
be a
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold of order
k
. The
top core
of
E^{k}
, denoted by
\widehat{E^{k}}
, is the set

\displaystyle\widehat{E^{k}}=\big\{v\in E^{k}\colon\sigma^{k}_{k-1}(v)\in 0_{M}^{k-1}(M)\subset E^{k-1}\big\},

where
\sigma^{k}_{k-1}\colon E^{k}\rightarrow E^{k-1}
as in (
3.2
). Locally,
\widehat{E^{k}}
is defined by
putting to zero
all fibre coordinates of weights less than
k
:

\displaystyle\widehat{E^{k}}=\big\{\big(x^{A},y^{a}_{w}\big)\colon y^{a}_{w}=0\text{ for any }y^{a}_{w}\text{ such that }1\leq w<k\big\}\subset E^{k},

hence
\big(x^{A},y^{a}_{k}\big)
are local coordinates on
\widehat{E^{k}}
. The top core
\widehat{E^{k}}
is naturally a vector bundle over
M
with the homotheties defined locally by

\displaystyle t.\big(x^{A},y^{a}_{k}\big)=\big(x^{A},t\cdot y^{a}_{k}\big)

for
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, the top core
\widehat{\cdot}
is a functor from the category
\mathcal{GM}[k]
to the category of vector bundles.

Example 3.3

(split
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifolds)
.

Given a sequence of vector bundles
E_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,k
, over the same base manifold
M
we can turn the Whitney sum
E=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{k}E_{j}
into a
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold of order
k
by assuming that linear coordinates on fibres of
E_{j}
have weight
j
. The obtained
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold will be denoted by
{\sf X}_{j=1}^{k}E_{j}[j]
, where the notation
V[j]
means that we assign weight
j
to linear coordinates on a vector space
V
. In other words, we have a functor from the category of graded vector bundles supported in degrees
-1,-2,\ldots,-k
to the category
\mathcal{GM}[k]
of
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifolds of order
k
.

\mathbb{N}
-graded manifolds equipped with a
splitting
, i.e., a
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold isomorphism
p\colon E^{k}\rightarrow{\sf X}_{j=1}^{k}E_{j}
as above are called
split
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifolds
. It is worth to remember that in the smooth real setting any
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold is (non-canonically) isomorphic to its
split form
which is obtained from the sequence of the top core bundles
\widehat{E^{j}}
,
j=1,\ldots,k
:
E^{k}\simeq{\sf X}_{j=1}^{k}\widehat{E^{j}}
.

Multi-
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifolds.
Of particular interests are geometric objects which admit several compatible
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold structures. Multi-grading appeared as a powerful tool in Voronov’s paper
[
50
]
, were it was used to study Mackenzie’s double Lie algebroids
[
32
]
.
We define a
k
-tuple
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold

(E,\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{k})
to be a manifold
E
with
k
pair-wise commuting weight vector fields, i.e.,
[\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j}]=0
for any
i,j=1,\ldots,k
. Equivalently,
E
admits
k
pair-wise commuting homogeneity structures
h^{i}\colon\mathbb{R}\times E\rightarrow E
with
i=1,\ldots,k
. That is,
h^{i}_{t}\circ h^{j}_{s}=h^{j}_{s}\circ h^{i}_{t}
for any
i,j=1,2,\ldots,k
and any
t,s\in\mathbb{R}
.
Interestingly, every
k
-tuple
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold admits local coordinates which are simultaneously graded with respect to any of its graded structures
[
17
, Theorem 5.2]
.

Double vector bundles, the tangent and cotangent bundles of a
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold
\sigma^{k}\colon\!E^{k}{\rightarrow}M
are examples of double
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifolds. There are numerous other constructions of multi-
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifolds: the iterated higher tangent bundles
\mathrm{T}^{k_{1}}\cdots\mathrm{T}^{k_{r}}M
, the substructures and quotients (e.g., the construction of the linearization of a
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold, the vertical subbundle
\mathbf{V}E^{k}=\ker{\mathrm{T}\sigma^{k}}\subset TE^{k}
) and others (see
[
6
, Definitions 1.6 and 1.8]
for “the removal of coordinates of weight greater than a given number
l
” and “putting to zero coordinates of
X
-negative weights” where
X
is linear combination of weight vector fields
\Delta_{i}
).

The notion of core of a DVB has a natural generalization for multi-
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifolds.

Definition 3.4

(the core and the ultracore of a multi-
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold)
.

Let
(E,\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{k})
be a
k
-tuple
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold and
k>1
. The
core
\mathbf{C}(E)
of
E
is obtained by putting to zero all fiber coordinates
y^{i}_{w}
with multi-weight
w
in which at least one of
w_{1},\ldots,w_{k}
is zero.
Formally, using the construction
E[X\geq 0]

[
6
, Definition 1.8]
of putting to zero all fiber coordinates with negative weight with respect to a vector field
X
being a linear combination of weight vector fields
\Delta_{i}
, we may write the core of
E
as the intersection

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}(E):=\bigcap_{j=1}^{k}E\bigg[N\Delta_{j}-\sum_{i\neq j}\Delta_{i}\geq 0\bigg],

for a sufficient large number
N
. Indeed,
E[N\Delta_{j}-\sum_{i\neq j}\Delta_{i}\geq 0]
is the subset of
E
obtained by putting to zero all
fiber
coordinates
y^{i}_{w}
of the total fibration
E\rightarrow M=E[\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{2}=\cdots=\Delta_{k}=0]
with multi-weight
w=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{k})
in which
w_{j}=0
. In general,
\mathbf{C}(E)
remains a
k
-tuple
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold.

By the
ultracore
of
E
we mean the top core
\widehat{E}
of the
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold
(E,\Delta_{1}+\cdots+\Delta_{k})
.

Note that for a double vector bundle, the core in the above sense coincides with the usual notion of the core of a DVB. What is more, our definition of the ultracore coincides with the one introduced by Mackenzie

[
33
]
.

Graded-linear manifolds.

Let us now take a closer look at double
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifolds with one of the homogeneity structures being linear. If
\big(E,h^{1},h^{2}\big)
is such a structure, we may treat it as a vector bundle
\sigma\colon E\rightarrow M
(say, that the VB structure on
E
corresponds to the homogeneity structure
h^{2}
) equipped with a
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold structure encoded in the homogeneity structure
h^{1}\colon\mathbb{R}\times E\rightarrow E
such that the structure maps of the vector bundle
\sigma
(vector bundle projection, zero section, addition, and scalar multiplication) are
weighted
or, in other words, homogeneous with respect to
h^{1}
. In particular, for each
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, the mapping
v\mapsto\lambda\cdot_{\sigma}v
,
v\in E
, should be a
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold morphism, or equivalently, it should commute with the homotheties
h^{2}_{t}
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
. A double
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold
\big(E,h^{1},h^{2}\big)
in which
\big(E,h^{2}\big)
is a vector bundle is called a
graded-linear manifold
or, equivalently, a
weighted vector bundle
, when we want to put emphasis on the underlining linear structure. In the language of
[
3
]
, we would speak about
graded-linear bundles
. A graded-linear manifold
\sigma\colon E\rightarrow M
can be depicted in a diagram

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 25.00554pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-9.43924pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{E^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.9391pt\raise 6.74501pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.74501pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\sigma^{k}_{0}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 67.66148pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-25.00554pt\raise-16.49501pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-5.37674pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\sigma^{\{k\}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 34.85591pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 67.66148pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{E^{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 76.8837pt\raise-16.49501pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-5.37674pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\sigma^{\{0\}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 76.8837pt\raise-24.34999pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-10.85591pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{M^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.75606pt\raise-25.68945pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.80057pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\underline{\sigma}^{k}_{0}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 64.85591pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 34.85591pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 64.85591pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{M^{0}.}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{split}

(3.3)

Here
E^{k}=E
,
\sigma^{\{k\}}=\sigma
and the superscript
k
at
E
indicates the order of the homogeneity structure. The base
M=M^{k}
of the vector bundle
\sigma
carries a
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold structure induced from
\sigma^{k}_{0}\colon E^{k}\rightarrow E^{0}
. The notation in parentheses
\{\ \}
is used in order to distinguish the vector bundle projection
\sigma^{\{k\}}
given by the homotheties
h^{2}
from the
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold projection
\sigma^{k}_{0}
given by the homogeneity structure
h^{1}
.

An upcoming definition of a higher (Lie) algebroid involves two particular examples of graded-linear manifolds: the tangent bundle of a
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold of order
k
, and the
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-order tangent bundle of a vector bundle. Thus it will be important to understand the core bundles of these examples in greater detail. For this we shall show now that the core of any graded-linear manifold admits a canonical splitting into a direct sum of vector bundles. In the special case of a double vector bundle we recover the fact that the core is a vector bundle over the final base
M
.

Proposition 3.5

(the core of a graded-linear manifold)
.

The core of any order-
k
graded-linear manifold
(D,\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2})
is a graded vector bundle, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}(D)=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{k}D_{j}[j]

for some vector bundles
D_{j}
canonically associated with
D
. In particular cases,

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}E\big)\simeq E[1]\oplus E[2]\oplus\cdots\oplus E[k],\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}(\mathrm{T}E^{k})\simeq E^{1}[1]\oplus\widehat{E^{2}}[2]\oplus\cdots\oplus\widehat{E^{k}}[k].

Proof.

Bi-graded coordinates on
D
have the form
\big(x^{A},y^{a}_{(i,j)}\big)
where
i=0
or
1
and
0\leq j\leq k
, and
(i,j)\neq(0,0)
. As
C:=\mathbf{C}(D)
is given locally in
D
by equations
y^{a}_{(1,0)}=0
and
y^{a}_{(0,j)}=0
,
1\leq j\leq k
, the transformation rules for the fibre coordinates
y^{a}_{(1,j)}|_{C}
,
j=1,\ldots,k
, are of the form
y^{a^{\prime}}_{(1,j)}|_{C}=Q^{a^{\prime}}_{j,b}\,y^{b}_{(1,j)}|_{C}
for some functions
Q^{a^{\prime}}_{j,b}
on the final base
M
of
D
. It follows that the submanifold of
D
defined locally by vanishing all fibre coordinates except those of weight
{(1,j)}
, where
1\leq j\leq k
is a fixed integer, form a vector bundle
D_{j}
over
M
and
C
is just the Whitney sum of these vector bundles.
The vector bundle
D_{j}
can be recognized as the top core bundle of
(D[\Delta_{2}\leq j],\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2})
. The decomposition of the core of
\mathrm{T}^{k}E
and
\mathrm{T}E^{k}
are due to the isomorphisms
\widehat{\mathrm{T}^{k}E}\simeq E
and
\widehat{\mathrm{T}E^{k}}\simeq\widehat{E^{k}}
. ∎

Weighted structures and their reductions from ‘higher’ to ‘lower’ order.
In a similar manner we can introduce notion of other weighted geometrical objects and structures, in particular VBCs.

Definition 3.6

(weighted VBC)
.

Let
\sigma_{i}\colon E_{i}\rightarrow M_{i}
, where
i=1,2
, be weighted VBs of order
k
with homogeneity structures
h_{i}
on
E_{i}
. A VBC
r\colon\sigma_{1}\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\sigma_{2}
is called a
weighted VBC
if
r\subset E_{1}\times E_{2}
is homogeneous with respect to
h_{1}\times h_{2}
. In other words,
r
is a graded submanifold of the product of
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifolds
(\sigma_{1})^{k}_{0}\times(\sigma_{2})^{k}_{0}\colon E_{1}\times E_{2}\rightarrow E_{1}^{0}\times E_{2}^{0}
. (See diagram (
3.3
) for the notation concerning weighted vector bundles.) In particular, the base map
\underline{r}\colon M_{2}\rightarrow M_{1}
is a
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold morphism. (We may assume that orders of both the homogeneity structures are the same, as every
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold of order
k
is also of order
{k^{\prime}}
for any
k^{\prime}>k
.)

Remark 3.7

(on weighted structures)
.

Weighted (Lie) algebroids and weighted (Lie) groupoids considered in
[
3
]
are defined in the same spirit. One simply requests that the structure maps of a (Lie) algebroid (resp. a (Lie) groupoid) are graded, in some sense. Particular examples are
\mathcal{VB}
-algebroids and
\mathcal{VB}
-groupoids extensively studied in the literature. (The graded structure is then of order 1.) We shall not use these notions in our paper.

For a weighted structure we may try to perform a
reduction from a ‘higher’ to a ‘lower’ order
in the tower of affine fibrations (
3.2
). If the starting point is a graded-linear manifold
\big(E^{k},\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}\big)
of order
k
, then its
j
-order reduction
E^{j}=E^{k}[\Delta_{1}\leq j]
is still a graded-linear manifold and
\sigma^{k}_{j}\colon E^{k}\rightarrow E^{j}
is a morphism in the category of graded-linear manifolds.
In particular, given a weighted VBC
r\colon\sigma_{1}\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\sigma_{2}
between weighted vector bundles
\sigma_{i}\colon E^{k}_{i}\rightarrow M_{i}^{k}
(
i=1,2
) of order
k
we may consider
r
as a
\mathbb{N}
-graded submanifold in the product bundle
\sigma_{1}^{k}\times\sigma_{2}^{k}\colon E^{k}_{1}\times E^{k}_{2}\rightarrow E^{0}_{1}\times E^{0}_{2}
and then project it to get its lower-order reduction
r^{j}\colon E^{j}_{1}\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright E^{j}_{2}
, which is not only a graded-linear submanifold, but also a weighted VBC of order
j
, for
j=0,1,2,\ldots,k
. This and another useful observation in a similar spirit is presented below.

Proposition 3.8

(reduction of weighted VBCs)
.

Let
r\colon\sigma_{1}\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\sigma_{2}
be a weighted VBC where
\sigma_{i}\colon E_{i}^{k}\rightarrow M_{i}^{k}

(i=1,2)
are weighted vector bundles of order
k
, and let
0\leq j\leq k
. Then

(i)

the lower-order reductions
r^{j}\colon\sigma^{\{j\}}_{1}\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\sigma^{\{j\}}_{2}
remain weighted VBCs.

(ii)

If
r^{\prime}\colon\sigma_{1}^{\prime}\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\sigma_{2}^{\prime}
is another weighted VBC and if
(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})\colon r\Rightarrow r^{\prime}
, where
\phi_{i}\colon\sigma_{i}\rightarrow\sigma_{i}^{\prime}
are morphism of graded-linear manifolds, is a
{\mathcal{VBC}}
-morphism, then the lower-order reductions
\big(\phi_{1}^{j},\phi_{2}^{j}\big)\colon r^{j}\Rightarrow{r^{\prime}}^{j}
are also
{\mathcal{VBC}}
-morphisms.

The proof is given in Appendix
A
.

4
Higher (Lie) algebroids

Definitions and fundamental examples.
As was already suggested in the introduction, the higher tangent bundle
\tau^{k}_{M}\colon\mathrm{T}^{k}M\rightarrow M
(cf. the beginning of Section
3
) together with the canonical isomorphism
\kappa^{k}_{M}\colon\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{T}M\rightarrow\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k}M
will be our fundamental example of a higher (Lie) algebroid. According to our considerations from the beginning of the previous section, a choice of local coordinates
(x^{a})
on
M
induces canonical coordinates
(x^{a},\dot{x}^{b})
on
\mathrm{T}M
,
\big(x^{a,(\alpha)}\big)_{\alpha=0,\ldots,k}
on
\mathrm{T}^{k}M
,
\big(x^{a},\dot{x}^{b},x^{a,(1)},\dot{x}^{b,(1)},\ldots,x^{a,(k)},\dot{x}^{b,(k)}\big)
on
\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{T}M
, and
\big(x^{a,(\alpha)},\dot{x}^{b,(\beta)}\big)_{\alpha,\beta=0,\ldots,k}
on
\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k}M
. In these coordinates
\kappa^{k}_{M}
reads as

\displaystyle\kappa^{k}_{M}\colon\ \big(x^{a},\dot{x}^{b},x^{a,(1)},\dot{x}^{b,(1)},\ldots,x^{a,(k)},\dot{x}^{b,(k)}\big)\longmapsto\big(x^{a},x^{a,(1)},\ldots,x^{a,(k)},\dot{x}^{b},\dot{x}^{b,(1)},\ldots,\dot{x}^{b,(k)}\big).

Clearly, for
k=1
we get
\kappa^{1}_{M}=\kappa_{M}
studied in Example
2.13
. It follows easily from the above coordinate formula that
\kappa^{k}_{M}
is an isomorphism in the category of graded-linear manifolds.

According to our motivating considerations from the introduction, we would now like to generalize the above example by substituting the fibration
\mathrm{T}^{k}M\rightarrow M
with an arbitrary
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold
E^{k}\rightarrow M
of order
k
. Instead of a graded-linear isomorphism
\kappa^{k}_{M}
we shall take a weighted vector bundle comorphism
\kappa^{k}
between
\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{1}
and
\mathrm{T}E^{k}
. Such postulates are motivated by the description of (Lie) algebroids (of order one) in terms of a VBC
\kappa
(cf. Proposition
2.15
and Lemma
2.11
).

Definition 4.1

(general higher algebroid, HA)
.

A
general higher
(
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-order)
algebroid
(
HA
, in short) is a
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold
\sigma^{k}\colon E^{k}\rightarrow M
of order
k
together with a weighted VBC
\kappa^{k}\subset\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{1}\times\mathrm{T}E^{k}
from
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma^{1}
to
\tau_{E^{k}}
(covering a (graded) mapping
\rho^{k}\colon E^{k}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}M
) such that the relation
\kappa^{1}\colon\mathrm{T}\sigma^{1}\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\mathrm{T}\tau_{E^{1}}
being the reduction to order one of
\kappa^{k}
equips
\sigma^{1}\colon E^{1}\rightarrow M
with a general algebroid structure.

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 15.29341pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-15.29341pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-16.49501pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.24501pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma^{1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 33.64522pt\raise 6.24501pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.24501pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\kappa^{k}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 67.04341pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 4.5pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-4.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}}}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 39.29341pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 69.29341pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{T}E^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 82.34377pt\raise-16.49501pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.25418pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\tau_{E^{k}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 82.34377pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-14.46703pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}M}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 39.29341pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 71.51564pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces E^{k}.}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 33.91162pt\raise-26.06445pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.56447pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\rho^{k}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.46703pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{split}

(4.1)

In addition:

(i)

If
\big(\sigma^{1},\kappa^{1}\big)
is skew, we call
\big(\sigma^{k},\kappa^{k}\big)
a
skew
HA.

(ii)

If
\big(\sigma^{k},\kappa^{k}\big)
is skew and
\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}\rho^{1},\mathrm{T}\rho^{k}\big)\colon\kappa^{k}\Rightarrow\kappa^{k}_{M}
is a morphism in the category of VBCs, where
\rho_{1}\colon E^{1}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M
is the order-1 reduction of
\rho^{k}
, then we call
\big(\sigma^{k},\kappa^{k}\big)
an
almost-Lie
(
AL
)
HA.

(iii)

A skew higher algebroid
\big(\sigma^{k},\kappa^{k}\big)
in which
\kappa^{k}
is a subalgebroid of the product of the algebroids
\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma^{1},\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\kappa^{1}\big)
(the
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-tangent lift of
\big(\sigma^{1},\kappa^{1}\big)
– see Section
4.1
– and the tangent algebroid
(\tau_{E^{k}},\kappa_{E^{k}})
), is called a
Lie
HA. In fact a Lie HA must be an almost-Lie HA – see Proposition
4.9
. In the language of
[
7
]
,

\big(\sigma^{k},\kappa^{k}\big)
is Lie if
\kappa^{k}
is a Lie algebroid comorphism.

(iv)

A general HA in which
\kappa^{k}
induces an isomorphism on the core bundles
\mathbf{C}\big(\kappa^{k}\big)\colon\mathbf{C}\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}E\big)\rightarrow\mathbf{C}\big(\mathrm{T}E^{k}\big)
is called a
strong
HA.

Analogously to our convention from Section
2
we will colectively refer to the above-defined objects as to
(
Lie
)
higher algebroids
.

We define a
morphism
between (Lie) higher algebroids
\big(\sigma^{k}_{E}\colon E^{k}\rightarrow M,\kappa^{k,E}\big)
and
\big(\sigma^{k}_{F}\colon F^{k}\rightarrow N,\kappa^{k,F}\big)
to be a
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold morphism
\phi^{k}\colon E^{k}\rightarrow F^{k}
such that
\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}\phi^{1},\mathrm{T}\phi^{k}\big)\colon\kappa^{k,E}\Rightarrow\kappa^{k,F}
is a
{\mathcal{VBC}}
-morphism. Obviously, general higher algebroids form a
category
which we denote by
\mathcal{HA}
, with Lie HA, AL HA, and skew HA being its full subcategories.

Let us remark, that the symmetric role of the left and the right anchor occurring in the first order case is no longer present for (Lie) higher algebroids. The defining relation
\kappa^{k}
covers a (graded) morphism
\rho^{k}\colon E^{k}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}M
, and its reduction to order 0 induces a relation (in fact a linear map)
\kappa^{0}\colon E^{1}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M
. For
k=1
these were the left and the right anchors, respectively. As we see, these maps are of quite a different nature. Note, however, that for skew higher algebroids
\kappa^{0}=\rho^{1}
.

Using Proposition
3.8
we easily see that reduction of a (Lie) HA
\big(E^{k},\kappa^{k}\big)
to a lower order
j=1,2,\ldots,k
gives an induced (Lie) higher algebroid structure on
E^{j}
which is skew (resp. AL, Lie, strong) if
\big(E^{k},\kappa^{k}\big)
was so. Moreover, a (Lie) HA morphism
\phi^{k}\colon\big(E^{k},\kappa^{k,E}\big)\rightarrow\big(F^{k},\kappa^{k,F}\big)
induces a (Lie) HA morphisms
\phi^{j}\colon\big(E^{j},\kappa^{j,E}\big)\rightarrow\big(F^{j},\kappa^{j,F}\big)
.

Examples of (Lie) higher algebroids will be discussed later in Sections
4.2
and
6
. For now let us take a closer look at general higher algebroids of order 2.

Remark 4.2

(a local form of a general higher algebroid of order 2)
.

Let
(x^{a},y^{i},z^{\mu})
be graded coordinates on an order-
2

\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold
E^{2}\rightarrow M
.
We shall find a general form of an algebroid
\big(E^{2},\kappa^{2}\big)
of order
2
. As in Remark
2.8
we underline coordinates in
\mathrm{T}E^{2}
when writing equations for
\kappa^{2}\subset\mathrm{T}^{2}E^{1}\times\mathrm{T}E^{2}
. Taking weights into account

weight

(0,0)

(0,1)

(0,2)

(1,0)

(1,1)

(1,2)

\mathrm{T}^{2}E^{1}

x^{a}

\dot{x}^{a}

\ddot{x}^{a}

y^{i}

\dot{y}^{i}

\ddot{y}^{i}

\mathrm{T}E^{2}

x^{a}

\underline{y}^{i}

\underline{z}^{\mu}

\dot{\underline{x}}^{a}

\dot{\underline{y}}^{i}

\dot{\underline{z}}^{\mu}

we get

\displaystyle\kappa^{2}\colon\ \begin{cases}\dot{x}^{a}=Q^{a}_{i}\underline{y}^{i},\\
\ddot{x}^{a}=\frac{1}{2}Q^{a}_{ij}\,\underline{y}^{i}\underline{y}^{j}+Q^{a}_{\mu}\,\underline{z}^{\mu},\qquad\text{where}\quad Q^{a}_{ij}=Q^{a}_{ji},\\
\dot{\underline{x}}^{a}=Q^{{}^{\prime}a}_{i}y^{i},\\
\dot{\underline{y}}^{i}=\dot{y}^{i}+Q^{i}_{jk}\,\underline{y}^{j}y^{k},\\
\dot{\underline{z}}^{\mu}=Q^{\mu}_{i}\ddot{y}^{i}+Q^{\mu}_{ij}\underline{y}^{i}\dot{y}^{j}+Q^{\mu}_{\nu i}\underline{z}^{\nu}y^{i}+\frac{1}{2}Q^{\mu}_{ij,k}\underline{y}^{i}\underline{y}^{j}y^{k},\qquad\text{where}\quad Q^{\mu}_{ij,k}=Q^{\mu}_{ji,k},\end{cases}

(4.5)

for some structure functions
Q^{\cdots}_{\cdots}
of base coordinates
x^{a}
. For a skew algebroid
\big(E^{2},\kappa^{2}\big)
we have
Q^{{}^{\prime}a}_{i}=Q^{a}_{i}
and
Q_{jk}^{i}=-Q_{kj}^{i}
. An algebroid is strong if the matrix
Q^{\mu}_{i}
is invertible. The conditions of being an almost-Lie or a Lie algebroid result in more complicated systems of equations for the structure functions.

Our considerations from Section
2
lead to a notion of a higher subalgebroid.

Definition 4.3

(subalgebroid of a (Lie) HA)
.

Let
\big(\sigma^{k}\colon E^{k}\rightarrow M,\kappa^{k}\big)
be a (Lie) higher algebroid and let
{\sigma^{\prime}}^{k}\colon{E^{\prime}}^{k}\rightarrow M^{\prime}
be a
\mathbb{N}
-graded submanifold of
\sigma^{k}
. We call
{\sigma^{\prime}}^{k}
a
higher subalgebroid
of
\big(\sigma^{k},\kappa^{k}\big)
if the VBC
\kappa^{k}
restricts fine to
\mathrm{T}^{k}{\sigma^{\prime}}^{1}\times\tau_{{E^{\prime}}^{k}}\subset\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma^{1}\times\tau_{E^{k}}
.

4.1
On the Lie axiom

In order to discuss the notion of a Lie higher algebroid, we shall first take a look at higher tangent lifts of a first order (Lie) algebroid structure. We will begin our considerations by studying the lifts of sections of a vector bundle.

Higher lifts of sections of a vector bundle.
Let
\sigma\colon E\rightarrow M
be a vector bundle and
s\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M}(E)
its section. The assignment
\operatorname{Sec}_{M}(E)\ni s\mapsto\mathrm{T}^{k}s\in\operatorname{Sec}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}M}\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}E\big)
is injective, and
\mathbb{R}
-linear, yet it cannot be onto, since
\operatorname{rank}\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma\big)=(k+1)\cdot\operatorname{rank}(\sigma)>\operatorname{rank}(\sigma)
. It is, however, possible to characterize all sections of
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma
in terms of sections of
\sigma
by means of the following procedure.

First, note that the multiplicative action of the reals
\mathbb{R}\times E\rightarrow E
lifts to the multiplicative action
\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathbb{R}\times\mathrm{T}^{k}E\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}E
of
\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathbb{R}
, the latter understood as an
\mathbb{R}
-algebra with addition and multiplication defined as
\mathrm{T}^{k}
-lifts of the standard addition and multiplication in
\mathbb{R}
. In fact, as an algebra
\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathbb{R}
is canonically isomorphic to the truncated polynomial algebra
\mathbb{R}[\varepsilon]/_{\left\langle\varepsilon^{k+1}\right\rangle}
. (This algebra is often denoted
\mathbb{D}_{k}
and called the
algebra of
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-order numbers
.) From the point of view of the theory of natural bundles
[
28
]
,
\mathrm{T}^{k}
is a product-preserving bundle functor associated with the
Weil algebra

\mathbb{D}_{k}
. In conclusion, on
\mathrm{T}^{k}E
we have an operation of multiplication by the elements of
\mathbb{D}_{k}
, which is in fact determined by the action of the generator
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{D}_{k}
. In coordinates
\big(x^{a,(\alpha)},y^{i,(\alpha)}\big)
on
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma
induced from linear coordinates
(x^{a},y^{i})
on
\sigma
it reads as

\displaystyle\varepsilon\cdot\big(x^{a,(\alpha)};y^{i,(0)},y^{i,(1)},\ldots,y^{i,(k)}\big)=\big(x^{a,(\alpha)};0,y^{i,(0)},2y^{i,(1)},\ldots,ky^{i,(k-1)}\big).

This construction leads us to

Definition 4.4

(lifts of a section)
.

Let
s
be a section of
\sigma
and
\alpha=0,1,\ldots,k
an integer. By the
(k-\alpha)
-lift of
s
we understand the section of
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma
defined as

\displaystyle s^{(k-\alpha)}:=\frac{(k-\alpha)!}{k!}\varepsilon^{\alpha}\cdot\mathrm{T}^{k}s.

In particular
s^{(0)}=\frac{1}{k!}\varepsilon^{k}\cdot\mathrm{T}^{k}s
is called the
vertical
and
s^{(k)}=\mathrm{T}^{k}s
the
total lift
of
s
, in agreement with the standard notions for
k=1
.

Using local coordinates it is easy to check that sections of the form
s^{(k-\alpha)}
with
\alpha=0,1,\ldots,k
span locally the full space of sections
\operatorname{Sec}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}M}\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}E\big)
. Recall the notion of an
(\alpha)
-lift of a smooth function introduced at the beginning of Section
3
. For any function
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
the operation of the
(k)
-lift has the following property

\displaystyle(f\cdot s)^{(k)}=\mathrm{T}^{k}(f\cdot s)=\mathrm{T}^{k}f\cdot\mathrm{T}^{k}s

\displaystyle\hphantom{(f\cdot s)^{(k)}}{}=\left(\sum_{\alpha=0}^{k}f^{(\alpha)}\frac{\varepsilon^{\alpha}}{\alpha!}\right)\cdot s^{(k)}=\sum_{\alpha=0}^{k}\frac{f^{(\alpha)}}{\alpha!}\frac{k!}{(k-\alpha)!}s^{(k-\alpha)}=\sum_{\alpha=0}^{k}\binom{k}{\alpha}f^{(\alpha)}s^{(k-\alpha)}.

We recognize the standard formula for the iterated derivative. An analogous formula for
(f\cdot s)^{(k-\alpha)}
can be easily derived from the latter.

Higher tangent lifts of (Lie) algebroids.
It is well-known that if a VB
\sigma\colon E\rightarrow M
carries a (Lie) algebroid structure, then its
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-tangent lift
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma\colon\mathrm{T}^{k}E\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}M
has the so-called lifted (Lie) algebroid structure (e.g.,
[
30
]
). It can be elegantly described in terms of the VBC
\kappa
related with the initial algebroid structure on
\sigma
.

Definition 4.5

(higher tangent lift of a (Lie) algebroid)
.

Let
(\sigma\colon E\!\rightarrow\!M,\rho_{L},\rho_{R}\colon E\!\rightarrow\!\mathrm{T}M,[\cdot,\cdot])

\simeq(\sigma,\kappa\colon\mathrm{T}\sigma\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\tau_{E})
be a (Lie) algebroid. A VBC
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\kappa\colon\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\tau_{\mathrm{T}^{k}E}
over an anchor map
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\rho_{L}\colon\mathrm{T}^{k}E\rightarrow\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k}M
obtained, up to natural identifications, by applying functor
\mathrm{T}^{k}
to
\kappa
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 90.15628pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 178.89589pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 249.55736pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 32.8796pt\raise-26.24223pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.74223pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\simeq\kappa^{k}_{M}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 18.07814pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 132.23442pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 165.84554pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 121.77272pt\raise-26.06445pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.56447pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}\rho_{L}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 108.23442pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 219.55736pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 251.77959pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{k}E,}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 216.4879pt\raise-28.64574pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.34428pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{=}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 191.94624pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{split}

(4.6)

i.e.,
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\kappa=\kappa_{k,E}^{-1}\circ\mathrm{T}^{k}\kappa\circ\kappa_{k,E}
, and
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\rho_{L}:=\mathrm{T}^{k}\rho_{L}\circ\kappa_{k}^{M}
, equips
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma\colon\mathrm{T}^{k}E\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}M
with a (Lie) algebroid structure. We call it the
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-tangent lift of the algebroid
(\sigma,\kappa)
.

We shall now check that
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\kappa
indeed defines a (Lie) algebroid structure and then describe it in the classical terms of the anchors and the bracket operation.

Proposition 4.6

(properties of the lifted (Lie) algebroid)
.

The relation
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\kappa:=\kappa_{k,E}\circ\mathrm{T}^{k}\kappa\circ\kappa_{k,E}^{-1}
defines a
(
Lie
)
algebroid structure on
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma
. Moreover,

(i)

The
(
left and right
)
anchor maps on
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma
are given by
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\rho_{L}:=\kappa^{k}_{M}\circ\mathrm{T}^{k}\rho_{L}
and
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\rho_{R}:=\kappa^{k}_{M}\circ\mathrm{T}^{k}\rho_{R}
, respectively.

(ii)

The algebroid bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}}
on
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma
satisfies

\displaystyle\left[\frac{k!}{(k-\alpha)!}s_{1}^{(k-\alpha)},\frac{k!}{(k-\beta)!}s_{2}^{(k-\beta)}\right]_{\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}}=\frac{k!}{(k-\alpha-\beta)!}([s_{1},s_{2}]_{\sigma})^{(k-\alpha-\beta)},

(4.7)

for any integers
\alpha,\beta=0,1,\ldots,k
such that
\alpha+\beta\leq k
and any sections
s_{1},s_{2}\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M}(E)
, and
\big[s_{1}^{(k-\alpha)},s_{2}^{(k-\beta)}\big]_{\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}}=0
if
\alpha+\beta>k
. These properties fully determine the bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}}
on the space of sections of
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma
.

(iii)

If
(\sigma,\kappa)
is a skew/AL/Lie algebroid, then so is
\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma,\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\kappa\big)
.

The proof is given in Appendix
A
.

Remark 4.7

(characterization of lifts by the canonical pairings)
.

The pairing
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{\sigma}\colon E^{\ast}\times_{M}E\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
lifts to the pairing
\mathrm{T}^{k}\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{\sigma}\colon\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{\ast}\times_{\mathrm{T}^{k}M}\mathrm{T}^{k}E\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
which enables us to identify sections of the vector bundle
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma
with linear functions on
\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{\ast}
. As
\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{\ast}
is, in particular, a
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold of order
k
, the space of sections of
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma
is naturally graded.

Consider a section
s
of
\sigma
. It is easy to see that its
(k-\alpha)
-lift
s^{(k-\alpha)}
(considered as a linear function on
\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{\ast}
) coincides with the
(k-\alpha)
-lift
\tilde{s}^{(k-\alpha)}
of the linear function
\tilde{s}\colon E^{\ast}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
canonically associated with the section
s
. Taking into account the
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold structure of
\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{\ast}
, a section of the form

f^{(\alpha)}s^{(k-\beta)}
, where
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
and
s\in\operatorname{Sec}(E)
, would have weight
\alpha+k-\beta
. However, in a presence of a (Lie) algebroid structure on
\sigma
, it is reasonable to shift this gradation by
-k
, so that the section
f^{(\alpha)}s^{(k-\beta)}
has weight
\alpha-\beta
. Then formula (
4.7
) shows that homogeneous sections of
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma
form a graded Lie algebra concentrated in degrees
-k,-k+1,\ldots
. It has a Lie subalgebra
\operatorname{Sec}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}M,\leq 0}\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}E\big)
consisting of sections of non-positive weights which is of finite rank (over
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
).

Algebroid lifts of sections and the Lie axiom.
By Remark
2.2
a VBC between two vector bundles induces a canonical map between the associated spaces of sections of these bundles. On the other hand, in Definition
4.4
we introduced natural lifts of sections of a vector bundle to sections of its higher tangent lift. Given a (Lie) higher algebroid structure, we can combine these two constructions to arrive at

Definition 4.8

(algebroid lift of a section)
.

Let
\big(\sigma^{k}\colon E^{k}\rightarrow M,\kappa^{k}\colon\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma^{1}\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\tau_{E^{k}}\big)
be a (Lie) higher algebroid. Given a section
s\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M}\big(E^{1}\big)
and an integer
\alpha=0,1,\ldots,k
we define the
(k-\alpha)
-algebroid lift of
s
as

\displaystyle s^{[k-\alpha]}:=\widehat{\kappa^{k}}\big(s^{(k-\alpha)}\big)\in\mathfrak{X}\big(E^{k}\big).

The above concept naturally generalizes the notion of a
vertical lift
and the
algebroid lift
(for
(k,\alpha)=(1,0)
and
(k,\alpha)=(1,1)
, respectively) known in the literature
[
19
]
.

In
[
18
]
Grabowski and Urbański observed that the Jacobi identity can be elegantly reformulated in terms of the algebroid lifts. Their results easily generalize to the setting of Lie higher algebroids.

Proposition 4.9

(characterization of the Lie axiom)
.

The map
\widehat{\kappa^{k}}\colon\operatorname{Sec}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}M}\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}E\big)\rightarrow\mathfrak{X}\big(E^{k}\big)
preserve gradation, i.e., for a homogeneous section
s
of
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma
the vector field
\widehat{\kappa^{k}}(s)
has the same weight as
s
. Moreover, a
higher algebroid
\big(\sigma^{k}\colon E^{k}\rightarrow M,\kappa^{k}\colon\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma^{1}\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\tau_{E^{k}}\big)
is Lie if and only if it is almost-Lie and if the associated algebroid lift satisfies

\displaystyle\left[\frac{k!}{(k-\alpha)!}s_{1}^{[k-\alpha]},\frac{k!}{(k-\beta)!}s_{2}^{[k-\beta]}\right]_{E^{k}}=\frac{k!}{(k-\alpha-\beta)!}([s_{1},s_{2}]_{\sigma^{1}})^{[k-\alpha-\beta]}

(4.8)

for any sections
s_{1},s_{2}\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M}\big(E^{1}\big)
and any integers
\alpha,\beta=0,1,\ldots,k
. Above
[\cdot,\cdot]_{E^{k}}
denotes the standard Lie bracket of vector fields on
E^{k}
, and
[\cdot,\cdot]_{\sigma^{1}}
the algebroid bracket on the reduced
(
Lie
)
algebroid
\big(\sigma^{1},\kappa_{1}\big)
.

Equivalently, an AL higher algebroid
\big(E^{k},\kappa^{k}\big)
is Lie if and only if

\displaystyle\widehat{\kappa^{k}}\colon\ \operatorname{Sec}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}M,\leq 0}\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}E\big)\rightarrow\mathfrak{X}_{\leq 0}\big(E^{k}\big)

(4.9)

is a graded Lie algebra morphism, where
\operatorname{Sec}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}M,\leq 0}\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}E\big)

(
resp.
\mathfrak{X}_{\leq 0}\big(E^{k}\big))
are Lie algebras generated by the homogeneous sections of
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma

(
resp. vector fields on
E^{k})
of non-positive weight.

Proof.

Let
s\in\operatorname{Sec}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}M}\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}E\big)
be a homogeneous section, denote
X:=\widehat{\kappa^{k}}(s)
and consider the corresponding functions
\tilde{s}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{\ast}\big)
, and
\tilde{X}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}\big(\mathrm{T}^{\ast}E^{k}\big)
on the dual vector bundles (cf. Remark
4.7
). They are related by
\tilde{X}=\tilde{s}\circ\varepsilon_{k}
, where
\varepsilon_{k}\colon\mathrm{T}^{\ast}E^{k}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{\ast}
is a (weighted) vector bundle morphism dual to
\kappa^{k}
. Since
\kappa^{k}
is bi-homogeneous, so is
\varepsilon_{k}
, hence weights of
\tilde{X}
and
\tilde{s}
are the same, and our first assertion follows.

By definition,
\big(\sigma^{k},\kappa^{k}\big)
is a Lie HA if and only if it is skew and if
\kappa^{k}
is an algebroidal relation between
\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma^{1},\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\kappa^{1}\big)
and
(\tau_{E^{k}},\kappa_{E^{k}})
. Proposition
2.26
provides a useful characterization of algebroidal relations. It follows that
\big(\sigma^{k},\kappa^{k}\big)
is a Lie HA if and only if for any sections
s,s^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sec}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}M}\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{1}\big)
we have (note that both
\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma^{1},\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\kappa^{1}\big)
and
(\tau_{E^{k}},\kappa_{E^{k}})
are skew algebroids and their anchor maps are
\kappa^{k}_{M}\circ\mathrm{T}^{k}\rho^{1}
and
\operatorname{id}_{\mathrm{T}E^{k}}
, respectively):

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}\rho^{k}\big(\widehat{\kappa^{k}}(s)\big)=\kappa^{k}_{M}\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}\rho^{1}(s)\big)=\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\rho(s)

(4.10)

and

\displaystyle\widehat{\kappa^{k}}([s,s^{\prime}]_{\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}})=\big[\widehat{\kappa^{k}}(s),\widehat{\kappa^{k}}(s^{\prime})\big]_{E^{k}}.

(4.11)

The first of these conditions means that for any section
s\in\operatorname{Sec}\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma\big)
the vector fields
\widehat{\kappa^{k}}(s)\in\mathfrak{X}\big(E^{k}\big)
and
\kappa^{k}_{M}\circ\mathrm{T}^{k}\rho^{1}(s)=\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\rho(s)\in\mathfrak{X}\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}M\big)
are
\rho^{k}
-related and it is equivalent to the commutativity of the diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa^{k}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}\rho^{1}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}E^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{T}\rho^{k}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{T}M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq\kappa^{k}_{M}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k}M,}

i.e., the axiom of an AL algebroid.
The second condition for lifts
s=s_{1}^{(k-\alpha)}
and
s^{\prime}=s_{2}^{(k-\beta)}
, with
s_{1},s_{2}\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M}\big(E^{1}\big)
, gives equality (
4.8
).

Assume now that the equation (
4.8
) holds and
\big(E^{k},\kappa^{k}\big)
is an almost-Lie higher algebroid. The latter condition implies (
4.10
), and from (
4.8
) it follows that equation (
4.11
) holds for sections of the form
s=s_{1}^{(k-\alpha)}
and
s^{\prime}=s_{2}^{(k-\beta)}
. To prove that
\big(E^{k},\kappa^{k}\big)
is Lie, we need to show that (
4.11
) holds for any sections
s,s^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sec}\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma\big)
.

Assuming that (
4.11
) holds for given sections
s
and
s^{\prime}
, we shall show that it also holds for sections
s:=\phi s
and
s^{\prime}
for any
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}M\big)
. Indeed, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\widehat{\kappa^{k}}([\phi s,s^{\prime}]_{\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}})\overset{\eqref{eqn:bracket}}{=}\widehat{\kappa^{k}}\big[\phi[s,s^{\prime}]_{\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}}-{\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}}\rho(s^{\prime})(\phi)s)\big]\\
&\hphantom{\widehat{\kappa^{k}}([\phi s,s^{\prime}]_{\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}})}{}\overset{\eqref{e:hat_r_axiom}}{=}\big(\rho^{k}\big)^{\ast}(\phi)\widehat{\kappa^{k}}([s,s^{\prime}]_{\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}})-\big(\rho^{k}\big)^{\ast}({\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}}\rho(s^{\prime})(\phi))\widehat{\kappa^{k}}(s),\end{split}

while

\displaystyle\big[\widehat{\kappa^{k}}(\phi s),\widehat{\kappa^{k}}(s^{\prime})\big]_{E^{k}}\overset{\eqref{e:hat_r_axiom}}{=}\big[\big(\rho^{k}\big)^{\ast}(\phi)\widehat{\kappa^{k}}(s),\widehat{\kappa^{k}}(s^{\prime})\big]_{E^{k}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\big[\widehat{\kappa^k}(\phi s),\widehat{\kappa^k}(s')\big]_{E^k}}{}\overset{\eqref{eqn:bracket}}{=}\big(\rho^{k}\big)^{\ast}(\phi)\big[\widehat{\kappa^{k}}(s),\widehat{\kappa^{k}}(s^{\prime})\big]_{E^{k}}-\widehat{\kappa^{k}}(s^{\prime})\big(\big(\rho^{k}\big)^{\ast}(\phi)\big)\widehat{\kappa^{k}}(s)

\displaystyle\hphantom{\big[\widehat{\kappa^k}(\phi s),\widehat{\kappa^k}(s')\big]_{E^k}}{}=\big(\rho^{k}\big)^{\ast}(\phi)\big[\widehat{\kappa^{k}}(s),\widehat{\kappa^{k}}(s^{\prime})\big]_{E^{k}}-\big(\rho^{k}\big)^{\ast}\big(\widehat{\kappa^{k}}(s^{\prime})(\phi)\big)\widehat{\kappa^{k}}(s)

\displaystyle\hphantom{\big[\widehat{\kappa^k}(\phi s),\widehat{\kappa^k}(s')\big]_{E^k}}{}\overset{\eqref{eqn:kappa_anchor}}{=}\big(\rho^{k}\big)^{\ast}(\phi)\big[\widehat{\kappa^{k}}(s),\widehat{\kappa^{k}}(s^{\prime})\big]_{E^{k}}-\big(\rho^{k}\big)^{\ast}(\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\rho(s^{\prime})(\phi))\widehat{\kappa^{k}}(s).

Comparison of the above equalities proves our claim.
Now, since all
(k-\alpha)
-lifts (with
\alpha\geq 0
) of sections from
\operatorname{Sec}_{M}\big(E^{1}\big)
span the whole space
\operatorname{Sec}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}M}\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{1}\big)
as the
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}M\big)
-module, equation (
4.11
) holds for every sections
s,s^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sec}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}M}\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{1}\big)
. This ends the proof.
∎

Remark 4.10
.

Note that
\operatorname{Sec}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}M,\leq 0}\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}E\big)
and
\mathfrak{X}_{\leq 0}\big(E^{k}\big)
are
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
-modules of finite rank. It follows that the Lie axiom for HA can be reduced to a finite number of equations of the form (
4.8
) with
s_{1}=e_{i}
,
s_{2}=e_{j}
where
(e_{i})
is a local basis of sections of
\sigma_{1}
.

Subalgebroids of Lie higher algebroids.
We shall end our considerations concerning the Lie axiom for HA by showing that it is inherited by subalgebroids. First we will need the following properties of higher tangent lifts.

Proposition 4.11

(properties of the lifted algebroid)
.

Let
k
be a positive integer. Consider vector bundles
\sigma\colon E\rightarrow M
,
\sigma_{1}\colon E_{1}\rightarrow M_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}\colon E_{2}\rightarrow M_{2}
and their subbundles
\sigma^{\prime}\colon E^{\prime}\rightarrow M^{\prime}
,
\sigma^{\prime}_{1}\colon E^{\prime}_{1}\rightarrow M^{\prime}_{1}
and
\sigma^{\prime}_{2}\colon E^{\prime}_{2}\rightarrow M^{\prime}_{2}
, respectively.

(i)

Let

r\colon\sigma_{1}\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\sigma_{2}
be a VBC that restricts fine to a VBC
r^{\prime}\colon\sigma_{1}^{\prime}\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\sigma_{2}^{\prime}
. Then
\mathrm{T}^{k}r\colon\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma_{1}\mbox{$\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright$}

\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma_{2}
is a VBC which restricts fine to a VBC
\mathrm{T}^{k}r^{\prime}\colon\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma_{1}^{\prime}\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma_{2}^{\prime}
.

(ii)

Let
(\sigma,\kappa)
be a
(
Lie
)
algebroid and
(\sigma^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
its subalgebroid. Then the
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-tangent lift algebroid
\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma^{\prime},\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\kappa^{\prime}\big)
is a subalgebroid of
\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma,\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\kappa\big)
.

(iii)

Let
(\sigma_{1},\kappa_{1})
and
(\sigma_{2},\kappa_{2})
be
(
Lie
)
algebroids and let
r\colon\sigma_{1}\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\sigma_{2}
be an algebroidal relation. Then
\mathrm{T}^{k}r\colon\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma_{1}\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma_{2}
is also an algebroidal relation between the
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-tangent lift algebroids
\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma_{1},\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\kappa_{1}\big)
and
\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma_{2},\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\kappa_{2}\big)
.

The proof is given in Appendix
A
.

Proposition 4.12

(the Lie axiom and subalgebroids)
.

A higher subalgebroid of a Lie
(
resp. almost-Lie
)
higher algebroid is Lie
(
resp. almost-Lie
)
.

Proof.

Assume that
\big(\sigma^{k}_{F}\colon F^{k}\rightarrow N,\kappa^{k,F}\big)
is a higher subalgebroid of an AL higher algebroid
\big(\sigma^{k}_{E}\colon E^{k}\rightarrow M,\kappa^{k,E}\big)
. To prove that
\big(\sigma^{k}_{F},\kappa^{k,F}\big)
is also AL we must verify that if two elements
X\in\mathrm{T}^{k}F^{1}
and
Y\in\mathrm{T}F^{k}
are
\kappa^{k,F}
-related then their images
\mathrm{T}^{k}\rho^{1}_{F}(X)
and
\mathrm{T}\rho^{k}_{F}(Y)
are
\kappa^{k}_{N}
-related. Now as
\kappa^{k,F}
is a fine restriction of
\kappa^{k,E}
, elements
X
and
Y
as above are
\kappa^{k,E}
-related, and thus, since
\big(\sigma^{k}_{E}\colon E^{k}\rightarrow M,\kappa^{k,E}\big)
was almost-Lie, elements
\mathrm{T}^{k}\rho^{1}_{E}(X)
and
\mathrm{T}\rho^{k}_{E}(Y)
are
\kappa^{k}_{M}
-related. The assertion follows from a simple observation that the anchor maps
\rho^{k}_{F}\colon F^{k}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}N
and
\rho^{1}\colon F^{1}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M
are the restrictions
\rho^{k}_{F}=\rho^{k}_{E}|_{F^{k}}
and
\rho^{1}_{F}=\rho^{1}_{E}|_{F^{1}}
, respectively.

Now assume that
\big(\sigma^{k}_{E},\kappa^{k,E}\big)
is Lie. This means that
\kappa^{k,E}
is an algebroidal relation between
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma^{1}_{E}
and
\tau_{E^{k}}
. Moreover, by definition,
\kappa^{k,F}
is a fine restriction of
\kappa^{k,E}
. In particular,
\big(\sigma^{1}_{E},\kappa^{1,F}\big)
is a subalgebroid of
\big(\sigma^{1}_{E},\kappa^{1,E}\big)
, hence by Proposition
4.11
(
ii
),
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma^{1}_{F}
is a subalgebroid of
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma^{1}_{E}
. Note also that
\tau_{F^{k}}
is a subalgebroid of
\tau_{E^{k}}
. Thus, by Proposition
4.11
(
iii
),
\kappa^{k,F}
is also an algebroidal relation, i.e.,
\big(\sigma^{k}_{F}\colon F^{k}\rightarrow N,\kappa^{k,F}\big)
is a Lie higher algebroid.
∎

4.2
Prolongations of an AL algebroid

In our previous publication
[
27
]
we studied a class of objects crafted to play the role of prototypes of (Lie) higher algebroids. Our construction was motivated by the procedure of reduction of a higher tangent bundle of a Lie groupoid. In fact, as we shall see shortly, these objects provide natural examples of (Lie) higher algebroids in the sense of Definition
4.1
. Let us now recall their construction.

Construction of the prolongations.
Let
(\sigma\colon E\rightarrow M,\kappa)
be an AL algebroid. For each
k=1,2,\ldots
, we will construct bundles
\sigma^{[k]}\colon E^{[k]}\rightarrow M
and relations
\kappa^{[k]}\subset\mathrm{T}^{k}E\times\mathrm{T}E^{[k]}
. We shall call the pair
\big(\sigma^{[k]},\kappa^{[k]}\big)
the
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-prolongation of the algebroid
(\sigma,\kappa)
. The total spaces of bundles
\sigma^{[k]}
are defined inductively:

\displaystyle E^{[1]}:=E,\qquad E^{[2]}:=\{A\in\mathrm{T}E\colon\mathrm{T}\sigma(A)=\rho\circ\tau_{E}(A)\},

\displaystyle E^{[k+1]}:=\mathrm{T}E^{[k]}\cap\mathrm{T}^{k}E,\qquad\text{for}\quad k\geq 2,

where
\rho\colon E\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M
is the anchor map of
(\sigma,\kappa)
. By the inductive hypothesis,
E^{[k]}
is considered, as a subset of
\mathrm{T}^{k-1}E
, hence both
\mathrm{T}E^{[k]}
and
\mathrm{T}^{k}E
can be understood here as subsets of
\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}E
, and
\sigma^{[k]}
is simply the projection
E^{[k]}\subset\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}E\rightarrow E\overset{\sigma}{\rightarrow}M
. (Recall that for any manifold
M
,
\mathrm{T}^{k}M
can be consider a subset of
\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}M
in a natural way.)

The bundle
\sigma^{[k]}\colon E^{[k]}\rightarrow M
has a very interesting algebraic structure encoded in a relation
\kappa^{[k]}
which can be defined directly as

\kappa^{[1]}=\kappa
,
\kappa^{[k]}=\big(\kappa^{k-1}_{E}\circ\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\kappa\big)\cap\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}E\times\mathrm{T}E^{[k]}\big)
(see
[
27
, Proposition 4.6]
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 20.00175pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-20.00175pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 33.73894pt\raise 6.24501pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.24501pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\kappa}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 71.75175pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 4.5pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-4.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise
