3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 168.00526pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 138.00526pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 168.00526pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}E}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-13.05035pt\raise-33.18446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.19444pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-5.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 84.46294pt\raise-26.80055pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.38391pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\kappa^{[k]}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 169.05388pt\raise-33.18446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 4.5pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-4.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 44.00175pt\raise-33.18446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 91.00351pt\raise-33.18446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 138.00526pt\raise-33.18446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 171.30388pt\raise-33.18446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{T}E^{[k]}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 188.00702pt\raise-23.80554pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 188.00702pt\raise-5.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{split}

(4.12)

Since
\kappa
is a VBC, it is easy to see that so is
\kappa^{k-1}_{E}\circ\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\kappa
. We stress that the fact that the latter restricts fine to a VBC from
\mathrm{T}^{k}E
to
\mathrm{T}E^{[k]}
is a non-trivial result which strongly depends on the fact that
\sigma
has an AL algebroid structure.

In particular, if we start from the tangent algebroid
(\sigma,\kappa)=(\tau_{M},\kappa_{M})
, then
\sigma^{[k]}
is just the higher tangent bundle
\tau^{k}_{M}\colon\mathrm{T}^{k}M\rightarrow M
and
\kappa^{[k]}
is the canonical isomorphism
\kappa^{k}_{M}
. In general,
\sigma^{[k]}\colon E^{[k]}\rightarrow M
is a
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold of rank
(r,r,\ldots,r)
where
r
is the rank of
E
(see
[
27
, Theorem 4.5]
). If
(\sigma,\kappa)
is the Lie algebroid of a Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}
(i.e., it is a reduction of the tangent algebroid
\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}
by the action of
\mathcal{G}
), then its
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-prolongation
\mathcal{A}^{k}(\mathcal{G})
can be interpreted as a reduction of the higher tangent bundle
\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathcal{G}
by the action of
\mathcal{G}
. More precisely,
\mathcal{A}^{k}(\mathcal{G})=\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}\big|_{M}
, where
M
is the base of
\mathcal{G}
and
\alpha\colon\mathcal{G}\rightarrow M
denotes the source map
[
27
]
. That is, bundle
\mathcal{A}^{k}(\mathcal{G})
consists of
k
-jets of curves in
\mathcal{G}
which are tangent to the
\alpha
-fibres at the base.

To our best knowledge the
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-order prolongations of Lie algebroids were for the first time studied by Saunders
[
43
]
, who introduced bundles
\mathcal{A}^{k}(\mathcal{G})
discussed above. He also proposed an abstract construction of the second prolongation of a Lie algebroid which is not necessarily integrable. Later Colombo and De Diego
[
9
]
generalized his ideas defining bundle
E^{[k+1]}
(under the name of
higher order Lie algebroid
) as the space of
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-tangent lifts of admissible curves in a Lie algebroid
E
. Their construction was later adopted by Martinez
[
39
]
to develop higher-order variational calculus on Lie algebroids. Later in Remark
5.6
we briefly discuss his and other applications of the notion of a prolongation in variational calculus.

Let us remark that in all the above-cited sources prolongations are constructed out of Lie algebroids, whereas our results from
[
27
]
recognize the almost-Lie class as fundamental to define a prolongation. Moreover, in all these sources prolongations are understood only as bundles
\sigma^{[k]}\colon E^{[k]}\rightarrow M
, and the prominent role of the VBC
\kappa^{[k]}
, being the generalization of the algebroid bracket operation, remains unrecognized.

Properties of the prolongations.
Prolongations of AL algebroids provide an example of strong AL higher algebroids. Moreover, prolongations of Lie algebroids are Lie higher algebroids.

Proposition 4.13

(properties of the prolongations)
.

Let
(\sigma\colon E\rightarrow M,\kappa)
be a Lie
(
resp. almost-Lie
)
algebroid. Then
\big(\sigma^{[k]}\colon E^{[k]}\rightarrow M,\kappa^{[k]}\big)
is an example of a strong Lie
(
resp. almost-Lie
)
higher algebroid.

The proof is given in Appendix
A
.

Example 4.14

(a local form of a second order prolongation)
.

Let
\sigma\colon E\rightarrow M
be a vector bundle with local coordinates
(x^{a},y^{i})
and consider an AL algebroid
(\sigma,\kappa)
given locally by structure function
Q^{b}_{i}(x)=\widetilde{Q}^{b}_{i}(x)
and
Q^{i}_{jk}(x)
(cf. Remark
2.8
). On
\mathrm{T}^{2}E
consider induced coordinates
(x^{a},y^{i},\dot{x}^{b},\dot{y}^{j},\ddot{x}^{c},\ddot{y}^{k})
, and coordinates
\big(x^{a},\underline{y}^{i},\underline{y}^{j,(1)},\dot{\underline{x}}^{b},\dot{\underline{y}}^{k},\dot{\underline{y}}^{l,(1)}\big)
on
\mathrm{T}E^{[2]}
. The inductive formula (
4.12
) allows to calculate the expression for
\kappa^{[2]}\colon\mathrm{T}^{2}E\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\mathrm{T}E^{[2]}
(recall a general local form of a second-order algebroid given in Remark
4.2
) which reads as

\displaystyle\kappa^{[2]}\colon\begin{cases}\dot{x}^{a}=Q^{a}_{i}(x)\underline{y}^{i},\\
\ddot{x}^{a}=\dfrac{\partial Q^{a}_{i}}{\partial x^{b}}(x)Q^{b}_{j}(x)\underline{y}^{i}\underline{y}^{j}+Q^{a}_{i}(x)\underline{y}^{i,(1)},\\
\dot{\underline{x}}^{a}=Q^{a}_{i}(x)y^{i},\\
\dot{\underline{y}}^{i}=\dot{y}^{i}+Q^{i}_{jk}(x)\underline{y}^{j}y^{k},\\
\dot{\underline{y}}^{i,(1)}=\ddot{y}^{i}+Q^{i}_{jk}(x)\underline{y}^{j}\dot{y}^{k}+Q^{i}_{jk}(x)\dot{\underline{y}}^{j}{y}^{k}+\dfrac{\partial Q^{i}_{jk}}{\partial x^{a}}(x)Q^{a}_{l}(x)\underline{y}^{l}\underline{y}^{j}{y}^{k}.\end{cases}

(4.13)

Observe that the equation for
\ddot{x}^{i}
can be obtained from the formula
\dot{x}^{a}=Q^{a}_{i}(x)\underline{y}^{i}
by a formal derivation, assuming that
(\underline{y}^{i})^{\dot{}}=\underline{y}^{i,(1)}
. In a similar manner, formula
\dot{\underline{y}}^{i,(1)}
can be derived from the equation
\dot{\underline{y}}^{i}=\dot{y}^{i}+Q^{i}_{jk}(x)\underline{y}^{j}y^{k}
. Thus it is instructive to think about
\kappa^{[2]}
as of first-order differential consequences of
\kappa=\kappa^{[1]}
. We will use this property later in Section
5.2
.

5
Variational calculus on (Lie) higher algebroids

In this section we shall discuss a geometric formalism of the
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-order variational calculus based on the notion of a (Lie) higher algebroid
\big(\sigma^{k}\colon E^{k}\rightarrow M,\kappa^{k}\big)
. Actually the geometry here is exactly the same as in our previous study on prolongations of AL algebroids
[
25
]
. The idea is that the relation
\kappa^{k}
is responsible for the construction of admissible variations of potential extremals of the system (the role of
\kappa
is to change a ‘jet of curves’ into a ‘curve of jets’ – cf. our discussion in Section
1
). By dualizing
\kappa^{k}
we may represent the variation of an action functional as a pairing of a certain curve of covectors with the
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-tangent lift of the curve of virtual displacements. Then all that is left to do is to perform an integration by parts according to the recipe from
[
26
]
.

Actually, it is straightforward to observe that the geometric formalism described above works well for even more general structures which we shall call pre-algebroids. We believe that they may have some potential applications in the theory of reductions. In fact, in the last paragraph of Section
5.1
we provide a rather trivial example of that sort. From the point of view of complexity it is not interesting, yet still it is an example of a non-standard reduction – cf. Remark
5.2
. On the other hand, more complex examples including the derivation of higher-order Euler–Poincaré and Hummel equations within our formalism can be found in
[
25
]
.

5.1
The formalism of variational calculus

Pre-algebroids.

Definition 5.1

(pre-algebroid)
.

By a
pre-algebroid of order
k
we understand a triple
\big(\sigma^{k},\tau,\kappa^{k}\big)
consisting of a
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold
\sigma^{k}\colon E^{k}\rightarrow M
of order
k
, a vector bundle
\tau\colon F\rightarrow M
(over the same base), and a weighted VBC
\kappa^{k}\colon\mathrm{T}^{k}\tau\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\tau_{E^{k}}
inducing the identity on
M
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 14.46703pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-12.98093pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-16.49501pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.24501pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}\tau}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 33.23203pt\raise 6.24501pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.24501pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\kappa^{k}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 66.21703pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 4.5pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-4.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}}}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 38.46703pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 68.46703pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{T}E^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 81.51738pt\raise-16.49501pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.25418pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\tau_{E^{k}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 81.51738pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-14.46703pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}M}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 38.46703pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 70.68925pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces E^{k}.}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 33.49843pt\raise-26.06445pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.56447pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\rho^{k}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.46703pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{split}

(5.1)

The reduction of the relation
\kappa^{k}
to order zero defines a vector bundle morphism
\rho^{F}=\kappa^{0}\colon F\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M
. We call a pre-algebroid
\big(\sigma^{k},\tau,\kappa^{k}\big)

almost-Lie
if
\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}\rho^{F},\mathrm{T}\rho^{k}\big)\colon\kappa^{k}\Rightarrow\kappa^{k}_{M}
is a
{\mathcal{VBC}}
-morphism.

The difference with Definition
4.1
is that we allow the weighted VBC
\kappa^{k}
to match the tangent bundle
\tau_{E^{k}}\colon\mathrm{T}E^{k}\rightarrow E^{k}
with the lifted bundle
\mathrm{T}^{k}\tau\colon\mathrm{T}^{k}F\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}M
of an arbitrary VB
\tau\colon F\rightarrow M
, a priori not related with
\sigma^{k}
. By taking
\tau=\sigma^{1}
and assuming that
\kappa^{1}
gives the identity on the core bundles we recover the definition of a general HA. Pre-algebroids of order one have an elegant description in terms of a certain bracket operation. We discuss it briefly in the concluding Section
7
.

It is easy to see that the construction of the algebroid lift can be straightforwardly extended to pre-algebroids. This time to a section
s\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M}(F)
of
\tau
and a number
\alpha=0,1,\ldots,k
we assign a vector field
s^{[k-\alpha]}:=\widehat{\kappa^{k}}\big(s^{(k-\alpha)}\big)\in\mathfrak{X}\big(E^{k}\big)
.

Remark 5.2
.

We believe that examples of pre-algebroids may appear in the theory of reductions. Let
G
be a Lie group (or more generally a Lie groupoid). In the standard reduction of a
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-order variational problem on
G
one divides the higher tangent bundle
\mathrm{T}^{k}G
by the natural action of
G
. In fact, here
G
acts on curves in
G
by, say, left multiplication and the action on
\mathrm{T}^{k}G
is the differential consequence of this action on
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-jets. However, one can consider more general actions by a subgroup
H<\mathrm{T}^{k}G
which properly contains
G
, and thus which would not be induced by the action of
G
on curves. In proper circumstances, the quotient
\mathrm{T}^{k}G/H
will have a structure of a
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold (space)
E^{k}
. However, in general the knowledge of
E^{1}
may not be enough to define admissible variations, if for example
E^{1}
is trivial but the entire
E^{k}
is not. Thus a reduction of
\kappa_{G}^{k}
(if it can be properly defined) may lead to a natural example of a pre-algebroid.

A very simple example in this direction is provided in the last paragraph of this subsection.

Admissible paths and admissible variations.
Consider now a pre-algebroid
\big(\sigma^{k},\tau,\kappa^{k}\big)
. Let
\gamma\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow E^{k}
be a curve over
\underline{\gamma}:=\sigma^{k}(\gamma)\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow M
. Choose another curve
a\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow F
and consider its
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-order tangent lift
\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k}a\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}F
. Along
\gamma
we would like to construct a vector field
\delta_{a}\gamma
by the formula

\displaystyle\delta_{a}\gamma(t):=\big(\kappa^{k}\big)_{\gamma(t)}\big(\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k}a(t)\big)\in\mathrm{T}_{\gamma(t)}E^{k}.

(5.2)

Note that, by the properties of
\kappa^{k}
, this definition is correct if and only if
\gamma
and
a
share the same base path, i.e.,
\sigma^{k}(\gamma)=\underline{\gamma}=\tau(a)
and if
\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k}\underline{\gamma}=\rho^{k}(\gamma)
. The latter equation is known as the (left)
admissibility
of
\gamma
. If these conditions hold, we call
\delta_{a}\gamma
an
admissible variation
along
\gamma
and
a
its
generator
or a
virtual displacement
. The set of all admissible curves on the considered pre-algebroid will be denoted by
\operatorname{Adm}\big(E^{k}\big)
, while the set of all admissible variations by
\mathcal{VA}\big(E^{k}\big)
.

Let us explain a relation between the notions of admissible variations and
variations
as understood in the standard variational calculus. In a general setting, a variation
\delta\gamma
of an
E^{k}
-valued path
\gamma
is a vector field along
\gamma
, i.e.,
\delta\gamma\colon t\mapsto\mathrm{T}_{\gamma(t)}E^{k}
. Hence a variation
\delta\gamma
can be represented by a family of paths
\gamma_{s}\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow E^{k}
, where
\gamma_{0}=\gamma
and
\delta\gamma(t)=\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{1}(s\mapsto\gamma_{s}(t))
. In a very general, but intuitive sense, a variation
\delta\gamma
is tangent to a subset
\mathcal{A}
of paths in
E^{k}
if it can be represented by a family
\gamma_{s}
lying in
\mathcal{A}
. Results of
[
38
]
and
[
13
, Theorem 3]
show that for an algebroid of order one the subspace
\operatorname{Adm}\big(E^{1}\big)

of admissible curves is a Banach submanifold in the space of all
C^{1}
-paths in
E^{1}
. Moreover, the condition
\mathrm{T}\operatorname{Adm}\big(E^{1}\big)=\mathcal{VA}\big(E^{1}\big)
is equivalent to the AL axiom, i.e., for AL algebroids admissible variations are precisely variations tangent to the space of admissible curves.

In the higher-order case we can prove the following two Lemmas in this direction. Actually, the presented reasonings simplify the proofs available in the literature for the order-one case.

Lemma 5.3

(tangent space for admissible variations)
.

Let
\delta\gamma\in\mathrm{T}_{\gamma}E^{k}
be a vector field along an admissible path
\gamma
in a pre-algebroid
\big(\sigma^{k}\colon E^{k}\rightarrow M,\tau\colon F\rightarrow M,\kappa^{k}\big)
. If
\delta\gamma
is tangent to
\operatorname{Adm}\big(E^{k}\big)
then

\displaystyle\big(\mathrm{T}\rho^{k}\big)(\delta\gamma)=\kappa^{k}_{M}\big(\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k}\underline{\delta\gamma}\big),

(5.3)

where
\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k}\underline{\delta\gamma}
denotes the
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-tangent lift of the curve
\underline{\delta\gamma}=\mathrm{T}\sigma^{k}(\delta\gamma)
.

Proof.

Assume that
\delta\gamma
is a variation of an admissible path
\gamma\in\operatorname{Adm}\big(E^{k}\big)
tangent to
\operatorname{Adm}\big(E^{k}\big)
, i.e.,
\delta\gamma(t)=\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{1}[s\mapsto\gamma(t,s)]
and paths
t\mapsto\gamma(t,s)
are admissible for each
s
. Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}\rho^{k}(\delta\gamma)(t)=\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{1}\big[s\mapsto\rho^{k}(\gamma(t,s))\big]=\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{1}[s\mapsto\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k}[t\mapsto\underline{\gamma}(t,s)]]\in\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k}M,

while
\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k}\underline{\delta\gamma}=\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k}\big[t\mapsto\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{1}[s\mapsto\underline{\gamma}(t,s)]\big]
. Due to the definition of
\kappa^{k}_{M}
, formula (
5.3
) follows.
∎

Remark 5.4

(problems with the Banach-manifold structure)
.

According to
[
38
]
, for
k=1
the converse of above lemma is also true, i.e., equation (
5.3
) describes the space
\mathrm{T}\operatorname{Adm}\big(E^{1}\big)
. For
k>1
some additional conditions are necessary to assure that the subspace of admissible curves in
E^{k}
is a Banach submanifold. Such conditions may follow directly from the geometry of the considered problem. For example in our preprint
[
25
]
additional assumptions imposed on admissible paths were motivated by the reduction procedure.

Lemma 5.5

(a property of AL pre-algebroids)
.

If a pre-algebroid
\big(\sigma^{k}\colon E^{k}\rightarrow M,\tau\colon F\rightarrow M,\kappa^{k}\big)
is almost-Lie,
\gamma
is an admissible path, and
a
is a curve in
F
such that
\tau(a)=\underline{\gamma}
, then the admissible variation
\delta_{a}\gamma
satisfies equation (
5.3
).

Proof.

Consider a diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}\rho^{F}}
\scriptstyle{\kappa^{k}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}E^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{T}\rho^{k}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{T}M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa^{k}_{M}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k}M,}

which is commutative as our pre-algebroid
\big(\sigma^{k},\tau,\kappa^{k}\big)
is almost-Lie. As
\big(\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k}a,\delta_{a}\gamma\big)\in\kappa^{k}
, we get
\mathrm{T}\rho^{k}(\delta_{a}\gamma)=\kappa^{k}_{M}\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}\rho^{F}\big(\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k}a\big)\big)=\kappa^{k}_{M}\big(\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k}\rho^{F}(a)\big)=\kappa^{k}_{M}\big(\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k}\underline{\delta_{a}\gamma}\big)
. The last equality follows from the commutativity of the diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau^{k}_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\kappa^{k}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}E^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{T}\sigma^{k}}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{F}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}M,}

from which we get
\rho^{F}(a)=\underline{\delta_{a}\gamma}
as
\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k}a\sim_{\kappa^{k}}\delta_{a}\gamma
.
∎

Variational calculus.
We understand Lagrangian mechanics on a
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-order pre-algebroid
\big(\sigma^{k},\tau,\kappa^{k}\big)
as the study how a functional, obtained by integrating the Lagrangian function
L\colon E^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
along an admissible curve
\gamma\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow E^{k}
, behaves under movement in the direction of admissible variations. By construction

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathrm{d}L(\gamma),\delta_{a}\gamma\right\rangle_{\tau_{E^{k}}}=\big\langle\mathrm{d}L(\gamma),\big(\kappa^{k}\big)_{\gamma}\big(\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k}a\big)\big\rangle_{\tau_{E^{k}}}=\big\langle{\big(\kappa^{k}\big)}^{\ast}(\mathrm{d}L(\gamma)),\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k}a\big\rangle_{\mathrm{T}^{k}\tau},

(5.4)

where
{\big(\kappa^{k}\big)}^{\ast}\colon\mathrm{T}^{\ast}E^{k}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}F^{\ast}
is a morphism of graded-linear bundles dual to
\kappa^{k}
. We have thus arrived at the pairing of a
\mathrm{T}^{k}F^{\ast}
-valued curve with the
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-tangent lift
\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k}a\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}F
of the generator
a
of
\delta_{a}\gamma
. Using our earlier results on the
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-order geometric integration by parts – see
[
26
]
– we may present this pairing as a sum of a complete derivative, and a paring
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{\tau}\colon F^{\ast}\times F\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
evaluated on the generator
a

\displaystyle\big\langle{\big(\kappa^{k}\big)}^{\ast}(\mathrm{d}L(\gamma)),\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k}a\big\rangle_{\mathrm{T}^{k}\tau}=\big\langle\mathcal{EL}_{k}\big(\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k}\gamma\big),a\big\rangle_{\tau}+\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{\big\langle\mathcal{P}_{k}\big(\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k-1}\gamma\big),\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k-1}a\big\rangle_{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\tau}}.

(5.5)

Here
\mathcal{EL}_{k}(\cdot)
denotes the Euler–Lagrange operator and
\mathcal{P}_{k}(\cdot)
the momentum operator associated with the Lagrangian (see
[
26
]
for the precise definitions). An admissible curve
\gamma
satisfies
Euler–Lagrange equations

\mathcal{EL}_{k}\big(\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k}\gamma\big)=0
(i.e., is a
trajectory
of the considered Lagrangian system) if and only if for every virtual displacement
a
we have

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathrm{d}L(\gamma),\delta_{a}\gamma\right\rangle_{\tau_{E^{k}}}=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{\big\langle\mathcal{P}_{k}\big(\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k-1}\gamma\big),\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k-1}a\big\rangle_{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\tau}},

(5.6)

or in a more familiar integral version

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\left\langle\mathrm{d}L(\gamma),\delta_{a}\gamma\right\rangle_{\tau_{E^{k}}}\mathrm{d}t=\big\langle\mathcal{P}_{k}\big(\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k-1}\gamma\big),\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k-1}a\big\rangle_{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\tau}\big|^{T}_{0}.

The presented formalism generalizes the construction of geometric Lagrangian mechanics on a general algebroid from
[
13
]
.

Remark 5.6
.

In the literature there have been a few attempts to develop higher-order mechanics on generalizations of Lie algebroids. With the sole exception of
[
4
]
, all trials known to us used the structure of a prolongation of a Lie algebroid
\big(E^{[k]},\kappa^{[k]}\big)
(see Section
4.2
), naturally appearing in reduction of higher tangent bundles of Lie groupoids.

As we already pointed out when discussing the notion of an AL algebroid prolongation for the first time, the role of the VBC
\kappa^{[k]}
in the structure of an algebroid prolongation remained unrecognised in all the primary sources
[
9
,
39
,
43
]
. On the other hand, in the theory presented above,
\kappa^{[k]}
is responsible for the construction of admissible variations. Martìnez in
[
39
]
constructed the
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-algebroid lift
s^{[k]}
of a section
s\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M}(E)
directly form the first-order data
(E,\kappa)
(which should not be surprising as the prolongation is completely determined by its base object). Then he defined an admissible variation generated by
s
as a restriction of
s^{[k]}
to an admissible trajectory
\gamma
. Apparently, his approach is equivalent to ours as
s^{[k]}|_{\gamma}=\delta_{a}\gamma
for
a=s|_{\underline{\gamma}}
(see the discussion of symmetries and conservation laws below). Summing up, on the prolongation of a Lie algebroid the geometry of Martìnez’s formalism is the same as ours, despite the presence of
\kappa^{[k]}
not being directly observed.

The case of
k=2
prolongations was intensively studied by Colombo and his collaborators (see
[
1
,
8
]
and the references therein). In his formalism the second prolongation
E^{[2]}
is treated as a subset in the prolongation of
E
along the projection
\tau\colon E\rightarrow M
(see Example
2.30
), i.e.,
E^{[2]}\subset\mathcal{T}^{E}E
. Now the initial problem on
E^{[2]}
can be treated as a constrained problem on
\mathcal{T}^{E}E
, the latter being a first-order algebroid. Equations of motion are derived by using the first-order formalism of
[
36
]
applied to
\mathcal{T}^{E}E
. This method seems to be quite complicated (prolongations of prolongations are required) in comparison to our approach, the presence of
\kappa^{[2]}
is hidden somewhere inside the inclusion
E^{[2]}\subset\mathcal{T}^{E}E
, and the direct relation with variational calculus is hard to observe.

The approach of
[
4
]
is conceptually similar to the one of Colombo, but much more general. Given a
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold
F^{k}
, the authors consider a first-order algebroid structure on its
linearisation

D\big(F^{k}\big)
. Again we have a canonical inclusion
F^{k}\subset D\big(F^{k}\big)
, and the
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-order dynamics on
F^{k}
is derived as the first-order constrained dynamics on the algebroid
D\big(F^{k}\big)
. The relation of the methods of
[
4
]
to the ones of this work is so far unclear, although in the case of prolongation of algebroids both approaches give consistent results.

Symmetries and conservation laws.
As explained above, Euler–Lagrange equations on higher pre-algebroids are derived by studying changes of the Lagrangian
L(\gamma)
in the direction of every admissible variation
\delta_{a}\gamma
. By contrast, conservation laws are related with special properties of a particular admissible variation. Below we will sketch basic concepts standing behind conservation laws in higher-order variational calculus.

Let
\gamma\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow E^{k}
be an admissible curve over
\underline{\gamma}\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow M
. The idea is to look for a generator
a\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow F
of an admissible variation
\delta_{a}\gamma
such that
\left\langle\mathrm{d}L(\gamma(t)),\delta_{a}\gamma(t)\right\rangle=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}f(t)
for some smooth function
f\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. Now if
\gamma
is a solution of the Euler–Lagrange equations, by (
5.6
) we have
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}f=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{\left\langle\mathcal{P}_{k}\big(\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k-1}\gamma\big),\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k-1}a\right\rangle_{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\tau}}
, and hence the difference
f-\left\langle\mathcal{P}_{k}\big(\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k-1}\gamma\big),\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k-1}a\right\rangle_{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\tau}
is constant along
\gamma
.

In practice such a method of finding constants of motion is not very effective, as
a
is defined only along
\underline{\gamma}
which is unknown until we actually solve (at least partially) the Euler–Lagrange equations
\mathcal{EL}_{k}\big(\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k}\gamma\big)=0
. Instead it is better to look for a generator universal for every possible
\gamma
by considering
a=s|_{\underline{\gamma}}
, where
s\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M}(F)
is some section of
\tau
. Note that in this case
s^{[k]}|_{\gamma}=\delta_{a}\gamma
, i.e., the admissible variation
\delta_{a}\gamma
is the restriction of the
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-order algebroid lift
s^{[k]}
of
s
.
Note that
\left\langle\mathrm{d}L,s^{[k]}\right\rangle_{\tau_{E^{k}}}
is a smooth function on
E^{k}
. If
\left\langle\mathrm{d}L,s^{[k]}\right\rangle_{\tau_{E^{k}}}=\mathrm{d}f\circ\rho^{k}
for some smooth function
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathrm{T}^{k-1}M)
(note that
\mathrm{d}f
may be regarded as a map
\mathrm{d}f\colon\mathrm{T}^{k}M\subset\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
), we call the section
s\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M}(F)
a
generator of the symmetry of
L
. If this is the case then

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathrm{d}L(\gamma),\delta_{a}\gamma\right\rangle=\big\langle\mathrm{d}L|_{\gamma},s^{[k]}|_{\gamma}\big\rangle_{\tau_{E^{k}}}=\mathrm{d}f\big(\rho^{k}(\gamma)\big)=\mathrm{d}f\big(\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k}\underline{\gamma}\big)=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{f\big(\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k-1}\underline{\gamma}\big)},

i.e.,
\left\langle\mathrm{d}L(\gamma),\delta_{a}\gamma\right\rangle
is a total derivative regardless of the choice of an admissible curve
\gamma
, as intended.
If follows that

f\big(\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k-1}\underline{\gamma}\big)-\left\langle\mathcal{P}_{k}\big(\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k-1}\gamma\big),{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}{s}\circ\rho^{k-1}(\gamma)}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}{\tau}}
is constant, i.e., we derived a
conservation law

related with the symmetry of
L
.

A simple example of a prealgebroid.
Let us end this part with a very simple example of a variational problem on a pre-algebroid. Our starting point is a standard second-order variational problem on the Euclidean plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, constituted by a Lagrangian function
L\colon\mathrm{T}^{2}\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. In this case the HA in question is simply the second tangent bundle
\tau^{2}_{M}\colon\mathrm{T}^{2}\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}
equipped with the standard higher algebroid structure
\kappa^{2}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\colon\mathrm{T}^{2}\mathrm{T}\mathbb{R}^{2}\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{2}\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Formula (
5.5
) leads to the standard Euler–Lagrange equations:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}t^{2}}\left(\frac{\partial L}{\partial\ddot{x}}\right)-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left(\frac{\partial L}{\partial\dot{x}}\right)+\frac{\partial L}{\partial x}=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}t^{2}}\left(\frac{\partial L}{\partial\ddot{y}}\right)-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left(\frac{\partial L}{\partial\dot{y}}\right)+\frac{\partial L}{\partial y}=0,

(5.7)

where
(x,y)
are standard coordinates on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
(x,y,\dot{x},\dot{y},\ddot{x},\ddot{y})
the adapted coordinates on
\mathrm{T}^{2}\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Consider now the following action of
(a,b,c)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
on (the germs at
t=0
of) curves in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
:

\displaystyle(a,b,c)\circ(x(t),y(t))=(x(t)+a+\dot{x}(0)bt,y(t)+c).

Obviously this action reduces to the
\mathbb{R}^{3}
-action on
\mathrm{T}^{2}\mathbb{R}^{2}
given by

\displaystyle(a,b,c)\circ(x,y,\dot{x},\dot{y},\ddot{x},\ddot{y})=(x+a,y+c,\dot{x}(1+b),\dot{y},\ddot{x},\ddot{y}).

If
L
is invariant under this action, then (
5.7
) reduces to

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}t^{2}}\left(\frac{\partial L}{\partial\ddot{x}}\right)=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}t^{2}}\left(\frac{\partial L}{\partial\ddot{y}}\right)-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left(\frac{\partial L}{\partial\dot{y}}\right)=0.

(5.8)

On the other hand, the latter equations can be obtained within the framework of variational calculus on pre-algebroids by an easy reduction procedure. Namely, note that the quotient of
\mathrm{T}^{2}\mathbb{R}^{2}
by the action of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
is naturally a graded space (i.e., a
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold over a point)
\sigma^{2}\colon E^{2}=\mathbb{R}[1]\oplus\mathbb{R}^{2}[2]\rightarrow\{\operatorname{pt}\}
. On
E^{2}
we can introduce graded coordinates
(y_{1},x_{2},y_{2})
induced by
\dot{y}
,
\ddot{x}
and
\ddot{y}
, respectively.

It is clear that an invariant Lagrangian
L
induces a function
l\colon E^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that
L=p\circ l
for
p\colon\mathrm{T}^{2}\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow E^{2}=\mathrm{T}^{2}\mathbb{R}^{2}/\mathbb{R}^{3}
being the quotient map.

We can further equip
\sigma^{2}
with a pre-algebroid structure

\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{2}\mathbb{R}^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{T}^{2}\tau}
\scriptstyle{\kappa^{2}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}E^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{E^{2}}}
\textstyle{\{\operatorname{pt}\}}
\textstyle{E^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

as follows

\displaystyle\big(\kappa^{2}\big)_{(y_{1},x_{2},y_{2})}\colon\ (a,b,\dot{a},\dot{b},\ddot{a},\ddot{b})\mapsto(y_{1},x_{2},y_{2},\dot{y}_{1}=\dot{b},\dot{x}_{2}=\ddot{a},\dot{y}_{2}=\ddot{b}).

For this structure the admissibility condition is empty, since
\tau\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\{\operatorname{pt}\}
has a trivial base. However, an additional condition

\displaystyle\dot{y}_{1}=y_{2}

(5.9)

should be imposed on admissible curves if we want to maintain the correspondence of admissible curves in
E^{2}
with the admissible curves in
\mathrm{T}^{2}\mathbb{R}^{2}
under the reduction procedure (cf. Remark
5.4
).

It is now an easy exercise to show that equations (
5.8
) are the Euler–Lagrange equations for the variational problem on
\big(\sigma^{2},\tau,\kappa^{2}\big)
constituted by function
l\colon E^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and the admissibility condition (
5.9
).

The above example suggests that pre-algebroids may naturally appear in reductions of standard variational problems by actions which are non-trivial on higher jets. (Note that in the standard Lie groupoid–Lie algebroid reduction
[
34
]
and its generalization to higher jets
[
27
]
the action of the groupoid on higher jets is merely a consequence of its action on points – see Remark
5.2
.)

5.2
Examples

Now we shall provide some basic examples of higher-order Euler–Lagrange equations obtained using the formalism introduced in Section
5.2
. For simplicity we will reduce our attention to the case
k=2
.

Variational problem on a prolongation of an AL algebroid.
In Example
4.14
we derived the local form of the second prolongation
\big(E^{[2]},\kappa^{[2]}\big)
of an AL algebroid structure
(E,\kappa)
. Now we can use it to obtain a coordinate formula of the second-order Euler–Lagrange equations. Consider an admissible curve
\gamma(t)=\big(x^{a}(t),y^{i}(t),y^{j,(1)}(t)\big)\in E^{[2]}
. From (
4.13
) the admissibility conditions for
\gamma(t)
are
\dot{x}^{a}=Q^{a}_{i}(x)y^{i}
and
\ddot{x}^{a}=\frac{\partial Q^{a}_{i}}{\partial x^{b}}(x)Q^{b}_{j}(x)y^{i}y^{j}+Q^{a}_{i}(x)y^{i,(1)}
. We can deduce the latter equation from the former under an additional assumption that
y^{i,(1)}(t)=\dot{y}^{i}(t)
, which can be easily justified by the reduction procedure (see
[
25
]
). A generator
a(t)=(x^{a}(t),\xi^{i}(t))\in E_{\gamma(t)}
produces the following admissible variation

\displaystyle\delta_{a}\gamma(t)\colon\ \begin{cases}\delta{x}^{a}=Q^{a}_{i}(x)\xi^{i}(t),\\
\delta{y}^{i}=\dot{\xi}^{i}+Q^{i}_{jk}(x)\,y^{j}\xi^{k},\\
\delta y^{i,(1)}=\ddot{\xi}^{i}+Q^{i}_{jk}(x)y^{j}\dot{\xi}^{k}+Q^{i}_{jk}(x)\dot{y}^{j}\xi^{k}+\dfrac{\partial Q^{i}_{jk}}{\partial x^{a}}(x)Q^{a}_{l}(x)y^{l}y^{j}\xi^{k}=\big(\delta y^{i}\big)^{\cdot}.\end{cases}

Now, for the Lagrangian
L\colon E^{[2]}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, the left hand-side of (
5.4
) reds as

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathrm{d}L(\gamma),\delta_{a}\gamma\right\rangle=\frac{\partial L\ }{\partial x^{a}}\big(x,y,y^{(1)}\big)\delta x^{a}+\frac{\partial L\ }{\partial y^{i}}\big(x,y,y^{(1)}\big)\delta y^{i}+\frac{\partial L\ \ }{\partial y^{i,(1)}}\big(x,y,y^{(1)}\big)\delta y^{i,(1)}.

Expressing it in terms of
\xi^{i}
’s and integrating by parts leads us to

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathrm{d}L(\gamma),\delta_{a}\gamma\right\rangle=\left\langle\left(\delta^{k}_{i}\frac{\mathrm{d}\ }{\mathrm{d}t}-Q^{k}_{ij}(x)y^{j}\right)\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\ }{\mathrm{d}t}\frac{\partial L\ \ }{\partial y^{k,(1)}}-\frac{\partial L\ }{\partial y^{k}}\right)+Q^{a}_{i}(x)\frac{\partial L\ }{\partial x^{a}},\xi^{i}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\hphantom{\left\langle\mathrm{d}L(\gamma),\delta_a\gamma\right\rangle =}{}+\frac{\mathrm{d}\ }{\mathrm{d}t}\left(\left\langle\frac{\partial L\ }{\partial y^{i,(1)}},\dot{\xi}^{i}\right\rangle-\left\langle\left(\delta^{k}_{i}\frac{\mathrm{d}\ }{\mathrm{d}t}-Q^{k}_{ij}(x)y^{j}\right)\frac{\mathrm{d}\ }{\mathrm{d}t}\frac{\partial L\ }{\partial y^{k,(1)}}-\frac{\partial L\ }{\partial y^{i}},\xi^{i}\right\rangle\right).

And hence (cf. (
5.5
)) the
second-order Euler–Lagrange equations
become

\displaystyle\left(\delta^{k}_{i}\frac{\mathrm{d}\ }{\mathrm{d}t}-Q^{k}_{ij}(x)y^{j}\right)\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\ }{\mathrm{d}t}\frac{\partial L\ }{\partial y^{k,(1)}}-\frac{\partial L\ }{\partial y^{k}}\right)+Q^{a}_{i}(x)\frac{\partial L\ }{\partial x^{a}}=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\dot{x}^{a}=Q^{a}_{i}(x)\,y^{i}.

Euler–Poincaré equations.
Let
(\mathfrak{g},[\cdot,\cdot])\simeq(\mathfrak{g},\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}})
be a Lie algebra, and let
l\colon\mathrm{T}\mathfrak{g}\simeq\mathfrak{g}\times\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a Lagrangian function its the second prolongation
\big(\mathfrak{g}^{[2]}=\mathrm{T}\mathfrak{g},\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}^{[2]}\big)
. By setting in the previous example
Q^{a}_{i}(x)=0
and
Q^{i}_{jk}(x)=c^{i}_{jk}
, where
c^{i}_{jk}
denote the structural constants of
\mathfrak{g}
in a given basis
\{e_{j}\}
, we easily arrive at the
second-order Euler–Poincaré equations
for a curve
(a(t),\dot{a}(t))\in\mathrm{T}\mathfrak{g}
:

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\ }{\mathrm{d}t}-\operatorname{ad}^{\ast}_{a(t)}\right)\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\ }{\mathrm{d}t}\frac{\partial l}{\partial\dot{a}}-\frac{\partial l}{\partial a}\right)(a(t),\dot{a}(t))=0.

(5.10)

An alternative, more geometric derivation is possible directly from (
5.2
) and (
5.4
) using formula (
6.1
) – see
[
25
]
for the treatment of the general case. The obtained results are consistent with these of Colombo and De Diego
[
10
]
.

6
Further examples – substructures and quotients of
\bm{\mathrm{T}^{k}G/G}

Another interesting class of examples of (Lie) higher algebroids is obtained from the reduction
\mathrm{T}^{k}G/G
of the
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-tangent bundle of a Lie group
G
. The resulting space
E^{k}:=\mathrm{T}^{k}_{e}G
, consisting of
k
-velocities in
G
based at the identity element
e\in G
, is a
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold over a single point
e\in G
(i.e., a
graded space
– see
[
17
]
). It can be equipped with the canonical HA structure
\kappa^{k}_{\mathfrak{g}}
which, in fact, can be identified with the
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-prolongation (in the sense of Section
4.2
) of
(\mathfrak{g},[\cdot,\cdot])
– the Lie algebra of the group
G
.

Throughout this part we shall describe all higher subalgebroids of
\mathrm{T}^{k}G/G
, and give examples of HA quotients of
\mathrm{T}^{k}G/G
by which we understand higher algebroids
(F^{k},\kappa^{k})
obtained from
\mathrm{T}^{k}G/G
by means of surjective HA morphism onto
F^{k}
.

A (Lie) higher algebroid structure on
\bm{\mathrm{T}^{k}_{e}G}
.

We shall begin by describing the higher algebroid structure on
\mathrm{T}^{k}_{e}G
. First of all,
\mathrm{T}^{k}_{e}G
is a split graded space concentrated in weights
1,2,\ldots,k
in which each homogeneous component is identified with
\mathfrak{g}
, the Lie algebra of
G
. This canonical identification is possible thanks to the group structure on
G
and is obtained by means of the local diffeomorphism
\exp\colon\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow G
inducing an isomorphism
\mathrm{T}^{k}_{0}\exp\colon\mathrm{T}^{k}_{0}\mathfrak{g}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{T}^{k}_{e}G
of graded spaces. (Note that in general, for
k\geq 2
, the
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-tangent space of a manifold at a given point has no canonical vector space structure.) Next,
\mathrm{T}^{k}_{0}\mathfrak{g}
(and so
\mathrm{T}^{k}_{e}G
) has a canonical identification with
\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathfrak{g}
which is, on the other hand, a graded Lie algebra (see Proposition
4.6
), yet the latter gradation is shifted by
-1
with respect to the former. Summing up,
\mathrm{T}^{k}_{e}G
is canonically equipped with two structures: of a graded space (which is split) and of a graded Lie algebra. Both structures are clearly recognized after canonical identifications
\mathrm{T}^{k}_{e}G=\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathbb{R}[t]/\langle{t^{k}}\rangle
.

Now we would like to recall a VBC
\kappa^{k}_{\mathfrak{g}}\colon\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathfrak{g}
constituting a HA structure on
\mathrm{T}^{k}_{e}G\simeq\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathfrak{g}
(see
[
27
, Section 6]
for details). An element
X\in\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathfrak{g}
represented by a curve
t\mapsto\sum_{j=0}^{k}X_{j}t^{j}\in\mathfrak{g}
can be identified with a
(k+1)
-tuple
(X_{0},X_{1},\ldots,X_{k})
of elements of
\mathfrak{g}
. In a similar manner a vector
Y\in\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathfrak{g}\simeq\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathfrak{g}\times\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathfrak{g}
(note that
\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathfrak{g}
is a tangent bundle of a vector space), can be identified with a
2k
-tuple
(Y_{0},\ldots,Y_{k-1},\dot{Y}_{0},\ldots,\dot{Y}_{k-1})
of elements of
\mathfrak{g}
. Now
(X,Y)\in\kappa^{k}_{\mathfrak{g}}
if and only if

\displaystyle\dot{Y}_{l}=(l+1)X_{l+1}-\sum_{i+j=l}[X_{i},Y_{j}],\qquad\text{for every}\quad l=0,1,\ldots,k-1.

(6.1)

The details of this relation can be found in
[
27
, Proposition 6.3]
.

Higher subalgebroids of
\bm{\mathrm{T}^{k}_{e}G}
.
Let
F^{k}
be a split graded subspace of
\mathrm{T}_{e}^{k}G\simeq\mathfrak{g}[t]/\langle{t^{k}}\rangle
, so there are linear subspaces
V_{j}\subset\mathfrak{g}
(
0\leq j\leq k-1
) such that
F^{k}=\big\{\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}v_{j}t^{j}+\langle{t^{k}}\rangle\colon v_{j}\in V_{j}\big\}
. We recall that homogeneous functions of weight
j+1
on
\mathrm{T}_{e}^{k}G
correspond to linear functions on the summand
t^{j}\mathfrak{g}
.

Proposition 6.1

(subalgebroids of
\mathrm{T}^{k}_{e}G
)
.

A split graded subbundle
F^{k}\subset\mathrm{T}^{k}_{e}G
is a higher subalgebroid if and only if

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}^{k-1}V_{0}\subset F^{k}\qquad\text{and}\qquad[V_{0},V_{i}]\subset V_{j}\qquad\text{for each}\quad i=0,1,\ldots,k-1\quad\text{and}\quad j\geq i.\!\!\!

(6.2)

In particular,
V_{0}
should be a Lie subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
and additionally each subspace
V_{i}
should contain
V_{0}
and be preserved by the action of the latter subspace.

Proof.

We should basically check if
\kappa^{k}_{\mathfrak{g}}
restricts fine to a VBC from
\mathrm{T}^{k}V_{0}
to
\mathrm{T}F^{k}
.
Take any
\underline{Y}=(Y_{0},\ldots,Y_{k-1})\in F^{k}\subset\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathfrak{g}
and
X=(X_{0},\ldots,X_{k})\in\mathrm{T}^{k}V_{0}\subset\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathfrak{g}
. So all the components
X_{j}
are in
V_{0}
and
Y_{i}\in V_{i}
for
i=0,\ldots,k-1
. Let
Y:=(\underline{Y},\dot{\underline{Y}})=\big(\kappa^{k}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big)_{\underline{Y}}(X)
be the element in
\mathrm{T}_{\underline{Y}}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathfrak{g}
which is
\kappa^{k}_{\mathfrak{g}}
-related to
X
. The components of
\dot{\underline{Y}}=(\dot{Y}_{0},\ldots,\dot{Y}_{k-1})
are determined by (
6.1
). We should examine when
\dot{\underline{Y}}\in F^{k}
, i.e., when
\dot{Y}_{i}\in V_{i}
for each
i=0,1,\ldots,k-1
. This happens if and only if

\displaystyle V_{0}+\sum_{i=0}^{j}[V_{0},V_{i}]\subset V_{j}

for each
j=0,1,\ldots,k-1
. This gives (
6.2
).
∎

Quotients of
\bm{\mathrm{T}^{k}_{e}G}
.
General quotients of Lie algebroids and the concept of an ideal in a Lie algebroid are quite involved subjects (see
[
34
]
). For this reason in the following definition of a quotient (Lie) higher algebroid structure we shall restrict our attention only to “quotient” maps covering the identity on the base. We call a
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold morphism
\phi^{k}\colon E^{k}\rightarrow F^{k}

surjective
if for each
1\leq j\leq k
the induced top core vector bundle morphisms
\widehat{\phi^{j}}\colon\widehat{E^{j}}\rightarrow\widehat{F^{j}}
are fibre-wise surjective linear maps. In particular, such a morphisms always is a surjective mapping
E^{k}\rightarrow F^{k}
but not vice versa: a
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold morphism which is a surjective mapping need not induce surjective mapping between the core bundles.

Definition 6.2

(a quotient of a (Lie) HA)
.

We shall say that a
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-order algebroid
\big(\sigma^{k}_{F}\colon F^{k}\rightarrow M,\kappa^{k,F}\big)
is a
quotient
of a (Lie) higher algebroid
\big(\sigma^{k}_{E}\colon E^{k}\rightarrow M,\kappa^{k,E}\big)
if there is a surjective
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold morphism
\phi^{k}\colon E^{k}\rightarrow F^{k}
covering the identity on the base manifolds such that
\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}\phi^{1},\mathrm{T}\phi^{k}\big)
is a
{\mathcal{VBC}}
-morphism
\kappa^{k,E}\Rightarrow\kappa^{k,F}
. In other words, elements
X^{\prime}\in\mathrm{T}^{k}F^{1}
and
Y^{\prime}\in\mathrm{T}F^{k}
are
\kappa^{k,F}
-related if and only if they are images under projections
\mathrm{T}^{k}\phi^{1}
and
\mathrm{T}\phi^{k}
, respectively, of some
\kappa^{k,E}
-related elements
X\in\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{1}
and
Y\in\mathrm{T}E^{k}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 24.88239pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-15.29341pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.56447pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{T^{k}\phi^{1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern
