83.66322pt\raise-16.49501pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.56447pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{T}\phi^{k}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 83.66322pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-15.22398pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}F^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.69553pt\raise-26.74501pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.24501pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\kappa^{k,F}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 67.04341pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 4.5pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-4.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 39.29341pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 69.29341pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{T}F^{k}.}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{split}

(6.3)

Example 6.3

(a class of examples)
.

A slight generalization of the example
\big(\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathfrak{g},\kappa^{k}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big)
is possible. Let
E^{k}=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{k-1}\mathfrak{g}_{i}
be a graded Lie algebra. In particular,
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
has a Lie algebra structure and, consequently,
\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathfrak{g}_{0}
also is graded Lie algebra. Let
\alpha\colon\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathfrak{g}_{0}\rightarrow E^{k}
be a graded Lie algebra homomorphism such that
\alpha_{0}=\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}_{0}}
, where
\alpha_{0}
is the restriction of
\alpha
to the subalgebra
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
being the component in degree
0
of the graded Lie algebra
\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathfrak{g}_{0}
. For
X\in\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathfrak{g}_{0}
let
(X_{\bar{0}},X_{\bar{1}})\in\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathfrak{g}_{0}\simeq\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathfrak{g}_{0}\times\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathfrak{g}_{0}
be the image of
X
under the canonical embedding
\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathfrak{g}_{0}\subset\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathfrak{g}_{0}
. We consider
E^{k}
as a (split) graded space defined by the weight vector field on
E^{k}
given by
\Delta=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}(i+1)\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}_{i}}
, where
\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}_{i}}
is the Euler vector field on
\mathfrak{g}_{i}
.
Then the formula

\displaystyle\big(\kappa^{k}\big)_{y}(X)=\alpha(X_{\bar{1}})+[y,\alpha(X_{\bar{0}})]_{E^{k}},

(6.4)

where
y\in E^{k}
, turns
E^{k}
into a higher Lie algebroid. The proof is left to the reader. (One should notice that vector fields
F_{(a,b)}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{g})
,
F_{(a,b)}\colon y\mapsto[y,a]_{\mathfrak{g}}+b
, where
\mathfrak{g}
is an arbitrary Lie algebra and
y,a,b\in\mathfrak{g}
, form a Lie subalgebra of
\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{g})
isomorphic with
\mathfrak{g}\oplus\varepsilon\mathfrak{g}
.) We shall show that
\big(E^{k},\kappa^{k}\big)
is a quotient of
\big(\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathfrak{g}_{0},\kappa^{k}_{\mathfrak{g}_{0}}\big)
if
\alpha
is surjective. Let
J:=\ker\alpha=\oplus_{i=0}^{k-1}J_{i}
, so
\mathfrak{g}_{i}=\mathfrak{g}_{0}/J_{i}
. Consider the diagram (
6.3
) in our case:

\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathfrak{g}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T^{k}\alpha_{0}=\operatorname{id}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathfrak{g}_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\kappa^{k}_{\mathfrak{g}_{0}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathfrak{g}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{T}\alpha}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathfrak{g}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa^{k}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}E^{k}.}

Take
y\in E^{k}
and
X\in\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathfrak{g}_{0}
as above. Let
\tilde{y}\in y+J
be any pre-image of
y
under the projection
\alpha
. Using (
6.1
) we find that the vector
\big(\kappa^{k}_{\mathfrak{g}_{0}}\big)_{\tilde{y}}X\in\mathrm{T}_{\tilde{y}}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathfrak{g}_{0}
is represented by the curve
t\mapsto\tilde{y}+t(X_{\bar{1}}+[\tilde{y},X_{\bar{0}}])\in\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathfrak{g}_{0}
. Therefore
\mathrm{T}\alpha\big(\big(\kappa^{k}_{\mathfrak{g}_{0}}\big)_{\tilde{y}}X\big)\in\mathrm{T}E^{k}
does not depend on the choice of
\tilde{y}
and is given by the formula (
6.4
) as
\alpha(\tilde{y})=y
and
\alpha([\tilde{y},X_{\bar{0}}])=[y,\alpha(X_{\bar{0}})]_{E^{k}}
.

7
Final remarks

The main idea of this paper was to use the framework of vector bundle comorphisms in order to provide a proper language to describe higher analogs of (Lie) algebroids, having in mind potential applications in variational calculus and geometric mechanics. Our studies suggest a few (in our opinion interesting) directions of future research, which we discuss below.

Left-twisted algebroids.
In Lemma
2.9
we studied linear VBCs
\kappa\colon\mathrm{T}\sigma\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\tau_{E}
which induce the identity on the core. By modifying the latter condition we can define generalization of the concept of an algebroid. For example if
\kappa
is a linear VBC which induces the identity on the base
\kappa\cap(M\times M)=\operatorname{graph}(\operatorname{id}_{M})\subset M\times M
then the left and right anchors
\rho_{L},\rho_{R}\colon E\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M
are still well-defined. Moreover, such a
\kappa
induces a VB endomorphism on the cores
\phi\colon\sigma\rightarrow\sigma
over
\operatorname{id}_{M}
. By an analogous argument to the one used in the proof of Proposition
2.15
such a
\kappa
defines a
left-
\phi
-twisted algebroid
structure which satisfies a modified equation (
2.6
)

\displaystyle[f\cdot a,g\cdot b]=f\rho_{L}(a)(g)\cdot\phi(b)-g\rho_{R}(b)(f)\cdot a+fg\cdot[a,b].

The converse implication is also true (the argument used in the proof of Lemma
2.11
works without any change): a left-
\phi
-twisted algebroid structure on
\sigma
uniquely determines a linear VBC
\kappa\colon\mathrm{T}\sigma\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\tau_{E}
which induces an endomorphism
\phi
on the cores.

For example, a linear connection on the tangent bundle
\mathrm{T}M
is an
\mathbb{R}
-bi-linear operator
\nabla\colon\mathfrak{X}(M)\times\mathfrak{X}(M)\rightarrow\mathfrak{X}(M)
satisfying
\nabla_{X}(f\cdot Y)=f\cdot\nabla_{X}Y+X(f)\cdot Y
and
\nabla_{f\cdot X}Y=f\cdot\nabla_{X}Y
for any vector fields
X,Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
and any smooth function
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
. Now
[X,Y]:=-\nabla_{Y}X
is a left-
\phi
-twisted algebroid structure with both anchors being the identities
\operatorname{id}_{\mathrm{T}M}
, and
\phi\colon\mathrm{T}M\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M
being the null map. The related VBC
\kappa
maps a given element
A\in\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}M
to the
\nabla
-horizontal lift of
\tau_{\mathrm{T}M}(A)
at
\mathrm{T}\tau_{M}(A)
.

Pre-algebroids and reduction.
As we have seen in Section
5
for many interesting applications, we do not need the full structure of a (higher) algebroid, but it is enough to have a less rigid structure of a (higher) pre-algebroid. It seems to us that such objects can naturally appear as reductions of higher tangent bundles, while considering (pseudo) group actions on the space of smooth curves on manifolds in the spirit of
[
31
]
(see an example of such a situation in the last paragraph of Section
5
). Thus it would be interesting to initiate a systematic study of such objects. For example a structure of a pre-algebroid of order one
(\sigma\colon E\rightarrow M,\tau\colon F\rightarrow M,\kappa)
can be equivalently characterized as a pair of anchor maps
\rho^{E}\colon E\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M
and
\rho^{F}\colon F\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M
a VB morphism
\phi\colon F\rightarrow E
over the identity on
M
and a bi-linear bracket operation
[\cdot,\cdot]\colon\operatorname{Sec}(E)\times\operatorname{Sec}(F)\rightarrow\operatorname{Sec}(E)
satisfying, for every sections
a\in\operatorname{Sec}(E)
,
b\in\operatorname{Sec}(F)
and functions
f,g\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
, a Leibniz-like rule

\displaystyle[f\cdot a,g\cdot b]=f\rho^{E}(a)(g)\cdot\phi(b)-g\rho^{F}(b)(f)\cdot a+fg\cdot[a,b].

In particular, left-twisted algebroids provide examples of pre-algebroids (with
\tau=\sigma
).

Applications to variational problems.
In Section
5
we only sketched some possibilities of applications of the theory of higher (pre)algebroids in geometric mechanics. We postpone a more detailed study to a separate publication. Let us only mention here the problem of developing a constrained higher-order Lagrangian dynamics in this setting. Following the elegant treatment of the first-order case in
[
13
]
, for a Lagrangian system constituted by a function
L\colon E^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
on a higher algebroid
\kappa^{k}\colon\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{1}\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\mathrm{T}E^{k}
, there are basically two different concepts of constraints.
Vakonomic constraints
should be understood as a graded subbundle
D^{k}\subset F^{k}
and the related dynamics is generated by these admissible variations which are valued in
\mathrm{T}D^{k}
. On the other hand,
nonholonomic constraints
are constituted by restricting the set of generators to a subbundle
D\subset E^{1}
. That is, admissible variations are obtained from the restriction
\kappa^{k}|_{\mathrm{T}^{k}D}\colon\mathrm{T}^{k}D\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\mathrm{T}E^{k}
.

Structure of (Lie) higher algebroids.
One of us initiated a study of the internal structure of (Lie) higher algebroids. It turns out that, at least in order 2, (Lie) higher algebroids have a geometric description in terms of a collection of bundle maps and differential operators which should satisfy certain compatibility conditions. The details can be find in a forthcoming publication
[
42
]
.

Appendix A
Appendix – proofs of technical results

A proof of Lemma
2.11
.
Note that, according to Lemma
2.9
(
iii
), every relation satisfying (
i
) and (
ii
) must respect the natural action of the core
C\simeq E
on
\mathrm{T}E
. Thus if the assertion holds, the following generalization of formula (
2.8
)

\displaystyle\kappa_{a}\left[\mathrm{T}b(\rho_{L}(a))\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}c\right]:=\mathrm{T}a(\rho_{R}(b))\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}[a,b]\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}c

(A.1)

is valid for every sections
a,b,c\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M}(E)
. We shall show that this generalized formula defines a differential relation
\kappa
satisfying conditions (
i
) and (
ii
). This will end the proof.

Denote
F(a,b):=\mathrm{T}b(\rho_{L}(a))
and
G(a,b):=\mathrm{T}a(\rho_{R}(b))\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}[a,b]
. Leaving aside (for a moment) the problem of correctness of the definition of
\kappa
, note that formula (
A.1
) relates an element
F(a,b)\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}c
, whose
\mathrm{T}\sigma
- and
\tau_{E}
-projections are, respectively,
\rho_{L}(a)
and
b
, with an element
G(a,b)\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}c
, for which these projections are
\rho_{R}(b)
and
a
, respectively. We clearly see that the VBC
\kappa
(if correctly defined) covers the left anchor
\rho_{L}
and projects to the graph of the right anchor
\rho_{R}
under
\tau_{E}\times\mathrm{T}\sigma
.

We want to prove that
\kappa
is a VBC over
\rho_{L}
. Note that every element
B
of the
\mathrm{T}\sigma
-fibre
(\mathrm{T}E)_{\rho_{L}(a)}
for any
a\in E_{x}
can be represented as
B=\mathrm{T}b|_{x}(\rho_{L}(a))\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}c|_{x}
for some sections
b,c\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M}(E)
, and thus (
A.1
) gives us the value of
\kappa_{a}
on every element of
(\mathrm{T}E)_{\rho_{L}(a)}
. (Note that if
\rho_{L}(a)=0
, then also
\mathrm{T}b|_{x}(\rho_{L}(a))
is a null vector regardless of the choice of section
b
and hence the initial formula (
2.8
) is not sufficient to determine the values of
\kappa_{a}
for every possible element of
(\mathrm{T}E)_{\rho_{L}(a)}
. This justifies the need of using the extended formula (
A.1
).) It is enough to show that the value
\kappa_{a}(B)
does not depend on the chosen presentation
B=F(a,b)|_{x}\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}c|_{x}
, and that the resulting differential relation is bi-linear.

It follows directly from (
2.6
) that for any smooth function
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
we have
G(f\cdot a,b)=f\cdot_{\mathrm{T}\sigma}G(a,b)
, i.e.,
G(a,b)
is tensorial with respect to
a
, and hence the value of
\kappa_{a}(B)
given by (
A.1
) (for
B
as above) does not depend on the particular choice of the section
a
, but only on the value
a(x)
. Now assume that we present
B=F(a,b)|_{x}\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}c|_{x}
in a different way as
B=F(a,b^{\prime})|_{x}\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}c^{\prime}|_{x}
. We shall show that formula (
A.1
) gives the same value of
\kappa_{a}(B)
for both presentations. Clearly,
b|_{x}=b^{\prime}|_{x}
and hence we may present
b^{\prime}-b=f\cdot b^{\prime\prime}
for some smooth function
f
vanishing at
x
and some (local) section
b^{\prime\prime}\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M}(E)
. By our assumption,

\displaystyle B=F(a,b)|_{x}\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}c|_{x}=\mathrm{T}b|_{x}(\rho_{L}(a))\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}c|_{x}=\mathrm{T}b^{\prime}|_{x}(\rho_{L}(a))\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}c^{\prime}|_{x}=\mathrm{T}(b+f\cdot b^{\prime\prime})|_{x}(\rho_{L}(a))\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}c^{\prime}|_{x}

\displaystyle\hphantom{B}=F(a,b)|_{x}+f|_{x}\cdot F(a,b^{\prime\prime})|_{x}\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}\rho_{L}(a)(f)\cdot b^{\prime\prime}|_{x}\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}c^{\prime}|_{x}=F(a,b)|_{x}\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}\rho_{L}(a)(f)\cdot b^{\prime\prime}|_{x}\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}c^{\prime}|_{x},

and we conclude that
\rho_{L}(a)(f)\cdot b^{\prime\prime}|_{x}=(c-c^{\prime})|_{x}
. Now we can use this fact to get

\displaystyle G(a,b)|_{x}\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}c|_{x}=\mathrm{T}a|_{x}(\rho_{R}(b))\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}[a,b]|_{x}\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}c|_{x}=\mathrm{T}a|_{x}(\rho_{R}(b^{\prime}))\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}[a,b-f\cdot b^{\prime\prime}]|_{x}\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}c|_{x}

\displaystyle\hphantom{G(a,b)|_x\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}c|_x}{}=G(a,b^{\prime})\bm{\boldsymbol{-}}f|_{x}\cdot[a,b^{\prime\prime}]|_{x}\bm{\boldsymbol{-}}\rho_{L}(a)(f)\cdot b^{\prime\prime}|_{x}\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}c|_{x}

\displaystyle\hphantom{G(a,b)|_x\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}c|_x}{}=G(a,b^{\prime})\bm{\boldsymbol{-}}\rho_{L}(a)(f)\cdot b^{\prime\prime}|_{x}\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}c|_{x}=G(a,b^{\prime})|_{x}\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}c^{\prime}|_{x}.

It follows that
\kappa_{a}(B)
is indeed well-defined.

Finally to show that
\kappa
defined by formula (
A.1
) is bi-linear we need to check that if
(B,A)\in\kappa
then also
(f\cdot_{\mathrm{T}\sigma}B,f\cdot_{\tau_{E}}A)\in\kappa
and
(f\cdot_{\tau_{E}}B,f\cdot_{\mathrm{T}\sigma}A)\in\kappa
. This can be easily done using the following properties od
F
and
G
:

\displaystyle G(f\cdot a,b)=f\cdot_{\mathrm{T}\sigma}G(a,b),\qquad F(f\cdot a,b)=f\cdot_{\tau_{E}}F(a,b),

\displaystyle G(a,f\cdot b)=f\cdot_{\tau_{E}}G(a,b)\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}\rho_{L}(a)(f)b,\qquad F(a,f\cdot b)=f\cdot_{\mathrm{T}\sigma}F(a,b)\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}\rho_{L}(a)(f)b,

(A.2)

which follow directly from (
2.6
).

A proof of Proposition
2.15
.
From the results of Lemma
2.9
we already know that every linear VBC inducing the identity on the cores gives rise to a well-defined left and right anchor maps. Thus we need only to check if formula (
2.9
) defines an algebroid bracket compatible with these anchors. First note that given any sections
a,b\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M}(E)
the right-hand side of (
2.9
) is a difference of two elements of
\mathrm{T}E
with the same
\tau_{E}
-projection
a
and the same
\mathrm{T}\sigma
-projection
\rho_{R}(b)
, thus a vertical vector. That is,
[a,b]\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M}(E)
is well-defined. To check that this bracket satisfies the Leibniz rule (
2.6
) we have to study the behaviour of formula (
2.9
) under rescaling
a\mapsto f\cdot a
and
b\mapsto g\cdot b
. The calculations are basically the same as in the proof of Lemma
2.11
– see formulas (
A.2
).

A proof of Proposition
2.21
.
Note first that if we have a
{\mathcal{VBC}}
-morphism
(\mathrm{T}\phi,\mathrm{T}\phi)\colon\kappa\Rightarrow\kappa^{\prime}
, then (see diagram (
2.3
)) the commutativity at the level of base maps (the left anchors) gives us precisely the commutativity of the first diagram in (
2.10
). The commutativity of the second diagram follows from an observation that
(\mathrm{T}\phi,\mathrm{T}\phi)
is also a
{\mathcal{VBC}}
-morphism between
\kappa^{\mathrm{T}}
and
{\kappa^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{T}}
, whose base maps are the right anchors (cf. Lemma
2.9
and Remark
2.12
). This is an immediate consequence of Proposition
2.6
where the characterisation of being a
{\mathcal{VBC}}
-morphism does not depend on the direction of the considered relations, in contrast to the formulation of Definition
2.3
. From now on we can thus assume that (
2.10
) holds.

Choose any two sections
a,b\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M}(E)
and let
\phi_{\ast}a=\sum_{i}f_{i}\cdot\underline{\phi}^{\ast}a_{i}
and
\phi_{\ast}b=\sum_{j}g_{i}\cdot\underline{\phi}^{\ast}b_{j}
as in Definition
2.19
. In the following calculations we understand a section
s\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M}(\underline{\phi}^{\ast}E^{\prime})
as a map
s\colon M\rightarrow E^{\prime}
such that
s(x)\in E^{\prime}_{\underline{\phi}(x)}
and thus
\mathbf{V}_{s}{s^{\prime}}
, where
s,s^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M}(\underline{\phi}^{\ast}E^{\prime})
, is a map
M\rightarrow\mathrm{T}E^{\prime}
,
x\mapsto\mathbf{V}_{s}{s^{\prime}}(x)
, where
\mathbf{V}_{s}{s^{\prime}}(x)
is represented by the curve
t\mapsto s(x)+ts^{\prime}(x)\in E^{\prime}_{\underline{\phi}(x)}
. We have

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}\phi[\mathrm{T}a(\rho_{R}(b))]=\sum_{i}[\rho_{R}(b)(f_{i})\cdot\mathbf{V}_{\phi_{\ast}a}\underline{\phi}^{\ast}a_{i}+f_{i}\cdot_{\mathrm{T}\sigma^{\prime}}\mathrm{T}a_{i}\mathrm{T}\underline{\phi}(\rho_{R}(b))]

\displaystyle\hphantom{\mathrm{T}\phi[\mathrm{T}a(\rho_R(b)) ]}{}\overset{\eqref{eqn:compatible_anchors}}{=}\sum_{i}[\rho_{R}(b)(f_{i})\cdot\mathbf{V}_{\phi_{\ast}a}\underline{\phi}^{\ast}a_{i}+f_{i}\cdot_{\mathrm{T}\sigma^{\prime}}\mathrm{T}a_{i}\,\rho_{R}^{\prime}(\phi_{\ast}b)]

\displaystyle\hphantom{\mathrm{T}\phi[\mathrm{T}a(\rho_R(b)) ]}{}=\sum_{i}\rho_{R}(b)(f_{i})\cdot\mathbf{V}_{\phi_{\ast}a}\underline{\phi}^{\ast}a_{i}+\sum_{i,j}f_{i}\cdot_{\mathrm{T}\sigma^{\prime}}g_{j}\cdot_{\tau_{E^{\prime}}}\mathrm{T}a_{i}\rho_{R}^{\prime}(\underline{\phi}^{\ast}b_{j}).

Similarly,

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}\phi[\mathrm{T}b(\rho_{L}(a))]=\sum_{j}\rho_{L}(a)(g_{j})\cdot\mathbf{V}_{\phi_{\ast}b}\underline{\phi}^{\ast}b_{j}+\sum_{i,j}g_{j}\cdot_{\mathrm{T}\sigma^{\prime}}f_{i}\cdot_{\tau_{E^{\prime}}}\mathrm{T}b_{j}\rho_{L}^{\prime}(\underline{\phi}^{\ast}a_{i})

and we conclude that

\displaystyle\kappa^{\prime}_{\phi(a)}[\mathrm{T}\phi[\mathrm{T}b(\rho_{L}(a))]]-\mathrm{T}\phi[\mathrm{T}a(\rho_{R}(b))]

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\mathbf{V}_{\phi_{\ast}a}\bigg[\sum_{j}\rho_{L}(a)(g_{j})\cdot\underline{\phi}^{\ast}b_{j}-\sum_{i}\rho_{R}(b)(f_{i})\cdot\underline{\phi}^{\ast}a_{i}+\sum_{i,j}f_{i}g_{j}\cdot\underline{\phi}^{\ast}[a_{i},b_{j}]^{\prime}\bigg].

Applying the tangent map
\mathrm{T}\phi
to formula (
2.9
) leaves us with

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}_{\phi_{\ast}a}\phi_{\ast}[a,b]:=\mathrm{T}\phi[\kappa_{a}[\mathrm{T}b(\rho_{L}(a))]]-\mathrm{T}\phi[\mathrm{T}a(\rho_{R}(b))].

The comparison of the latter two equations shows that (
2.11
) holds if and only if
\mathrm{T}\phi[\kappa_{a}\![\mathrm{T}b(\rho_{L}(a))]]\!\!

=\kappa^{\prime}_{\phi(a)}[\mathrm{T}\phi[\mathrm{T}b(\rho_{L}(a))]]
. The latter equality (for every
a
and
b
) is equivalent to
(\mathrm{T}\phi,\mathrm{T}\phi)
being a
{\mathcal{VBC}}
-morphism between
\kappa
and
\kappa^{\prime}
.

Equivalent characterizations of algebroid morphisms.

Proposition A.1
.

Let
\sigma\colon E\rightarrow M
and
\sigma^{\prime}\colon E^{\prime}\rightarrow M^{\prime}
be vector bundles carrying
(
Lie
)
algebroid structures as in Definition
2.19
. Let

\phi\colon E\rightarrow E^{\prime}
over
\underline{\phi}\colon M\rightarrow M^{\prime}
be a VB morphism. The following are equivalent:

(i)

\phi
is an algebroid morphism
(
in the sense of Definition
2.19
)
.

(ii)

The graph of
\phi
is a subalgebroid of the product
(
Lie
)
algebroid on
\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime}
.

(iii)

The graph of
\phi^{\ast}
is a coisotropic submanifold of the Leibniz manifold
[
19
]

(E^{\ast}\times{E^{\prime}}^{\ast},-\Lambda_{E}+\Lambda_{E^{\prime}})
.

Proof.

Recall
[
19
]
that a
Leibniz manifold

(M,\Lambda)
is a manifold
M
equipped with a contravariant 2-tensor
\Lambda
, and that
linear
contravariant 2-tensors on the dual
\sigma^{\ast}\colon E^{\ast}\rightarrow M
of a vector bundle
\sigma\colon E\rightarrow M
are in one-to-one correspondence with general algebroid structures on
\sigma
. This correspondence is completely parallel to that between linear Poisson tensors and Lie algebroid structures.

The equivalence between (
ii
) and (
iii
) is proved in
[
15
, Theorem 5.1]
for skew algebroids, however, the proof can be directly rewritten for general algebroids. Thus we need only to show the equivalence between (
i
) and (
ii
).

Let
F\subset E\times E^{\prime}
,
N\subset M\times M^{\prime}
be the graphs of
\phi
and
\underline{\phi}
, respectively, and assume that
\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime}|_{F}\colon F\rightarrow N
is a subalgebroid of
\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime}
. As
\phi
is a vector bundle morphism, the base map
\underline{\phi}
is smooth. Because
(\rho_{L},\rho_{L}^{\prime})
and
(\rho_{R},\rho_{R}^{\prime})
map
F
to
\mathrm{T}N\subset\mathrm{T}(M\times M^{\prime})
we have commuting diagrams in (
2.10
). To prove (
2.11
) take sections
a,b\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M}(E)
, write
\phi\circ a=\sum_{i}f_{i}\underline{\phi}^{\ast}a_{i}
and similarly,
\phi\circ b=\sum_{j}g_{j}\underline{\phi}^{\ast}b_{j}

and notice that
(x,\underline{\phi}(x))\mapsto(a(x),\phi\circ a(x))
is a section of
F
whose extension to a section of
\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime}
can be written as follows

\displaystyle\tilde{a}(x,y)=\bigg(a(x),\sum_{i}f_{i}(x)a_{i}(y)\bigg),

and similarly for section
b
. We clearly have

\displaystyle[\tilde{a},\tilde{b}]_{\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime}}(x,y)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\bigg([a,b](x),\sum_{i,j}f_{i}(x)g_{j}(x)[a_{i},b_{j}]^{\prime}(y)+\rho_{L}(a)(g_{j})(x)b_{j}(y)-\rho_{R}(b)(f_{i})(x)a_{i}(y)\bigg),

hence
[\tilde{a},\tilde{b}](x,\underline{\phi}(x))\in F
implies (
2.11
). This reasoning can be inverted proving the equivalence of conditions (
i
) and (
ii
).
∎

A proof of Proposition
2.26
.
By definition, condition (
i
) is equivalent to
\kappa
being a subalgebroid of the product (Lie) algebroid structure on
\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{2}
. Note that for this structure the left and right anchors are, respectively,
\rho_{L}:=(\rho_{1L},\rho_{2L})
and
\rho_{R}:=(\rho_{1R},\rho_{2R})
, whereas the algebroid bracket reads as

\displaystyle[(s_{1},s_{2}),(s_{1}^{\prime},s_{2}^{\prime})]:=([s_{1},s_{1}^{\prime}]_{1},[s_{2},s_{2}^{\prime}]_{2})

for any sections
s_{1},s_{1}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M_{1}}(E_{1})
and
s_{2},s_{2}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M_{2}}(E_{2})
. It is thus enough to prove that conditions (
iia
) and (
iib
) are equivalent to conditions (
i
) and (
ii
) of Definition
2.22
taken for
\sigma^{\prime}:=\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{2}|_{r}\colon E_{1}\times E_{2}\supset r=:E^{\prime}\rightarrow M^{\prime}:=\operatorname{graph}(\underline{r})\subset M_{1}\times M_{2}
,
\sigma:=\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{2}
and
\kappa:=\kappa_{1}\times\kappa_{2}
.

First observe that for sections
s_{1}\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M_{1}}(E_{1})
and
s_{2}\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M_{2}}(E_{2})
the restriction
(s_{1},s_{2})|_{M^{\prime}}
is a section of the vector subbundle
\sigma^{\prime}\subset\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{2}
if and only if
s_{2}=\hat{r}(s_{1})
and, moreover, every section of
\sigma^{\prime}
can be presented as such a restriction. Thus condition (
iia
) is equivalent to the fact that
\sigma_{I}=(\sigma_{1I},\sigma_{2I})
maps
E^{\prime}\subset E_{1}\times E_{2}
to
\mathrm{T}M^{\prime}=\mathrm{T}\operatorname{graph}(\underline{r})\subset\mathrm{T}M_{1}\times\mathrm{T}M_{2}
for
I=L,R
. That is precisely condition (
i
) of Definition
2.22
for
\sigma^{\prime}
,
\sigma
and
\kappa
as above.

Next note that condition (
iib
) means that the algebroid bracket on
\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{2}
is closed with respect to sections of the form
(s_{1},\widehat{r}(s_{1}))
, where
s_{1}\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M_{1}}(E_{1})
. As we already observed such sections are
\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{2}
-extensions of all possible sections of
\sigma^{\prime}
. Since, by the remark following Definition
2.22
, condition (
i
) of this definition guarantees that the induced bracket defined on
\sigma^{\prime}
does not depend on the choice of extensions of sections of
\sigma^{\prime}
, we conclude that if (
iia
) holds then (
iib
) is equivalent to condition (
ii
) of Definition
2.22
for
\sigma^{\prime}
,
\sigma
and
\kappa
as above. This ends the proof.

A proof of Lemma
2.29
.
The relation between the algebroid bracket and VBC
\kappa
is given by formula (
2.8
). As has been observed in Remark
2.12
,
\kappa^{T}
corresponds to another general algebroid structure with the bracket
[a,b]^{T}:=-[b,a]
(note that this passage is possible due to the fact that
\kappa
is bi-linear and that it induces the identity on the core). Consequently the symmetry condition
\kappa=\kappa^{T}
is equivalent to the antisymmetry of the bracket
[a,b]=-[b,a]
. This proves (
i
).

Assume now that the algebroid structure on
\sigma
is skew. To prove (
ii
) apply
\mathrm{T}\rho
to (
2.9
) to get

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}_{\rho(a)}\rho([a,b])=\mathrm{T}\rho(\kappa_{a}[\mathrm{T}b(\rho(a))])-\mathrm{T}\rho(a)(\rho(b)).

On the other hand, formula (
2.9
) for the commutator of vector fields
\rho(a),\rho(b)\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M}(\mathrm{T}M)
gives us

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}_{\rho(a)}[\rho(a),\rho(b)]_{\mathrm{T}M}:=(\kappa_{\mathrm{T}M})_{\rho(a)}[\mathrm{T}\rho(b)(\rho(a))]-\mathrm{T}\rho(a)(\rho(b)).

By comparing the above two formulas it is clear that (
2.7
) is equivalent to (
2.12
).

Point (
iii
) is equivalent to the fact that the dual map
\kappa^{\ast}\colon\mathrm{T}^{\ast}E\rightarrow\mathrm{T}E^{\ast}
(cf. Remark
2.17
) is a linear Poisson map, which is a well-known equivalent characterization of the Jacobi identity
[
19
]
. By the results of
[
15
]
the latter condition is equivalent to
\kappa
being the algebroidal relation.

A proof of Proposition
3.8
.

(i)

Let
\big(x^{a}_{w},X^{A}_{w}\big)
,
\big(y^{i}_{w},Y^{I}_{w}\big)
be graded coordinates on
E_{1}^{k}
and
E_{2}^{k}
, respectively, where the capital letters indicate linear coordinates, i.e.,
X^{A}_{w}
and
Y^{I}_{w}
are of weight
(1,w)
. A VBC
r
is given locally in
E_{1}^{k}\times E_{2}^{k}
by a system of equations of the form

\displaystyle r\colon\ \begin{cases}x^{a}_{w}=f^{a}_{w}(y),\\
Y^{I}_{w}=\sum_{A}X^{A}_{w^{\prime}}g_{A,w-w^{\prime}}^{I}(y),\end{cases}

(A.3)

where
f^{a}_{w}
and
g_{A,w}^{I}
are (local) functions on
M_{2}^{k}
of weight
w
. Equations for
r^{j}\subset E_{1}^{j}\times E_{2}^{j}
are obtained from (
A.3
) by removing all equations in which at least one coordinate has weight greater than
j
. Clearly, such a system defines a VBC as
r^{j}
is a vector subspace of
E_{1}^{j}\times E_{2}^{j}\rightarrow M_{1}^{0}\times M_{2}^{0}
with the property: given
(y^{i}_{w})\in M_{2}^{j}
and
\big(x_{w}^{a},X_{w}^{A}\big)\in E_{1}^{j}
such that
x^{a}_{w}=f^{a}_{w}(y)
there is a unique
\big(y^{i}_{w},Y^{I}_{w}\big)\in E_{2}^{j}
(namely,
Y^{I}_{w}=\sum_{A}X^{A}_{w^{\prime}}\,g_{A,w-w^{\prime}}^{I}(y)
) satisfying the equations for
r^{j}
.

(ii)

In view of Proposition
2.6
, we should check whether
\big(A^{j},B^{j}\big)\in r^{j}
implies
\big(\phi_{1}^{j}\big(A^{j}\big),\phi_{2}^{j}\big(B^{j}\big)\big)

\in(r^{\prime})^{j}
. This is straightforward: there exist
\big(A^{k},B^{k}\big)\in r^{k}
which project to
\big(A^{j},B^{j}\big)
. We have
\big(\phi_{1}\big(A^{k}\big),\phi_{2}\big(B^{k}\big)\big)\in(r^{\prime})^{k}
as
(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})\colon r\Rightarrow r^{\prime}
is a
{\mathcal{VBC}}
-morphism. But the latter element projects under the bundle reduction map
(\sigma_{1})^{k}_{j}\times(\sigma_{2})^{k}_{j}
to
\big(\phi_{1}^{j}\big(A^{j}\big),\phi_{2}^{j}\big(B^{j}\big)\big)
which is therefore an element of
r^{j}
.

A proof of Proposition
4.6
.
By Proposition
2.15
, to prove that
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\kappa
defines a general algebroid structure on
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma
it is enough to show that it is a linear VBC and that it induces the identity on the cores. By the results of
[
27
, Theorem A.5]
, the higher tangent lift
\mathrm{T}^{k}\kappa
has these two desired properties. So do
\kappa^{k}_{E}
and
(\kappa^{k}_{E})^{-1}
, which are not only VBCs but, in fact, even isomorphisms of graded-linear bundles. We conclude that
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\kappa
, which is a composition of these three VBCs, also shares these properties, i.e.,
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\kappa
indeed defines a graded algebroid structure. In fact, all the considered VBCs are also
\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathbb{R}
-linear, with respect to the natural
\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathbb{R}
-action on the
\mathrm{T}^{k}
-lift of a vector bundle introduced in at the beginning of Section
4.1
. It follows that
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\kappa
is also
\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathbb{R}
-linear. We will use this property shortly.

The formula for anchor maps follows immediately from the definition of
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\kappa
– we simply need to calculate the relevant projections of this VBC to the legs of the considered DVBs.

Consider now any two sections
s_{1},s_{2}\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M}(E)
. By applying the functor
\mathrm{T}^{k}
to (
2.8
), we get

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}^{k}\kappa_{\mathrm{T}^{k}s_{1}}\big[\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{T}s_{2}\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}\rho_{L}\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}s_{1}\big)\big)\big]:=\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{T}s_{1}\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}\rho_{R}\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}s_{2}\big)\big)\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}\mathrm{T}^{k}[s_{1},s_{2}].

Note that in the above formula element
\mathrm{T}^{k}[s_{1},s_{2}]
belongs to the core. Now let us compose this equality with maps
\kappa^{k}_{E}
and
\big(\kappa^{k}_{E}\big)^{-1}
, which are core-identities, and use the definition of
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\kappa
to get

\displaystyle(\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\kappa)_{\mathrm{T}^{k}s_{1}}\big[\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k}s_{2}\big(\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\rho_{L}\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}s_{1}\big)\big)\big]:=\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k}s_{1}\big(\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\rho_{R}\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}s_{2}\big)\big)\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}\mathrm{T}^{k}[s_{1},s_{2}].

By comparing the above with formula (
2.8
) for the algebroid structure
\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma,\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\kappa\big)
, we may conclude that the algebroid bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}}
on
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma
satisfies

\displaystyle\big[\mathrm{T}^{k}s_{1},\mathrm{T}^{k}s_{2}\big]_{\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}}=\mathrm{T}^{k}[s_{1},s_{2}],\qquad\text{i.e.,}\qquad\big[s_{1}^{(k)},s_{2}^{(k)}\big]_{\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}}=[s_{1},s_{2}]^{(k)}

for any sections
s_{1},s_{2}\in\operatorname{Sec}_{M}(E)
. What is more, since, as we have observed before,
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\kappa
is
\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathbb{R}
-linear, the resulting bracket operation is
\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathbb{R}
-bilinear, and hence (
4.7
) holds:

\displaystyle\left[\frac{k!}{(k-\alpha)!}s_{1}^{(k-\alpha)},\frac{k!}{(k-\beta)!}s_{2}^{(k-\beta)}\right]_{\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}}=\big[\varepsilon^{\alpha}\cdot s_{1}^{(k)},\varepsilon^{\beta}\cdot s_{2}^{(k)}\big]_{\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\left[ \frac{k!}{(k-\alpha)!}s_1^{(k-\alpha)}, \frac{k!}{(k-\beta)!}s_2^{(k-\beta)}\right]_{\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}}}{}=\varepsilon^{\alpha+\beta}\cdot[s_{1},s_{2}]^{(k)}=\frac{k!}{(k-\alpha-\beta)!}[s_{1},s_{2}]^{(k-\alpha-\beta)}.

This formula completely determines the algebroid bracket on
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma
since
(k-\alpha)
-lifts of sections of
\sigma
for all possible
\alpha
’s span the module of sections of
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma
(cf. the remark following Definition
4.4
).

Finally, the fact that the lifted algebroid structure preserves the properties of being skew/AL/Lie follows directly from the anchor and the bracket formulas.

A proof of Proposition
4.11
.
To prove (
i
) note first that, by
[
27
, Theorem A.5]
, the relations
\mathrm{T}^{k}r
and
\mathrm{T}^{k}r^{\prime}
are VBCs. We have to prove that
\mathrm{T}^{k}r
restricts fine to a subbundle
\mathrm{T}^{k}E_{1}^{\prime}\times\mathrm{T}^{k}E_{2}^{\prime}
. Take a
k
-jet
\underline{v}_{2}^{k}\in\mathrm{T}^{k}M_{2}^{\prime}
which is represented by a curve
\underline{\gamma}_{2}(t)\in M_{2}^{\prime}
and denote
\underline{\gamma}_{1}(t):=\underline{r}(\underline{\gamma}_{2}(t))
. Since
\underline{r}(M_{2}^{\prime})\subset M_{1}^{\prime}
, the curve
\underline{\gamma}_{1}(t)
lies in
M_{1}^{\prime}
. Let us take any
k
-jet
v_{1}^{k}\in\mathrm{T}^{k}E_{1}^{\prime}
which lies over
\underline{v}_{1}^{k}:=\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k}\underline{\gamma}_{1}\in\mathrm{T}^{k}M_{1}^{\prime}
. We can choose a representative
\gamma_{1}(t)\in E_{1}
of
v_{1}^{k}
such that
\gamma_{1}(t)
projects to
\underline{\gamma}_{1}(t)
. Then we may write

\displaystyle\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}r\big)_{\underline{v}_{2}^{k}}\big(v_{1}^{k}\big)=\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k}r_{\underline{\gamma}_{2}(t)}(\gamma_{1}(t))\in\mathrm{T}^{k}E_{2}^{\prime},

which ends the proof of point (
i
).

To show (
ii
) observe that by our hypothesis,
\kappa
restricts fine to
\mathrm{T}E^{\prime}\times\mathrm{T}E^{\prime}
, hence by (
i
)
\mathrm{T}^{k}\kappa\colon\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{T}\sigma\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright\mathrm{T}^{k}\tau_{E}
restricts fine to
\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{T}E^{\prime}\times\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{T}E^{\prime}
. Since
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\kappa
is obtained by composing
\mathrm{T}^{k}\kappa
with the vector bundle isomorphisms
\kappa^{k}_{E}
and its inverse, the relation
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\kappa
also restricts fine to
\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{\prime}\times\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{\prime}
, hence
\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma^{\prime},\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\kappa^{\prime}\big)
is a subalgebroid of
\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma,\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\kappa^{\prime}\big)
, as was claimed.

Finally, point (
iii
) follows easily from (
ii
). Since
r
is a subalgebroid of
(\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{2},\kappa_{1}\times\kappa_{2})
, by (
ii
)
\mathrm{T}^{k}r
is a subalgebroid of
\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}(\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{2}),\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}(\kappa_{1}\times\kappa_{2})\big)\simeq\big(\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma_{1}\times\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma_{2},\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\kappa_{1}\times\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\kappa_{2}\big)
, hence
\mathrm{T}^{k}r
is an algebroidal relation.

A proof of Proposition
4.13
.
Assume first that
(\sigma,\kappa)
is an AL algebroid. From the results of
[
27
, Theorem 4.5(ix)]
we already know that
\kappa^{[k]}
is a VBC. We shall prove first that it is a weighted VBC. For this it is enough to check that
\kappa^{[k]}
is a
\mathbb{N}
-graded submanifold of
\tau^{k}_{E}\times\mathrm{T}\tau^{[k]}\colon\mathrm{T}^{k}E\times\mathrm{T}E^{[k]}\rightarrow E\times\mathrm{T}M
. A general idea is to consider the latter as a
\mathbb{N}
-graded submanifold of
\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathrm{T}E\times\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}E\rightarrow E\times\mathrm{T}M
and notice that although
\kappa
interchanges the two homogeneity structures on
\mathrm{T}E
, the homogeneity structure in our concern (which is defined by the sum of weight vector fields of degrees
1
and
k-1
) is respected by both
\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\kappa
and
\kappa^{k-1}_{E}
, thus also by
\kappa^{[k]}
(which is a fine restriction of the composition of these relations). In detail,

\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}E
is a triple-
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold of
3
-order
(1,k-1,1)
with legs
\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\tau\colon\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}E\rightarrow\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}M
,
\mathrm{T}\tau^{k-1}_{E}\colon\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}E\rightarrow\mathrm{T}E
, and
\tau_{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}E}\colon\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}E\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k-1}E
, respectively. The multi-
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold structure on
\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}E
is defined by the weight vector fields
\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\Delta_{E}
,
\mathrm{T}\Delta^{k-1}_{E}
, and
\Delta^{1}_{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}E}
. Since
\kappa
is homogeneous with respect to
\big(\Delta^{1}_{E},\mathrm{T}\Delta_{E}\big)\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathrm{T}E\times\mathrm{T}E)
and
\big(\mathrm{T}\Delta_{E},\Delta^{1}_{E}\big)
, its
(k-1)^{\mathrm{th}}
-tangent lift
\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\kappa
is homogeneous with respect to

\displaystyle\big(\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\Delta^{1}_{E},\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathrm{T}\Delta_{E}\big),\big(\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathrm{T}\Delta_{E},\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\Delta^{1}_{E}\big),\big(\Delta^{k-1}_{\mathrm{T}E},\Delta^{k-1}_{\mathrm{T}E}\big)\in\mathfrak{X}\big(\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathrm{T}E\times\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathrm{T}E\big).

Similarly,
\kappa^{k-1}_{E}
is homogeneous with respect to

\displaystyle\big(\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathrm{T}\Delta_{E},\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\Delta_{E}\big),\big(\Delta^{k-1}_{\mathrm{T}E},\mathrm{T}\Delta^{k-1}_{E}\big),\big(\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\Delta^{1}_{E},\Delta^{1}_{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}E}\big)\in\mathfrak{X}\big(\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathrm{T}E\times\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}E\big).

In particular,
\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\kappa
is homogeneous with respect to
\big(\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\Delta^{1}_{E}+\Delta^{k-1}_{\mathrm{T}E},\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathrm{T}\Delta_{E}+\Delta^{k-1}_{\mathrm{T}E}\big)
while
\kappa^{k-1}_{E}
is homogeneous with respect to
\big(\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathrm{T}\Delta_{E}+\Delta^{k-1}_{\mathrm{T}E},\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\Delta_{E}+\mathrm{T}\Delta^{k-1}_{E}\big)
thus the composition
\kappa^{k-1}_{E}\circ\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\kappa
is homogeneous with respect to
\big(\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\Delta^{1}_{E}+\Delta^{k-1}_{\mathrm{T}E},\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\Delta_{E}+\mathrm{T}\Delta^{k-1}_{E}\big)
. The result follows because the order-
k

\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold structures on
\mathrm{T}^{k}E\rightarrow E
and
\mathrm{T}E^{[k]}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M
are encoded by
\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\Delta^{1}_{E}+\Delta^{k-1}_{\mathrm{T}E}
and
\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\Delta_{E}+\mathrm{T}\Delta^{k-1}_{E}
, respectively (see
[
27
, Theorem 4.5(i)]
).

(A.4)

follows immediately from
[
27
, Theorem 4.5(xi)]
, while the reduction of
\kappa^{[k]}
from order
k
to order
1
coincides obviously with the relation
\kappa
defining AL algebroid structure on
E
.

Now if
(\sigma,\kappa)
is Lie,
\kappa
is an algebroidal relation between
\mathrm{T}\sigma\colon\mathrm{T}E\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M
and
\tau_{E}\colon\mathrm{T}E\rightarrow E
. If follows from Proposition
4.11
(
iii
) that
\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\kappa
is an algebroidal relation between the
(k-1)^{\mathrm{st}}
-tangent lift algebroids on
\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathrm{T}\sigma
and
\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\tau_{E}
. What is more,
\kappa^{k-1}_{E}
is an algebroid isomorphism between
\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\tau_{E}
and
\tau_{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}E}
, and thus the composition
\kappa^{k-1}_{E}\circ\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\kappa
is an algebroidal relation between
\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathrm{T}\sigma
and
\tau_{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}E}
. By
[
27
, Proposition 4.6]
,
\kappa^{k-1}_{E}\circ\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\kappa\subset\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathrm{T}E\times\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}E
restricts fine to
\kappa^{[k]}\subset\mathrm{T}^{k}E\times\mathrm{T}E^{[k]}
, and so, in light of Proposition
2.28
,
\kappa^{[k]}
is also an algebroidal relation, i.e.,
\big(\sigma^{[k]},\kappa^{[k]}\big)
is Lie.

It remains to prove that
\big(\sigma^{[k]},\kappa^{[k]}\big)
is strong. We shall proceed by induction on
k
and assume that
\kappa^{[k-1]}
is the identity on the core bundle. To show the same for
\kappa^{[k]}
we observe that the reduction from order
k
to
k-1
of
\kappa^{[k]}
is
\kappa^{[k-1]}
, hence it is enough to show that
\kappa^{[k]}
is the identity on the ultracore bundle. But the inclusions
\mathrm{T}^{k}E\subset\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathrm{T}E
and
\mathrm{T}E^{[k]}\subset\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}E
of graded-linear bundles give identity on the ultracores. The same is true for relations
\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\kappa
and
\kappa^{k-1}_{E}
what completes our proof.
